### 3: Cold Calls that Don't Trigger Sales Resistance


https://youtu.be/WOoJkN667qo

# Lecture Notes: Cold Calls That Don't Trigger Sales Resistance

---

## Why Traditional Cold Calls Fail

Traditional sales training teaches you that the purpose of a call is to **make an appointment or make a sale.** The problem: it's all about you and your agenda.

Even if your intentions are noble, the person you're calling **can feel** that your only agenda is to sell them something. And they get calls like this every day.

### What 99% of Salespeople Say

> "Hi, is John there? Hey John, hope you're having a great day! My name is Sam and I'm with XYZ Company, and what we do is we're the leading manufacturer of..."

**This approach:**
- Sounds like a robot
- Only offers a solution without finding out if there's a problem
- Follows the old model: introduce yourself → name your company → list benefits → hope something sticks

### What the Prospect Hears

They hear the same thing they hear from every other salesperson who calls them daily. Nobody claims they're the fifth or sixth best — everyone says they're the best. Prospects have picked up on this.

### What Comes Out of Their Mouth

- "We already have that."
- "We don't need that."
- "I'm too busy."
- "We can't afford that / don't have budget."
- "Mr. Johnson's not in today, call back next week."

**The sale is lost at hello.**

---

## Why Cold Calls Break Down in Seconds

Your prospect feels you are trying to sell them something. The momentum you impose triggers **sales resistance and self-defense mechanisms** they use to protect themselves from what they feel is an intrusion.

When prospects say no or hang up, they are trying to **protect themselves from what they view as an attack by an outsider** — someone who doesn't know anything about their world and just wants to stuff something down their throat.

**No trust = No sale.** In the post-trust era, the old model simply doesn't work anymore.

---

## The NEPQ Cold Call Approach

### Step 1: The Opening — Ask for Help

When the prospect picks up, reply in a **very relaxed, conversational tone.** There is zero trust between you — being low-key and relaxed is critical, or you will immediately trigger sales pressure.

> **You:** "Hey, this is Jeremy. I was wondering if you could possibly help me out for a moment."
>
> **Prospect:** "Sure, how can I help you?"

**Why this works:** It's normal human reaction to want to help people. Just like walking into a gas station and asking for help — 25 out of 25 people will say "How can I help you?" This simple exchange creates a **two-way dialogue** instead of a one-way pitch.

You are NOT like other salespeople who call with canned phrases: "Hi, my name is ___, I'm with ___, and what we do is ___."

---

### Step 2: The Problem Statement

Once they ask how they can help, proceed with a **problem statement** — focused on a problem they can identify with. Do NOT talk about your solution yet. It's too soon.

**The framework:**
> "Well, I'm not sure you could yet... um, I called to see if your company would be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in [their area] that might be causing you to [negative consequence]."

### Critical Delivery Notes

**Tonality matters enormously.** Go through it slowly. Pause. Do NOT sound like a robot.

❌ **Wrong:** "Well I'm not sure you could yet but I was calling to see if you'd be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps..."

✅ **Right:** "Well, I'm not sure you could yet... um... I called to see if you'd be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in your advertising... that might be causing you to lose sales."

---

### Industry-Specific Problem Statement Examples

| Industry | Problem Statement |
|---|---|
| **Advertising / Lead Gen** | "...if your company would be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in your advertising that might be causing you to lose sales." |
| **Business Coaching / Consulting** | "...if your company would be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in your business that might be causing you to lose sales." |
| **Auditing Services** | "...if you would be open to looking at whether any department within your company might be losing revenue due to vendors overcharging you." |

**Key principle:** Focus on the **customer and their problems** — not on your pitch and your solution.

---

## The Critical Phrase: "Would You Be Open To..."

This language is essential in the post-trust era. You're simply asking whether they're **open-minded about considering something new** that could possibly help them solve their problems.

It's neutral. It's non-assumptive. It doesn't trigger resistance.

### What Happens Next

After the problem statement, the prospect will usually ask:
- "Well, who is this again?"
- "What does your company do exactly?"

This is a **buying signal** — they're curious and engaged. At this point, you would deliver your personalized introduction (covered in the previous chapter).

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **The sale is lost at hello** if you sound like every other salesperson
2. **Let go of your attachment** to making the sale — focus on whether there's a sale to be made
3. **Open with a help request**, not a pitch — it triggers natural human cooperation
4. **Use a problem statement**, not a solution statement — lead with their world, not yours
5. **Tonality is everything** — slow, relaxed, conversational. Never robotic or rushed.
6. **"Would you be open to..."** is the key neutral phrase that avoids triggering resistance
7. **Be exclusively focused on their needs and issues** — when you do this, they begin to trust you and sales pressure disappears

---

### 4: How to Call Your Leads


https://youtu.be/jZzYvHigmmA
# Lecture Notes: How to Call Your Leads (Leads Who Responded to an Ad)

---

## The Common Mistake with Inbound Leads

Most salespeople believe that when someone responds to an ad, it indicates **real interest** in their product or service. So when they call back, they go into **full presentation mode** — trying to convince the prospect by presenting how wonderful they, the company, and the product are.

**The fundamental error:** The salesperson doesn't even ask if the prospect has any problems. They just **assume** that because the prospect responded to an ad, they must be looking for what the salesperson sells.

---

## Example: The Traditional (Wrong) Approach

**Industry: Staffing Services**

> **Salesperson:** "Hi, is Julie there? Hey Julie, I'm John Gray, I'm with XYZ Company, and I noticed you were on our website interested in our services, so I'm calling you back."
>
> **Prospect:** "Yeah, can you tell me more about what it's about? I saw it on the internet."
>
> **Salesperson:** "Yes! It's a great product — with our Zym Software, we've got a unique way to actually help you get your ideal employee and look at the requirements of what you're looking for when hiring. This product has been named the number one product of the year in our industry, and I know it's really going to help you. In fact, we've helped one of your competitors, XYZ..."

### What Went Wrong

- Never asked if the prospect needs better employees
- Never asked if they're looking to change their situation
- Never found out if they're having any problems at all
- Went straight into pitching features and credentials
- **Assumed** interest = readiness to buy

### What Typically Happens

The prospect disengages — they give brush-off responses, ask to "think about it," or disappear entirely. The sale is lost because no trust was built and no problems were uncovered.

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **A response to an ad ≠ a buying decision.** It indicates curiosity at best — not commitment. Treat it as an opening for conversation, not permission to pitch.
2. **Never go into presentation mode on the callback.** The prospect clicking an ad doesn't mean they've identified a problem or that your solution is the answer.
3. **Ask before you tell.** Find out if they have problems, what those problems are, and whether your solution can actually help — before presenting anything.
4. **The same NEPQ principles apply to warm leads.** Just because they came to you doesn't change the process: connect → uncover problems → determine fit → then present.
5. **Assuming kills the sale.** Whether it's a cold call or a lead callback, the moment you assume instead of asking, you become just another salesperson pushing a product.

---

*Note: This transcript appears to be partial. The next chapter covers how to call leads from purchased/generic lists.*

---

### 5: How to Call Generic Leads


https://youtu.be/LHreZiqB-wg
# Lecture Notes: How to Call Generic Leads

---

## Important Distinction: Lead Calls Are NOT Cold Calls

A **cold call** is calling a complete stranger — like picking a random name out of the phone book. They've never requested information and have no idea what you do.

**Calling a lead** is different. Whether from a lead list, lead provider, or leads you've generated yourself (Facebook, Google, Instagram) — these are people who **specifically asked you to call them.** They responded to an ad because they're looking to make a change. Otherwise, they wouldn't have responded in the first place.

**If you're hesitant about calling leads, reframe it:** You'd lack integrity if you DIDN'T call them back. They asked for your help.

---

## The Traditional (Wrong) Approach — Full Roleplay

**Industry: Coaching Services**

> **Salesperson:** "Hi, is this Chris? Hey Chris, this is John Smith with XYZ Company. How are you doing today?"
>
> **Prospect:** "Well, great."
>
> **Salesperson:** "Hey, do you have two minutes to talk right now?"
>
> **Prospect:** "No, I really don't."
>
> **Salesperson:** "Okay, well I saw you saw one of our ads yesterday about getting a coach to help you close more deals in your business, and I've been asked to personally call you to show you how we can help. Which works best — should I call you back later today or tomorrow?"

**What went wrong so far:**
- Now chasing the prospect
- Looks like just another salesperson pitching
- Using the **option close** ("today or tomorrow?") which triggers pressure

The conversation continues to deteriorate — the salesperson keeps pushing for "two problems," presents features and awards nobody asked about, and pressures the prospect into a trial close ("Thursday at two or Friday at one?").

**The result:** The prospect agrees to a Friday callback just to get rid of the salesperson. Friday comes → phone rings → no answer → voicemail → **they never call back.**

### What Went Wrong — Summary of Mistakes

1. Went into presentation mode immediately
2. Kept pushing when the prospect said they were busy
3. Pressured for problems before building any trust
4. Used trial close / option close techniques that prospects feel as pressure
5. Over-enthusiasm ("I'm so excited, you're going to love it!")
6. Created a ghost appointment — the prospect agreed just to escape

---

## The NEPQ Approach — How the New Model Calls a Lead

**Same industry: Coaching Services**

> **Salesperson:** "Hey Chris, this is John Smith, I'm with XYZ Coaching Company. Looks like you responded to an ad yesterday about possibly having a coaching system to help you in your business. And I should probably ask you first — have you found what you're looking for, or are you still looking?"

### Why "Have You Found What You're Looking For, or Are You Still Looking?"

- **Always ask this first.** If they've already found a solution, you want to know upfront — don't waste their time or yours.
- 99% of the time they'll say "No, I'm still looking" — because they just responded to the ad.
- It's neutral and non-assumptive.

### Next: "What Was It About the Ad That Attracted Your Attention?"

> **Prospect:** "No, I'm still looking."
>
> **Salesperson:** "Okay, and I was just curious — what was it about the ad that attracted your attention?"

**Why this question is so powerful:**
- It reminds them why they responded in the first place
- They're not just telling YOU why — they're telling **themselves**
- This is the **start of getting them to persuade themselves** to want to look at your solution

**Follow up with:** "Was there anything else that attracted you?"

Listen carefully and take notes.

### Transition to Qualification

> "Okay, and I should probably ask you a few questions just to see if I could actually help. For example, what type of business do you own?"

This naturally transitions from the **connecting stage** into **situation questions** (covered in the engagement stage).

---

## Key Takeaways

| Traditional Lead Call | NEPQ Lead Call |
|---|---|
| Assumes interest = ready to buy | Asks if they've found what they're looking for |
| Presents immediately | Asks what attracted them to the ad |
| Pushes for problems and appointments | Lets the prospect tell themselves why they're interested |
| Uses trial closes and option closes | Uses neutral, curiosity-driven questions |
| Creates ghost appointments | Creates genuine two-way dialogue |
| Prospect goes MIA | Prospect leans in and engages |

**The pattern is always the same:** Don't pitch. Don't assume. Don't pressure. Ask questions that let the prospect reveal their own motivation — and let them persuade themselves.

---

### 6: Asking for and Calling Referrals


https://youtu.be/uGi8q6F5sFQ
# Lecture Notes: How to Call Referrals

---

## Why Referrals Matter

Referrals are one of the most powerful lead sources in sales because they come with **built-in trust.** The prospect already has a positive association through someone they know and respect, which means you start the conversation miles ahead of a cold call.

Referrals are also **higher quality** — these are people who are more likely to be genuinely interested in what you offer, which increases your chances of a meaningful conversation and a successful outcome.

---

## When and How to Ask for Referrals

### Timing Is Everything

The best time to ask is **after you've successfully helped or satisfied a client.** Their positive experience makes them naturally inclined to recommend you. Don't ask before you've delivered value — it feels premature and self-serving.

### How to Ask

Be polite, direct, and frame it around **helping others** — not about you getting more business.

> "I'm glad we were able to help you with [service/product]. Do you know anyone else who might benefit from what we offer?"

**Key:** Position the ask so it highlights how their contacts can benefit. This keeps it consistent with the NEPQ philosophy — it's about the other person, not your agenda.

---

## How to Call Referrals — The NEPQ Way

### Step 1: Lead with the Referrer's Name

Always mention who referred you. This builds **immediate trust and credibility** — you're no longer a stranger, you're connected to someone they know.

> "Hi [Name], my name is [Your Name] from [Company]. [Referrer's Name] suggested I reach out to you because they thought you might benefit from what we offer."

### Step 2: Ask Questions First

Just like any other NEPQ conversation — **don't go into presentation mode.** Start by understanding their situation and needs.

> "Can you tell me a bit about your current situation and how you're managing [specific problem]?"

The same principles apply: be curious, listen carefully, take notes. The fact that it's a referral doesn't give you permission to skip the engagement process.

### Step 3: Build Rapport Through the Shared Connection

The referral already has a connection to your existing client — use this naturally to make the conversation feel more familiar and personal. But don't over-rely on it. The real rapport comes from showing genuine interest in **their** situation.

---

## Following Up with Referrals

**Be persistent but respectful.** Follow up just as you would with any lead, but leverage the mutual connection when you do — reiterate who referred you to maintain trust and keep the conversation focused on how you can help.

Don't let the referral advantage go to waste by being passive. These leads deserve prompt, thoughtful follow-up precisely because someone put their name on the line for you.

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Referrals come with built-in trust** — use that advantage, but don't abuse it by skipping the NEPQ process
2. **Ask for referrals after delivering value** — timing and framing matter
3. **Always lead with the referrer's name** — it opens doors instantly
4. **Ask questions before presenting** — the same rules apply regardless of how warm the lead is
5. **Follow up promptly and respectfully** — someone vouched for you, honor that by being responsive and professional
6. **Frame everything around helping others** — from the ask to the call to the follow-up, keep it about the prospect's needs, not your quota

---

### 7: Effective Voicemail Messages - Neutral Language


https://youtu.be/8B1VPgTc-1s

# Lecture Notes: Effective Voicemail Messages Using Neutral Language

---

## Mindset Shift: Voicemails Are an Opportunity

Before learning the technique, check your own mindset: Do you see voicemails as a **dead end and waste of time**, or as an **opportunity**?

The right frame: voicemails are another touchpoint to create curiosity and differentiate yourself from every other salesperson leaving generic messages.

---

## Key Principles (Based on NEPQ Methodology)

### 1. Use Neutral Language — Don't Sell on the Voicemail

The voicemail is NOT the place to pitch. Just like every other stage of NEPQ, the goal is to **create enough curiosity** for them to call you back or take your next call — not to close a sale in 30 seconds.

### 2. Focus on Their Problems, Not Your Solution

Frame the voicemail around something the prospect would identify with — a problem in their industry or situation — rather than listing your product features or company credentials.

### 3. Keep It Short and Conversational

Sound like a real person, not a salesperson reading a script. The same tonality rules apply: relaxed, calm, conversational. No over-enthusiasm, no robotic delivery.

### 4. Don't Sound Like Every Other Salesperson

If your voicemail sounds like "Hi, this is [Name] from [Company], and I'm calling because we have a great solution that can help you..." — you'll be deleted before the message ends. You need to stand out by **not** sounding like a pitch.

---

## Voicemail Framework

Based on the NEPQ principles covered so far, an effective voicemail would follow this structure:

1. **Your name and a brief identifier** (keep it simple)
2. **A problem-focused statement** — reference something relevant to their world
3. **A neutral, curiosity-driven close** — invite them to call back without pressure

**The goal:** Get them curious enough to return your call or be receptive when you call again. That's it. Nothing more.

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Never pitch on a voicemail** — it triggers the same resistance as a bad cold call opening
2. **Lead with a problem they'd recognize** — make it about their world
3. **Sound human, not scripted** — tonality matters even in a recorded message
4. **The only objective is a callback or warmer reception next time** — don't try to close on a voicemail
5. **Neutral language applies everywhere** — voicemails are no exception to the NEPQ system

---

*Note: This transcript appears to be partial — only the opening was captured. The principles above are derived from the NEPQ framework covered in previous chapters and the direction indicated by the opening of this lesson.*

---

### 8: Connecting Stage Summary


https://youtu.be/5oPn6R0Xzu0
# Lecture Notes: Connecting Stage — Summary

---

## The 5 Key Learnings from the Connecting Stage

### 1. Connecting Questions — What, Why, and When

Connecting questions make it easy to **put the focus on your prospect** and establish trust. They eliminate anxiety instantly — for **both you and your potential customer.**

**Key connecting questions include:**
- "I should probably start off by asking — have you found what you're looking for, or are you still looking?"
- "What was it about the ad that attracted your attention?"
- "Do you know what you're looking for?"

---

### 2. Personalized Introduction & Problem Statement

**Your personalized introduction** demonstrates that you're there to help people solve problems — by painting a picture of **how what you do helps other people** (not by listing features or credentials).

**Your problem statement** (used in cold calling) identifies a **general problem** that most people in your prospect's industry are aware of and can relate to.

Together, these show that you are a **problem finder and problem solver** — not a product pusher like most salespeople.

---

### 3. How to Call Without Triggering Resistance

You learned how to:
- **Cold call** using a problem statement
- **Call leads** (from ads, lists, or self-generated) without assuming interest = readiness to buy
- **Call referrals** by leveraging the referrer's name and asking questions first

All without triggering sales resistance.

---

### 4. Effective Voicemails Using Neutral Language

Voicemails follow the same NEPQ principles: lead with a problem, stay conversational, don't pitch, and use neutral language to create curiosity — not pressure.

---

### 5. Always Focus on Your Customer

This is the overarching principle of the entire connecting stage:

**Always focusing on your customer is the best way to operate in sales if you want to be at the top.** It's the most efficient way to connect with prospects in a meaningful way that brings value to the conversation and trust to the relationship you're building.

> **No trust = No sale.**

---

## What's Next

With the connecting stage complete, you're ready to move into the **Engagement Stage** — where you'll learn the skilled questions that uncover problems, causes, and emotional impact to move the conversation forward.

---

## Chapter 4

### 1: Introduction  Why Ask Skilled Questions


https://youtu.be/7udrkYJBTao

# Lecture Notes: Introduction to the Engagement Stage — Why Ask Skilled Questions

---

## What Is the Engagement Stage?

In romance, "engagement" refers to a period of getting to know each other before marriage. In sales, it has a very similar meaning — it's the period where you get to know your prospect as an individual human being before moving forward with any solution.

**The engagement stage is the core of NEPQ.** This is where the sale is actually made. It represents **85% of the new model of selling.**

---

## Why the Engagement Stage Matters

Trust only revolves around relationships. In the post-trust era, this has become the **dominating factor** in your success as a sales professional.

More than ever, salespeople must engage prospects as **individual people** before presenting solutions. This is true in one-on-one conversations and equally true at a macro level in advertising and marketing.

### What Happens When You Do It Right

Your customers will **buy you first** — based on your skills of:
- Listening
- Being understanding
- Asking the right questions at the right time

By listening and asking highly skilled questions, your prospects will **tell you things they've never told a salesperson before.** You'll learn facts and feelings about their situation, their problems, and what those problems are doing to them, their company, and even their families.

---

## Your Goal in the Engagement Stage

Help them find out:
1. **What their problems are** (if any)
2. **What's caused the problems**
3. **How the problems are affecting them** (most importantly)

You find all of this out by asking skilled questions.

---

## The 5 Types of Engagement Questions

| # | Question Type | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Situation Questions** | Understand their current circumstances and context |
| 2 | **Problem Awareness Questions** | Uncover the problems they're experiencing |
| 3 | **Solution Awareness Questions** | Explore what solutions they've considered or tried |
| 4 | **Consequence Questions** | Reveal the emotional and practical impact of their problems |
| 5 | **Qualifying Questions** | Determine if they're ready and able to move forward |

---

## The Three Destination Points

As you travel through the engagement stage with your prospect, you'll arrive at one of three outcomes:

| Destination | Meaning | Your Action |
|---|---|---|
| **1. No need** | They genuinely don't have a problem your solution addresses | Move on — there's no sale to be made |
| **2. Need but no desire to change** | They have a problem but aren't motivated to fix it | This may not be the right time — or your questions haven't gone deep enough yet |
| **3. Need AND desire to change** | They have a problem and genuinely want to solve it | This is where you proceed to presentation and commitment |

**Key insight:** The answers your prospects give you are like **signs on a signpost.** It's easy if you just follow the signs. Ask clarifying questions — "under the surface" questions — based on what they've already told you.

---

## The Power of Detachment

Using NEPQ and **letting go of the expectation of making the sale** — instead focusing on whether or not you can help — allows both you and your prospect to travel through the engagement stage naturally. When you have a complete understanding of your potential customer, you'll:

- Make more appointments
- Get more commitments
- Make far more sales, faster and easier

This applies to selling by phone or in person, regardless of product, service, or industry.

---

## What's Next

The deep dive begins with **Situation Questions** — the first of the five engagement question types.

---

### 2: Situation Questions


https://youtu.be/LiukHFzhYAk

# Lecture Notes: Situation Questions

---

## What Happens During the Engagement Stage

Five things happen when you conduct the engagement stage correctly:

1. **You start building trust** — which strengthens your relationship with the prospect
2. **You learn exactly what your customer is looking for**
3. **You help your prospect think about their problems** and persuade themselves to want to make a change — with YOU. This completely eliminates your competition. They'll trust you so much they won't even price shop.
4. **You find out what emotions they're feeling** — the WHY behind why they're looking. You do this by asking questions that bring out their feeling side, which introduces urgency to move forward.
5. **You qualify them** — making sure they actually want to change their situation

### If You're Still Getting Objections...

If you keep getting rejected, it's likely because you keep reverting to old habits: presenting your solution too early, talking about how great your product or company is before finding out their problems. **Resist doing that.**

> 💡 **Tip:** Put a rubber band on your wrist and snap it every time you go back into pitch mode before discovering their problems.

---

## The Golden Rule — Write This Down

> **"Selling is the art of finding and solving problems by asking skilled questions and listening for the answers."**

Post this in your cubicle, your office, your home. Live by it.

Your sales conversation should feel like a **relaxed, friendly conversation you'd have with a friend** — except it's a very skilled conversation.

---

## What Are Situation Questions?

Situation questions are the **foundation** on which you build your entire sales conversation. They come after connecting questions and allow you to **get facts** about the prospect — their present situation and how they got there.

### Key Characteristics

- They bring out **facts**, not emotions
- They tend to be somewhat boring (but still necessary)
- They help you understand **what they're doing now** and **what they've done in the past**
- The answers are more factual than emotional

### The Rule of Three to Four

**Don't ask more than 3–4 situation questions in a row.** More than that and your prospect feels like they're being interrogated.

---

## The Situation Question Framework

The pattern typically follows this flow:

| Question # | Purpose | Example Pattern |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | What's their current situation? | "What do you [do/have/use] now?" |
| **2** | How long have they been in it? | "How long have you been doing/having that?" |
| **3** | What caused them to get into it? | "What got you involved in / caused you to choose that?" |

---

## Industry-Specific Examples

### Network Marketing / Direct Sales
1. "John, what do you do for a living?"
2. "How long have you been doing that type of work?"
3. "What got you involved in that career?"

**Adapt for context:**
- Business owner → "How long have you owned that type of business?"
- Retired → "How long have you been retired?" / "What did you do before you retired?"

### Financial Services (High Net Worth Clients)
1. "Can I ask, Amy — what does your investment portfolio actually look like now?"
2. "How long have you been allocating your principal in those types of investments?"
3. "I'm curious — what got you involved in that type of investing?"

### Automotive Sales
1. "Tell me, Julie — what type of car do you drive now?"
2. "How long have you had that vehicle?"
3. "I'm curious — what caused you to get that car a few years ago rather than something else?"

### Insurance
1. "What type of insurance coverage do you have now?" / "What type of policy do you have with that company?"
2. *(Duration implied)*
3. "I'm curious — what caused you to go with that company over somebody else?"

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Situation questions get the facts** — they're the setup for everything that follows
2. **Keep it to 3–4 questions max** — avoid interrogation mode
3. **Follow the pattern:** What now → How long → What caused you to choose that
4. **Adapt to every industry** — the framework is universal, the specifics change
5. **These are the foundation, not the destination** — situation questions lead into Problem Awareness Questions, which is where the emotional power lives

---

## What's Next

**Problem Awareness Questions** — the heart of the engagement stage, where you begin uncovering the real problems and bringing out the emotions that drive buying decisions.

---

## Chapter 5

### 1: What Problem Awareness Questions Give You


https://youtu.be/noiswBQ9azo

# Lecture Notes: What Problem Awareness Questions Give You

---

## Definition: What Are Problem Awareness Questions?

Problem awareness questions are questions you ask **based on the answers you obtained from your situation questions.** They are not random — they flow directly from what the prospect just told you about their current situation.

### What They Do

- Help your prospect **think about their problems and needs**
- Help them **fill out the picture** — going beyond surface-level facts
- Encourage a **flow of detail** that helps prospects match their needs or problems to your solution
- Go **beyond facts** — you're now exploring their **feelings and emotions**

---

## Why Problem Awareness Questions Are So Powerful

### They Earn Respect and Trust

Your awareness and understanding of your prospect's problems will gain you their **respect and trust.** When you demonstrate genuine understanding of their world, you become the trusted advisor — not just another salesperson.

### Decisions Are Driven by Emotion, Not Logic

Your prospect's decision to make a change will be **motivated by their emotions** — not their intellect or logic. Problem awareness questions are the tool that accesses this emotional layer.

---

## How to Use Problem Awareness Questions

### Let Them Tell Their Story — Don't Reinterpret

**Never let your own reality, background, and beliefs reinterpret what they're saying to you.** Let them tell you what is going on for them. Let them tell you their pain.

This is critical: their pain is part of their picture and it's **attached to their need.**

### Go Back in Time

You want them to:
- **Feel that pain**
- **Take you back in time** — specifically to the moment of their hurt or pain
- **Tell you the pain in the details**

### Your Job: Draw Out Their Story

- Help them **draw out their story**
- Ask them to **clarify and expand** on what they're saying
- **Listen** — while they're sharing, your job is to listen deeply, not plan your pitch

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Problem awareness questions flow from situation questions** — they're built on the facts you've already gathered
2. **They go beyond facts into feelings** — this is the emotional core of NEPQ
3. **Don't reinterpret their reality** — let them tell their own story in their own words
4. **Pain is attached to need** — the deeper they feel the pain, the more motivated they are to change
5. **Take them back to the moment** — the most powerful responses come when prospects revisit the specific moment their problem became real
6. **Your primary job is to listen** — ask, clarify, expand, and listen. The more they talk, the more they sell themselves.

---

## What's Next

Specific problem awareness question frameworks and examples — how to actually construct and deliver these questions across different industries.

---

### 2: Turning Your Statements Into Questions


https://youtu.be/hm9LzF3BWT0
# Lecture Notes: Turning Your Statements Into Questions

---

## The Core Concept

> **Instead of making statements, compose your statements into questions.**

Write this down. This concept can make you a lot of money once you master it. It is absolutely essential with today's Information Age buyer.

---

## Why This Matters

When you're faced with a prospect who has a concern, **don't jump to treat it like an objection.** Instead, address their concerns by asking questions to:
1. Fully understand their concern
2. Get them to **sell themselves** on the change they need to make

**Don't tell them. Get them to persuade themselves.**

### What Happens When You Turn Statements Into Questions

- Your prospects become **open to your ideas**
- It helps them **overcome their own concerns**
- They start **processing information internally** as they talk
- Their answers help them **think about their problems** and own the idea that they want to change
- They consciously and subconsciously **internalize what they're saying** — examining and challenging their own beliefs about why they keep allowing their present situation to continue

---

## How to Do It — The Phrases

Use these question stems to convert statements into questions:

- **"What if..."**
- **"What do you think about..."**
- **"Do you think..."**
- **"If you could..."**

### Example

Instead of telling: *"It's not what you think it is — let me show you another perspective."*

Ask: **"John, what if it wasn't what you thought it was... would you be open to looking at it from another perspective?"**

---

## The Critical Rule: Don't Tell — Ask

Don't tell potential customers about what you need and what you have. Instead, ask questions that **uncover and explore what THEY know** about the subject first.

**If you tell your prospects your solution right away:**
- YOU end up owning the problem and the solution
- The prospect becomes far less attached because they weren't involved in the process
- This is far less persuasive

---

## What Happens Inside the Prospect's Mind

When prospects start listening to themselves answer your questions, they begin questioning their own situation:

| Industry | Internal Dialogue Triggered |
|---|---|
| **Life Insurance** | "Why do I keep putting off buying insurance for my family? Something could happen to me..." |
| **Financial Services** | "Why do I keep investing with this other firm? Maybe I could get a higher rate of return..." |
| **Network Marketing** | "Why do I keep commuting an hour each way when I could be working from home?" |
| **Advertising / Lead Gen** | "Why do I keep getting leads from this company? Maybe I should try another one for higher conversions..." |

**The pattern:** They question themselves → question why they stay in their situation → start thinking about changing → persuade themselves they're ready to change **now** — not in 6 months, not in a year.

---

## Self-Persuasion Is Always More Powerful Than External Persuasion

When the motivation is **not external**, the decision feels natural to the prospect. It becomes **their idea** to buy — not yours. You're just the **facilitator** along that journey through the questions you've asked.

If a prospect convinces themselves to buy that expensive red car based on your questions, that is infinitely more powerful than you listing reasons why they should buy it.

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Convert every statement into a question** — this is the fundamental skill of NEPQ
2. **Use "What if," "Do you think," "What do you think about," "If you could"** as your go-to stems
3. **Never own the problem and solution yourself** — if you present too early, the prospect detaches
4. **Let them internalize** — as they answer your questions, they challenge their own beliefs and create their own urgency
5. **Self-persuasion creates commitment** — when it's their idea, they buy in fully
6. **You are a facilitator, not a convincer** — your questions guide the journey, but they walk the path themselves

---

### 3: A Tale of Two Truths


https://youtu.be/zGHSfjtzovI
# Lecture Notes: A Tale of Two Truths

---

## The Concept: Your Customer's Two Truths

Understanding this principle is extremely important for reaching the top in sales. The very top salespeople in the world master this concept and apply it throughout the engagement stage.

---

## The Setup: When Prospects Say They Love What They Already Have

When your prospect tells you they love the company, product, or service they're already using — how does that make you feel?

Most salespeople feel deflated, defeated, or ready to give up. They hear "I love my current provider" and think the sale is over.

**But here's the critical insight: Every customer has TWO truths.**

### Truth #1: What They Tell You on the Surface

This is the positive version — "We love our current provider," "Everything is great," "We're happy with what we have." This is often what prospects say reflexively, especially early in a conversation. It's their **protective truth** — the one that keeps salespeople at arm's length.

### Truth #2: What's Really Going On Underneath

This is the deeper reality — the frustrations, gaps, unmet needs, and dissatisfactions that exist beneath the surface. No product, company, or service is perfect. There are always areas where the prospect is not fully satisfied, even if they genuinely like their current solution overall.

---

## Why This Matters for NEPQ

Your job is **not** to argue with Truth #1 or try to convince them they're wrong about liking their current provider. That triggers resistance immediately.

Your job is to use **skilled problem awareness questions** to gently help the prospect explore Truth #2 on their own — the gaps, frustrations, and unmet needs they may not have fully articulated, even to themselves.

**When they discover Truth #2 through your questions, they create their own motivation to change.**

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Never argue with a prospect's positive feelings** about their current solution — that's their first truth and it's real to them
2. **Every customer has a second truth** — areas of dissatisfaction, unmet needs, or hidden gaps beneath the surface
3. **Your questions are the tool** that helps prospects access and articulate their second truth
4. **Self-discovered dissatisfaction is far more powerful** than being told by a salesperson that their current solution is inadequate
5. **This principle will be applied with specific questions** later in the engagement stage — understanding it now sets the foundation

---

*Note: This transcript appears to be partial — only the opening was captured. The principles above are derived from the concept introduced and the NEPQ framework established in previous chapters.*

---

### 4: Probing & Clarifying Questions


https://youtu.be/8MdS2NPjKcY
# Lecture Notes: Probing & Clarifying Questions

---

## Probing Questions — What They Are and Why They Matter

Once your potential customer tells you what their problem is, probing questions make it easy for them to **relive their experience** of what the problem has done to them. They allow the prospect to develop the story in **very painful detail** — and that's good for you.

### What Probing Questions Do

- Invite your prospect to **elaborate**
- **Bring out their emotions and feelings**
- Help them **feel the need to change**

**Without probing questions at the right time, you don't bring out the emotional side of your prospect — so they don't feel the urgency to change.** This cannot be stressed enough if you want to be a top salesperson.

---

## Examples of Probing Questions

Notice the tonality on all of these — **calm, collected, relaxed.**

- **"Has that had an impact on you?"** → follow up: **"Well, in what way though?"**
- **"What bothers you the most about this?"**
- **"How tough a position did that put you in?"**

### Key Characteristics of Good Probes

- They're short and direct
- They invite elaboration, not yes/no answers
- They focus on **emotional impact**, not facts
- They're delivered with calm, empathetic tonality — never aggressive or interrogating

---

## Why Probing Questions Are Critical at This Stage

By this point in the sales conversation, you've gathered facts through situation questions and begun uncovering problems through problem awareness questions. Probing questions are the tool that **deepens the emotional connection** to those problems.

They don't introduce new topics — they go **deeper** into what the prospect has already shared, pulling out the feelings and impact that drive buying decisions.

---

## Clarifying Questions

Clarifying questions work alongside probes to ensure you **fully understand** what the prospect is telling you. They demonstrate that you're listening carefully and that you care about getting it right.

**Purpose:**
- Confirm your understanding of what they've said
- Invite them to expand or correct your interpretation
- Show genuine interest and active listening

**Examples might include:**
- "When you say [X], what do you mean by that exactly?"
- "Can you tell me more about that?"
- "Help me understand — what did that look like for you?"

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Probing questions unlock emotion** — they're the bridge between knowing the problem and feeling the problem
2. **Timing matters** — ask probes after the prospect reveals a problem, not before
3. **Tonality is everything** — calm, collected, relaxed. Never pushy or interrogating.
4. **Go deeper, not wider** — probes drill into what they've already told you rather than jumping to new topics
5. **Clarifying questions show you're listening** — they build trust and ensure accuracy
6. **Without probes, prospects don't feel urgency** — and without urgency, there's no motivation to change

---

*Note: This transcript appears to be partial. The principles above are derived from the content captured and the NEPQ framework established in previous chapters.*

---

### 5: Problem Awareness Questions Summary


https://youtu.be/m-DxAHjsZag
# Lecture Notes: Problem Awareness Questions — Summary & Neutral Language in Practice

---

## Using Neutral Language in Problem Awareness Questions

As you ask problem awareness questions, you must **stay neutral without assuming anything.** Your goal is to see if you can help — not to presume you already know the answer.

---

## Example: Recruiter at a Staffing Agency

**Scenario:** You're a recruiter talking to a prospect named Mary.

### The Neutral Problem Awareness Question

> "So Mary, I guess just so I can see the rationale behind why you're looking for a change..."

### Key Neutral Language Elements

Notice the phrases that keep it neutral:

- **"I guess"** — softens the question, removes any sense of interrogation
- **"just so I can see"** — positions it as understanding, not judging
- **"the rationale behind"** — invites them to explain their thinking, not justify themselves
- **"why you're looking for a change"** — acknowledges they may be looking without assuming they've already decided

These are the **same neutral language principles** that run through all of NEPQ: you're not assuming, not pushing, not leading — you're genuinely exploring.

---

## Recap: What Problem Awareness Questions Give You

Building on everything covered in this section:

1. **They flow from situation questions** — built on facts you've already gathered
2. **They go beyond facts into feelings** — exploring the emotional reality behind the prospect's situation
3. **They help the prospect fill out their own picture** — they discover their problems, not you telling them
4. **Turning statements into questions** makes your suggestions feel like their ideas
5. **The Two Truths principle** means you never argue with their surface truth — you help them explore the deeper one
6. **Probing questions ("Why though?" / "In what way?")** deepen the emotional impact
7. **Clarifying questions** show you're listening and ensure you truly understand
8. **Neutral language throughout** keeps the conversation pressure-free

---

## The Thread Through Everything

Problem awareness questions are the **emotional engine** of the NEPQ process. Situation questions get the facts. Problem awareness questions get the feelings. And when prospects feel their own pain through your skilled questions, they create their own urgency to change — with you.

---

*Note: This transcript appears to be partial. The key principles are captured above based on the content available and the cumulative NEPQ framework.*

## Chapter 6

### 1: 4 Reasons to Use Solutions Awareness Questions

https://youtu.be/YzG72j2r0dQ

# Lecture Notes: Solution Awareness Questions — 4 Reasons to Use Them

---

## Where We Are in the Sales Conversation

By this stage, you've already completed the **Problem Awareness Questions (PAQs)** phase. If done correctly, you've achieved something critical: you've differentiated yourself from every other salesperson your prospect has ever spoken to.

**Why?** Because while your competitors only listen to the facts, you've dug deeper — you've uncovered the prospect's **emotions, feelings, and the full story** behind their situation.

---

## The Result of Good Problem Awareness Questions

When you understand more about your prospect than any other advisor:

-   You are seen as **more qualified** to solve their problems
    
-   You've built **trust** — the critical stepping stone
    
-   You've transitioned from "just another salesperson" to **The Trusted Authority** in their eyes
    
-   The prospect **feels understood**, not sold to
    

**Key Principle:** People buy on emotion, not logic. Their feelings will always determine whether they purchase from you.

---

## Emotion-Pulling Questions (Recap from PAQ Phase)

These are the types of questions that extract deeper emotion and build connection:

-   *"Amy, I sense you might be frustrated by that..."*
    
-   *"John, what type of impact has that had on you? In what way?"*
    
-   *"Sally, what's bothering you the most about this?"*
    
-   *"Carol, why is that so important to you now?"*
    
-   *"Kevin, I sense this means something to you..."*
    
-   *"Mary, has that put you in a tougher position? In what way? How does that affect you?"*
    

**Notice the pattern:** These questions pull out more emotion. They go beyond surface-level facts to uncover what the prospect truly feels and cares about.

---

## Understanding Where Your Prospect Stands

**What the prospect knows well:**

-   Their own story — better than anyone, better than you
    
-   How things currently are in their world
    
-   How they got to their current situation
    

**What the prospect may NOT fully understand:**

-   How your product or service fits into the picture of their world
    
-   How to connect their past situation to a future solution
    

**Your role:** Help the prospect create a clear picture that bridges their **past and present situation** with a **future solution**. You do this by asking easy-to-answer Problem Awareness Questions (PAQs).

---

## The Transition to Solution Awareness Questions

At this point in the conversation:

-   The prospect's **need is almost complete** and ready for you to provide a solution — but **not quite yet**
    
-   You now need to help your prospect **broaden the horizon** of their picture
    
-   This is where you help them **look toward finding a solution**
    
-   This is the purpose of **Solution Awareness Questions (SAQs)**
    

---

## Key Takeaways for Application

1.  **Differentiation comes from depth** — go beyond facts, uncover emotions
    
2.  **Trust is the gateway** — without it, no solution will be accepted
    
3.  **Don't rush to the solution** — the prospect's picture must be complete first
    
4.  **Bridge the gap** — help prospects connect where they are to where they need to be
    
5.  **Emotion drives decisions** — never forget this fundamental truth

---

### 2: Telling is Not Selling

https://youtu.be/yS18jA8PMZw

# Lecture Notes: Telling Is Not Selling — Solution Awareness Questions in Practice

---

## Core Principle: Telling Is Not Selling

The key is to **use your knowledge to ask skilled questions**, not to tell your prospects what you know. Never let your need to demonstrate expertise stand in the way of allowing your prospect to give you the answers about what they're looking for.

**Write this down and live it: Telling is not selling.**

---

## The Two Foundational Solution Awareness Questions

SAQs can be asked at any time during the sales conversation. They come in different forms for different situations, but they boil down to two core questions:

1.  **"What have you done about changing your situation?"** (Past-focused)
    
2.  **"What would you do if you could?"** (Future-focused)
    

Many prospects are already looking for ways to solve their problems — they may have explored options but came up empty, or tried things that didn't work. Your job is to find out what they've done (if anything) by asking variations of:

> *"Have you been out there looking for anything that would give you what you're wanting?"*

**The technique:** Plug in what they've already told you they're looking for and the results they want.

---

## Examples by Industry

### Financial Services

> *"John, before we talked today, were you out there looking for different investments that would give you a higher return than what you're presently getting?"*

### Franchise Sales

(Amy has told you she wants a business to escape her job and make more money)

> *"Amy, before you found us here at XYZ Franchise, were you out there looking for a business where you could start making more money?"* Or: *"What have you done about changing this, if anything?"*

### Insurance

(Stuart's wife is pressuring him to increase coverage because her father passed away when she was young, leaving the family with very little)

> *"Stuart, what have you done in the past about getting a policy that would give your wife more financial security in case something did happen to you?"*

**Notice the pattern:** You're plugging in their specific situation, their words, and their emotional drivers into the question framework.

---

## Going Deeper — Peeling the Layers

If your prospect tells you they've tried to solve their problems before or are currently looking for solutions, **ask them to expand**. You must go deeper if you want to be at the top of the sales pyramid.

**Follow-up questions to go deeper:**

-   How did you get involved with that company/product?
    
-   What worked? What didn't work?
    
-   What would you have changed if you could?
    
-   What criteria would you use if you wanted to make a decision to do something else or work with somebody else?
    

**The more layers you peel off, the more your prospect persuades themselves** that they need to make a change — and they'll want to make that change with **you**.

---

## Critical Tip: Give Them Time to Think

When you ask SAQs, **give your prospect time to think**. Never jump in and answer the question you just asked them.

-   Jumping in is rude
    
-   It prevents your prospect from participating and coming to their own conclusions
    
-   When they answer themselves, they **connect emotionally**, own the problem, and want to find the solution
    

**Focus on helping them, not selling them.**

---

## Probing Deeper After They Answer

Instead of moving on to the next question after they answer, **probe deeper**:

-   *"Hold on — why is that though?"*
    
-   *"Why is that so important to you now?"*
    
-   *"Why now though?"*
    

These questions let the prospect reveal the **advantages and benefits** of your solution themselves — what it means for them, what it will allow them to do once their problem is solved.

**The result:** Your prospect convinces and sells themselves on the idea of changing. And who do they want to change with? **You** — because you're different from any other salesperson they've ever spoken to. They feel your intent, they bond with you, they trust you. You are now **The Trusted Authority**.

---

## Future-Focused SAQs — Quantifying the Need

These same questions can be placed in the future to bring change and progress to your customer's world. Use these categories to fully understand their needs:

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Question</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Purpose</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>What</strong> are they looking for?</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Products, services, features, applications</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>How much / how many</strong> do they need?</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Quantify the need</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>When</strong> will it be used?</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Startup date, delivery date, implementation date, agreement signing date</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>How long / how often</strong> will it be used?</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Frequency, duration, time frame</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Who</strong> is involved?</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Decision-makers, users of the product/service</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Where</strong> will it be used/delivered?</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Location relevance</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

**Important:** Not every question applies to every scenario. Only use the questions relevant to your industry and situation.

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Telling is not selling** — ask skilled questions, don't lecture
    
2.  **Plug in their words** — use what they've told you to frame your SAQs
    
3.  **Go deeper, always** — peel layers like an onion; the deeper you go, the more they self-persuade
    
4.  **Silence is powerful** — give them time to think and answer
    
5.  **Probe after every answer** — don't just move on; ask "why is that important?"
    
6.  **Let them sell themselves** — the best close happens when they convince themselves
    
7.  **Quantify the future need** — use what, how much, when, how long, who, and where to complete the picture

---

### 3: Pretend Rules

https://youtu.be/pVeavuHdyQA

# Lecture Notes: Pretend Rules — Using Pretend Questions to Remove Sales Pressure

---

## The Post-Trust Era Challenge

Customers today are more skeptical than ever. Even after you've built rapport and uncovered their problems, they may still fear:

-   *"What if this product or service doesn't solve my challenges?"*
    
-   *"What if I transfer my money and they let me down?"*
    

This is normal. Your job during the Solution Awareness stage is to move them forward **without triggering that fear**.

---

## What Are Pretend Questions?

Pretend questions are designed to get your prospect to **make smaller decisions** that move them forward in the buying process. These small decisions act as **stepping stones** leading up to the final purchase decision.

**The key:** The prospect answers as if it's hypothetical — *"Let's just pretend for a moment."* What they answer doesn't feel like it "counts" because it's seen through their imagination. This removes pressure entirely.

---

## Why Direct Questions Create Pressure

If you ask direct closing-type questions too early, you create nearly as much pressure as the final purchase decision itself:

-   *"When do you want to start?"* → Creates pressure
    
-   *"How many do you want?"* / *"How much do you need?"* → Asked too early, makes them uncomfortable
    

**Your customer knows where those questions lead.** They're not stupid. They'll think: *"Wait, slow down — I'm not ready for this."*

When prospects feel sales pressure, they move **away** from buying. That's when you hear objections like:

-   *"Why don't you leave me some information and let me think it over."*
    
-   *"I'll get back to you in a few weeks."*
    
-   *"I need to talk to my boss about this."*
    
-   *"We're just kind of looking around."*
    

**Now you know why you get those objections.**

---

## The Power of Imagining the Future

When you ask questions that get prospects to **imagine the future**, they:

-   See themselves in that future
    
-   See their own role in shaping it
    
-   Take **responsibility** for making it happen
    
-   Start to **own the process**, own their problems, and own the solution
    

---

## The Generic Pretend Question Framework

> *"John, how do you see your life being different than it is now if you solved this issue?"*

Then follow up with: *"How would that make you feel though?"* — this brings out the emotion.

**Delivery matters:** Calm, relaxed, conversational tonality. Use pauses deliberately.

---

## Three Pretend Rules

### Rule #1: Use Conditional Language

Make the decision **conditional**. The question has two parts:

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 75px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Part</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Purpose</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Example</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Lead-in</strong> (front end)</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Creates the hypothetical frame</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"If you were to go ahead with this..."</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Specific decision</strong> (back end)</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Elicits a micro-commitment</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"...when would you do that?"</em></p></td></tr></tbody></table>

**The formula:**

> *"If you were to go ahead with \_\_\_..."*
> 
> -   ...when would you?
>     
> -   ...where would you?
>     
> -   ...what kind would you?
>     
> -   ...how many would you?
>     

**Why this works:** Conditional language like *"if you were to"* and *"would"* are **neutral and pressure-free**. They assure the prospect that the decision doesn't count — it's just pretend.

### Insurance Example:

> *"Sally, if you were to go ahead with a new policy... when would you be starting?"*

Notice the pause and soft tonality.

---

### Critical Rule: No "I" or "We" — Only "You"

Always include the **customer** in the formula. Never insert yourself:

-   ❌ *"If we were to set this up..."*
    
-   ❌ *"If I were to get you started..."*
    
-   ✅ *"If you were to go ahead..."*
    

---

## Correct vs. Incorrect Pretend Questions

### Real Estate Example:

**✅ Correct (Conditional, pressure-free):**

> *"Susan, if you were to decide on a home we've looked at, which one would you want to make an offer on?"*

**❌ Incorrect (Direct, creates pressure):**

> *"Susan, it's obvious which property is your favorite. How much do you want to offer for it?"*

The second version assumes the decision is made, skips the conditional frame, and puts immediate pressure on the prospect.

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Pretend questions are stepping stones** — small, low-risk decisions that lead to the final purchase
    
2.  **Direct questions too early = objections** — you'll hear "let me think about it" every time
    
3.  **Use conditional language** — "if you were to" and "would" remove all pressure
    
4.  **Never use "I" or "we"** — always frame it around "you" (the customer)
    
5.  **Tonality matters** — calm, relaxed, conversational with deliberate pauses
    
6.  **Let them imagine the future** — when they see themselves in the solution, they take ownership
    
7.  **Emotion seals it** — always follow up with "how would that make you feel?"
    

---

---

### 4: Solution Awareness Question Examples

https://youtu.be/UC1hwgellt8

# Lecture Notes: Solution Awareness Question Examples — A Full Dialogue Breakdown

---

## Full SAQ Dialogue Example: Lead Generation Services

This example walks through a complete Solution Awareness conversation. The prospect, **Keith**, has told you he's looking for better, higher quality leads for his business.

---

### Phase 1: What Have You Done So Far? (Past-Focused SAQ)

> **You:** *"Keith, before you started talking with us, were you out there looking for a higher quality lead for the business?"*

> **Keith:** *"We started using a company about 9 months ago. They've increased our sales by about 16%, which we're happy about."*

**Key move:** Don't accept surface-level satisfaction. Listen for qualifier words.

> **You:** *"You said a minute ago they were doing a 'somewhat decent' job. Can you tell me what you meant by 'somewhat'?"*

> **Keith:** *"Our sales people are selling more, but they're having to call more leads to make those sales. So we're spending about 10% more on leads."*

**Lesson:** Catching words like "somewhat" and asking the prospect to clarify opens the door to the real problem.

---

### Phase 2: Going Deeper — Uncovering the Emotional Stakes

> **You:** *"Getting the lead cost down — why is that so important to you now though?"*

> **Keith:** *"Our CEO wants a net 20% increase in sales. We're only at 6% halfway through the year."*

> **You:** *"If you don't get that 20% increase, would that have an impact on you?"*

> **Keith:** *"My whole department will get replaced."*

> **You:** *"Losing the job — what would that do to you?"*

> **Keith:** *"It would be devastating. I have two young kids, my spouse to take care of. It would be really hard to find a high-level position for several years."*

**Lesson:** You've moved from a business problem (lead cost) to a deeply personal, emotional reality (family security, career survival). This is where real motivation lives.

---

### Phase 3: Defining Their Ideal Criteria

> **You:** *"Besides wanting a higher quality lead, what else are you looking for in a lead provider? What would be your ideal criteria?"*

**Keith's criteria:**

1.  **More targeted leads** — when salespeople call, the prospect already knows who they are and why they're calling
    
2.  **Responsive support** — current provider takes 1-2 days to respond; they don't feel like a priority
    

> **You:** *"So the support's important to you then?"*

> **Keith:** *"The most important."*

**Lesson:** Let the prospect define their own criteria. You now know exactly what to emphasize when you present your solution.

---

### Phase 4: Future-Focused Pretend Questions

> **You:** *"Let's say we're able to get you that higher quality, more targeted lead — what would that do for your sales team?"*

> **Keith:** *"I think we could get to that 20% increase really easy."*

> **You:** *"But what would a 20% increase do for you personally though?"*

> **Keith:** *"It would take away so much stress."*

**The One-Word Probe:**

> **You:** *"Stress?"*

> **Keith:** *"Since my CEO put that 20% on my shoulders, it's hard to even sleep at night. It's causing family problems — I'm always zoned out at home thinking about work."*

**Lesson:** Sometimes the most powerful probe is a single word repeated as a question. *"Stress?"* opened up everything — sleep issues, family strain, emotional weight.

---

### Phase 5: Imagining the Transformed Future

> **You:** *"Once you get to that 20% sales increase, how do you see things being different than they are for you now?"*

> **Keith:** *"It would relieve stress, my CEO would be happy, and I think he would promote me — possibly to partner in the firm."*

> **You:** *"How would that make you feel?"*

> **Keith:** *"If I could get promoted, my family would be so happy. It would actually be a dream come true."*

**Lesson:** You've taken Keith from "I need better leads" all the way to "this could be a dream come true." He's now emotionally invested in the outcome — and you're the person helping him get there.

---

### Phase 6: Setting Up the Consequence Question

> **You:** *"What would you do if nothing changes though, and your sales stay at the same level they're at now?"*

This is the bridge to the next stage: **Consequence Questions** — getting the prospect to imagine and feel the pain of inaction.

---

## What This Dialogue Demonstrates

This conversation is a **two-way dialogue**, not a pitch. The skilled solution-clarifying and probing questions bring out the prospect's deepest feelings and emotional side.

**When you bring out their emotions:**

-   They **own their problems**
    
-   They see you as the person who can **help them solve** those problems
    
-   They no longer view you as just another salesperson trying to push them into something
    

---

## The Complete Picture You Now Have

By the end of Solution Awareness Questions, you can see:

1.  **Their present situation** — what's happening now
    
2.  **Their problems** — what's not working
    
3.  **What caused the problems** — root issues
    
4.  **What they've done in the past** — previous attempts and failures
    
5.  **Their ideal criteria** — what they actually want
    
6.  **Their emotional stakes** — what's really driving them
    

---

## What Comes Next in the Sales Process

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Next Step</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Purpose</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Consequence Questions</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Get them to imagine what happens if they do nothing — the cost of inaction</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Qualifying Questions</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Determine how important it is for them to change their situation</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Solution Presentation</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Present the specific advantages and benefits that solve their stated problems</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

**Preview:** The next course will teach you the most powerful question you can ask a prospect — one that respectfully challenges them to question their own way of thinking that has allowed the problem to continue. When they confront this, they become **open to change** — and open to doing it with you.

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Catch qualifier words** — "somewhat," "decent," "okay" are doors to the real problem
    
2.  **The one-word probe is incredibly powerful** — repeating a single emotional word as a question opens everything up
    
3.  **Move from business to personal** — the real motivation is never about leads or policies, it's about family, security, dreams
    
4.  **Let them define their criteria** — you'll know exactly what to present
    
5.  **Future visualization creates ownership** — when they see the dream, they'll fight to make it real
    
6.  **Never rush past emotion** — dig deeper every time they reveal something personal
    
7.  **Set up consequence questions** — after they see the dream, help them feel the cost of not pursuing it
    

---

# Lecture Notes: Solution Awareness Questions — Summary

---

## The Four Reasons to Use Solution Awareness Questions

### Reason 1: Prospects Tell You the Benefits Themselves

Your prospects will tell you what **they** see as the benefits and advantages of solving their problem. You don't have to sell the value — they articulate it for you.

### Reason 2: You Understand How They Think

You'll get an understanding of how your prospect thinks and **what they're prepared to do** about their situation — if anything.

### Reason 3: Their Ideas Become Part of the Solution

As prospects start to **own their problems**, their ideas become part of the solution. They'll tell you how the changes will make them feel.

### Reason 4: They Attach Emotions to the Outcome

Prospects will **attach their emotions** to the outcome of their own problem-solving. Once emotion is connected to the solution, the motivation to act becomes personal and powerful.

---

## Core Principle Reminder

**"Telling is not selling."**

When you ask the right questions, your prospect does the selling for you — they define the problem, envision the solution, feel the emotion, and take ownership of the outcome.

---

---
