# Lecture Notes: Consequence Questions — Understanding What Your Prospect's Questions Really Mean

---

## Course Overview

In this course, you will learn:

-   How to understand **what your prospect's questions really mean** before you answer them
    
-   **Consequence questions** and **qualifying questions** — the correct way to ask them
    
-   How these questions help you and your prospect discover how important it is for them to change their situation
    
-   How this ultimately gets them to **tell you the truth** so you never have to chase them
    

---

## Critical Skill: Never Answer a Question at Face Value

Before diving into consequence questions, there's a foundational principle you must master: **Understand what your prospect's question means before you rattle off an answer.**

Your previous experiences and assumptions might not reflect what your prospect is actually thinking. If you assume the reason behind their question, you risk answering the wrong question entirely — and killing the sale.

---

## Example 1: The Knife Set

**Scenario:** You sell knives. A prospect walks in.

> **Prospect:** *"Jeremy, how sharp are these knives you have on sale?"*

**What most salespeople say:**

> *"Oh yeah, they're extremely sharp — rated the sharpest on the market. They cut very deep, no rough edges. They're great!"*

**What the prospect then reveals:**

> *"I was looking for a knife set for my 15-year-old daughter. She loves to cook, but I'd be afraid she could really get a bad cut if they're that sharp."*

**What went wrong:** The prospect's question wasn't about sharpness as a feature — it was about **safety for their daughter**. But they didn't phrase it that way. The salesperson assumed the intent and gave a factual answer that actually scared the prospect away.

**What you should have asked:**

> *"I'm curious — did you ask me that for a reason?"*

This simple clarifying question would have uncovered the real concern (daughter's safety), putting you in a much better position to address the actual need.

---

## Example 2: The Consumer Goods Returns Policy

**Scenario:** A prospect is looking to stock a popular line of consumer goods and is meeting with you for the first time.

> **Prospect:** *"Jeremy, does your company accept returns?"*

**What most salespeople say:**

> *"We don't accept returns anymore. We found that accepting returns meant customers weren't incentivized to move the goods. About a year ago we established a policy of working out appropriate arrangements before doing business. It protects both of us."*

**What the prospect then reveals:**

> *"We got stuck with an inventory of torn labels and damaged products about six months ago and lost tens of thousands of dollars. There's no way I could survive a loss of that magnitude again. This just isn't going to work for us."*

**What went wrong:** The salesperson assumed the prospect was asking about returning unsold inventory (based on past experience with other retailers). But the prospect's real concern was about **receiving damaged goods and being stuck with the loss**. The salesperson answered the wrong question, the conversation ended, and no sale was made.

**The tragedy:** The prospect wanted the goods. The salesperson wanted the business. Both walked away empty-handed because of an assumption.

---

## The Core Lesson

**The question is never "Did the prospect ask the wrong question?"**

The real question is: **Did the salesperson assume they knew why the prospect was asking?**

When a prospect asks you a question:

-   **Don't assume** you know the reason behind it
    
-   **Don't rattle off** a factual answer based on your past experience
    
-   **Ask them to clarify** why they're asking
    

**The universal clarifying question:**

> *"I'm curious — did you ask me that for a reason?"*

This one question can save deals, uncover the real concern, and position you to provide the answer your prospect actually needs.

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Your assumptions are dangerous** — what you think the question means and what it actually means can be completely different
    
2.  **Never answer at face value** — always clarify the intent behind the question first
    
3.  **One clarifying question changes everything** — *"Did you ask me that for a reason?"* reveals the real concern
    
4.  **Both examples lost the sale** — not because the product was wrong, but because the salesperson answered the wrong question
    
5.  **Past experience can mislead you** — just because other prospects asked for one reason doesn't mean this prospect has the same reason
    
6.  **Understanding intent positions you as a trusted authority** — you solve the real problem, not the surface question
    

---

---

### 2: Qualifying Questions

https://youtu.be/Fv2eiE0K3xc

# Lecture Notes: Qualifying Questions — When, Where, and How to Use Them

---

## Why Qualifying Questions Matter

Qualifying questions exist so you **don't waste your time or your prospect's time** if they're not ready to move forward. The engagement stage takes your prospects through the sales conversation while your skilled questions qualify them throughout the process.

**Important nuance:** Sometimes it's not necessary to ask a qualifying question at all. If your prospect's answers to your earlier questions already show they want to change their situation, they've **qualified themselves**. You only need to ask qualifying questions when you pick up on clues that suggest uncertainty or inability to proceed.

---

## The Three Points Where You Qualify

### 1\. At the Beginning of the Sales Conversation

**Purpose:** Determine if they have the ability to access the necessary resources to pay for your solution, in addition to having interest in making a change.

**When to use:** If you pick up clues early on that they may be in a bad financial situation or may not have the funds to do business with you.

**Example:**

> *"Mr. Prospect, if you were able to find something that could get you what you're looking for... what type of funding would you have to put into it?"*

**Notice:**

-   It's not pushy or aggressive
    
-   You're simply asking to see if you can help
    
-   The focus shifts **onto them and away from you**
    
-   Now **they need to sell you** — you see the difference in this approach
    

If they genuinely can't access funding, you can't help them — and continuing wastes both your time.

---

### 2\. During the Middle of the Sales Conversation

**When to use:** If you pick up on clues during the conversation that suggest they may not be fully committed or engaged.

These are **light, neutral questions** designed to tactfully motivate them to move forward:

-   *"Amy, why is that so important to you now... though?"*
    
-   *"Shaq, how would that make you feel to be able to do that?"*
    
-   *"Asema, is this important for you to change your situation?"*
    
-   *"Damian, how important is it for you to solve this problem?"*
    
-   *"Dr. Jones, how do you see this being beneficial to you and your hospital?"*
    
-   *"What are some ways this could help you, in your opinion?"*
    
-   *"If you could, what would it do for you personally?"*
    
-   *"How important is this for you now?"*
    

**Delivery tip:** Pause naturally. Don't rush through these questions or you'll sound scripted — and you'll get pushback.

---

### 3\. Toward the End of the Engagement Stage

**When to use:** After you've asked a powerful consequence question — especially if the prospect **hasn't been very engaged** during the conversation.

**On the flip side:** If they've already qualified themselves repeatedly through their answers ("I need to do this now," "I need to make a change"), you don't need to ask a qualifying question. They've already done it for you.

**If they haven't self-qualified, ask something like:**

> *"Joseph, how important is it for you to change your situation and start \_\_\_?"*

**Solar example:**

> *"Barbara, how important is it for you to change your situation and start saving money on your utility bills every month?"*

**Key rule:** Plug in **one or two things they've told you** they want. Keep it brief — just mention what your solution can provide that they've already expressed wanting.

---

## The Psychological Power of Qualifying Questions

The qualification process is actually **more important for your prospects than it is for you**. Here's why:

-   It **reinforces and imprints** in their mind the decision to change their situation with you
    
-   It gets them to **persuade themselves**
    
-   It makes them **psychologically own** the benefits of your solution
    

**Example of a self-qualifying SAQ:**

> *"Sally, tell me — how do you see this working for you and your organization?"*

Then sit back and let them tell you in their own words how they see the benefits. This psychologically qualifies them and starts the self-persuasion process.

**Remember:** You're the facilitator taking them through this journey.

---

## The Fine Line: Don't Over-Qualify

Most salespeople waste enormous time by **not qualifying** their prospects. However, there's a fine line — if you go **too heavy** on the qualifying process, you'll turn off many qualified prospects.

**Balance is key:** Qualify enough to ensure you're not wasting time, but not so aggressively that you push good prospects away.

---

## Cardinal Sin: Never Say "If I Could Show You..."

**Never start a qualifying question like this:**

> ❌ *"Mr. Jones, if I could show you how to do that, would you be interested?"*

**Why this is fatal:**

-   It puts the focus on **you and your agenda**
    
-   It puts you in a position where you now have to **prove** with logical facts and features that your solution works
    
-   It puts the prospect **on the defensive**
    
-   It creates **sales resistance** and builds a wall between you and your customer
    

---

## The Neutral Alternative

Instead of "If I could show you," reframe with **neutral language** that keeps the focus on them:

-   ❌ *"How about this..."* — Still focuses on you
    
-   ❌ *"Let me show you..."* — Still focuses on you
    
-   ✅ *"If there was a way..."* — Neutral, keeps focus on the prospect
    

The correct choice is always the one that keeps the prospect at the center of the conversation.

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Qualify at three points** — beginning (funds), middle (engagement), and end (commitment) — but only when needed
    
2.  **Listen for self-qualification** — if they're already saying "I need to change," you don't need to ask
    
3.  **Qualifying is more for them than you** — it reinforces their decision and imprints commitment
    
4.  **Don't over-qualify** — too heavy and you lose good prospects
    
5.  **Never say "If I could show you"** — it shifts focus to you and triggers defensiveness
    
6.  **Use "If there was a way..."** — neutral language that keeps the prospect at the center
    
7.  **Pause and deliver naturally** — scripted delivery kills credibility
    
8.  **Plug in their words** — reference what they've told you when asking qualifying questions
    

---

---

### 3: Sequencing Consequence and Clarifying Questions

# Lecture Notes: Sequencing Consequence and Qualifying Questions — From Engagement to Transition

---

## The Practice Principle

To master these questions, you must **rehearse and practice** — repeatedly. Think of it like Hollywood actors: every line is 100% scripted, yet when they deliver it on screen, it sounds completely natural. That's the level you need to reach.

**How to practice:**

-   Role-play with a colleague, friend, or spouse
    
-   Practice in front of a mirror
    
-   Use flashcards with the right questions for each stage on the back
    

**If you want to be a top performer, you have to be willing to do what others are not willing to do.** You will always get paid the most.

---

## The Full Sequence: Consequence → Qualifying → Transition

This section demonstrates how the three elements flow together in a real conversation. The example uses a lead generation services scenario with a prospect named Alex.

---

### Step 1: The Consequence Question

**The setup — soften before you ask:**

> *"Alex, can I ask you another question? I don't want to offend you because I've enjoyed the conversation, but..."*

**The consequence question:**

> *"What are you going to do if nothing changes? If you keep getting the same results from your leads the next 6 or even 12 months?"*

**Alternative phrasing:**

> *"What if you don't do anything about this and you keep getting the same results the next 3, 6, or even a whole year?"*

**Alex's response:** *"We'd have to cut back on expanding the business for sure."*

**Follow-up probe:**

> *"Are you willing to settle for that?"*

**Alex:** *"No way. We have to do something about this."*

---

### Step 2: The Qualifying Question

> *"How important is it for you to change your situation and start getting a higher quality lead so you can make more money to expand the company?"*

**Alex:** *"Very important."*

**Probe deeper:**

> *"Why now though?"*

**Alex:** *"I'm tired of this other company. They keep telling us it'll get better but it doesn't."*

**Continue probing:**

> *"Is that having an impact on the company?"*

**Alex:** *"Absolutely. We want to expand but the lack of sales from not having better leads is hurting us."*

> *"Time for a change, possibly?"*

**Alex:** *"Absolutely."*

---

### Step 3: The Transition into Presenting

**The transition formula:**

> *"Based on what you've told me, what we're doing might actually work for you. Because you know how you said \[their problem\]... and because of that it's making you feel \[their emotion\]... well, this is what I do, and this is how it would work for you."*

**The three components:**

1.  **"Based on what you've told me..."** — anchors everything to their own words
    
2.  **"You know how you said..."** — references their specific problem
    
3.  **"And it's making you feel..."** — connects to their emotion
    

---

## Critical Technique: Downplay Their Emotions

When reflecting their emotions back during the transition, **always downplay** with soft language:

-   ✅ *"a little bit of stress"*
    
-   ✅ *"a little bit frustrated sometimes"*
    
-   ✅ *"a little bit worried"*
    
-   ✅ *"a little bit of anxiety"*
    

**Why this works:** When you downplay, the prospect will **correct you upward** and make the pain bigger themselves:

> **You:** *"I think you mentioned a little bit of stress..."* **Prospect:** *"A little bit?! You have no idea — I can't even sleep at night!"*

**Why NOT to overstate:** If you say "this is causing you a LOT of stress," certain personalities (especially A-types) will push back and downplay to avoid looking weak in front of you: *"Well, it's not really that bad."* — This is the opposite of what you want.

**Then probe the amplified emotion:**

> *"What's that doing to you?"* *"I see... so it's important for you to do something then."*

---

## Transition Example: Real Estate

> *"Amy, based on what you told me, what we're doing might actually work for you. Because you know how you said that you want to find a home in a better neighborhood... and right now you're in your house, but because of the recent break-ins in the area, it's caused you to feel, I think you mentioned... a little bit of worry sometimes..."*

**Delivery notes:**

-   Don't rush — slow down
    
-   Keep it conversational
    
-   Keep the focus on them and their problems
    

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **The sequence matters:** Consequence question → Qualifying question → Transition to presenting
    
2.  **Soften before consequence questions** — *"I don't want to offend you, but..."*
    
3.  **Always downplay emotions** — use "a little bit of..." so the prospect amplifies the pain themselves
    
4.  **The transition formula:** "Based on what you've told me... you said \[problem\]... it's making you feel \[emotion\]... here's how we can help"
    
5.  **Never overstate their pain** — A-type personalities will push back and minimize
    
6.  **Practice until it sounds natural** — scripted delivery kills the entire process
    
7.  **Reference their own words** — everything you present should be anchored to what they told you
    

---

---

# Lecture Notes: Engagement Stage Summary — Complete Review

---

## What You've Learned in the Engagement Stage

If you've completed the courses in the correct order, you now have knowledge of the following question types and skills:

1.  **Connecting Questions** — building rapport and trust
    
2.  **Situation Questions** — understanding their current reality
    
3.  **Problem Awareness Questions** — uncovering their problems and the emotions behind them
    
4.  **Solution Awareness Questions** — discovering what they've done, what they want, and how they see the future
    
5.  **Consequence Questions** — getting them to feel the cost of inaction
    
6.  **Clarifying and Probing Questions** — going deeper at every stage
    
7.  **Qualifying Questions** — ensuring they're ready, willing, and able to move forward
    

---

## Core Skills Mastered

Beyond the question types, you now understand:

-   The power of **active listening**
    
-   How to **keep the focus on your prospect** (not on you)
    
-   How to use **neutral language** that doesn't trigger sales resistance
    

---

## Self-Assessment

If you're not fully confident in any of these areas, **go back and repeat those specific chapters**. The more you go through the training, the more you master the methodology — and the more prospects you'll be able to help, leading to more sales for you, your company, and your family.

---

## The Bigger Picture: Old Model vs. New Model

By now you should have a solid understanding of the differences between the **old model** and the **new model** of selling. As you move into the Transitional and Commitment stages, the contrast between the two will become even more clear.

---

## What's Next

You're now ready to move from the **Engagement Stage** into the **Transitional Stage** — where you'll learn how to bridge from understanding the prospect's world to presenting your solution in a way that feels natural, not pushy.

# Lecture Notes: Transition to Your Presentation — Opening the Door to Your Solution

---

## Where You Are in the Sales Process

You've now completed:

1.  **The Connecting Stage** — building rapport and trust
    
2.  **The Engagement Stage** — skilled questioning, active listening, uncovering needs
    

Now it's time for the **Transitional Stage** — where you bridge from understanding their world to presenting how you can help them.

---

## What You'll Learn

How to:

-   Open the door to present your solution
    
-   Give your prospects the specific **advantages and benefits** of what you're offering
    
-   Base everything on **the answers they gave** to your skilled questions
    

---

## Why You're in a Powerful Position

By this point, you've:

-   Asked skilled questions throughout the engagement stage
    
-   Been fully present and attentive
    
-   Shown your prospect you **care about them** by listening carefully to their answers
    

This creates a **strategic moment** — you're now ready to present your solution from a position of trust and authority.

---

## Key Principle: Presenting Is Only 10%

In the new model of selling, presenting is only **10% of the process**. This is where you:

-   **Feed back** what they told you they're looking for
    
-   **Match** your solution to their stated needs
    
-   **Piece together** all of their logical AND emotional needs
    
-   **Demonstrate** that you have a real understanding of what they want
    

---

## The Four Steps of the Transitional Presentation

### Step 1: Briefly Summarize Their Problems

Recap the main **logical and emotional problems** they told you they're having. Keep it concise — use their own words.

### Step 2: Explain What You Do

Keep this **brief yet specific**. This is not the time for a long company pitch — just enough for them to understand what you offer.

### Step 3: Present Your Features and Benefits

Go over your solution — but only the **features and benefits that solve their specific problems**, based on the answers they've given you. Nothing extra, nothing generic.

### Step 4: Ask a Question

After presenting, you ask a question. *(The type of question to ask will be covered in the next section.)*

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **The transition is earned** — you can only present effectively because you listened first
    
2.  **Presenting is only 10%** — the other 90% was the connecting and engagement work
    
3.  **Feed back their own words** — summarize their logical and emotional needs before presenting
    
4.  **Keep it brief and specific** — no generic pitches; only what solves their stated problems
    
5.  **Always end with a question** — the conversation stays two-way, even during the presentation
    

---

---

### 2: Understanding the Objections

https://youtu.be/QnUL2oBjEbk

# Lecture Notes: Understanding Objections — Helping Prospects Overcome Their Own Concerns

---

## Context: Where You Are

You've completed your presentation. At this point in the sales conversation, your prospect **might have some concerns**. This section covers how to handle them.

---

## Reframing: Objections Are "Concerns"

The word "objection" carries a **negative connotation** — it implies confrontation and resistance. Instead, reframe objections as **concerns**.

This isn't just semantics. When you think of them as concerns, your mindset shifts from "overcoming" to "helping" — which changes your entire approach.

---

## The Core Principle

The goal is to **help your potential customers overcome their own concerns** — rather than you trying to overcome them for them.

This is consistent with everything you've learned so far: the prospect owns the problem, owns the solution, and now they need to own the resolution of their concerns too. Your role remains that of a **facilitator**, not a debater.

---

## Self-Reflection Question

How do you normally react when a prospect brings up an objection? *(Consider this honestly — your default reaction reveals whether you're operating from the old model or the new model of selling.)*

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Call them concerns, not objections** — language shapes your approach
    
2.  **Help them overcome their own concerns** — don't try to do it for them
    
3.  **Stay in facilitator mode** — you're guiding, not arguing
    
4.  **Your reaction matters** — how you respond to concerns determines whether trust is maintained or broken
    

---

---

### 3: The Suppose Question

https://youtu.be/1nMMhAMnZ4k

# Lecture Notes: The Suppose Question — Uncovering All Concerns Before Resolving Any

---

## The Critical Discovery: One Concern or Multiple?

Before you can help a customer overcome their concerns, you must first **accurately discover if they have just one concern or multiple concerns**.

**Why multiple concerns arise:**

-   Influences from **outside your conversation** — a CEO, other departments, or organizational stakeholders may have concerns you haven't addressed
    
-   If selling to a family, the **spouse** may have separate concerns
    
-   These are factors that **may not have surfaced** during your engagement with the prospect
    

---

## The Process

1.  Through your skilled questions, you discover their **first concern**
    
2.  You clarify **what's behind** that concern
    
3.  But you're **not done** — you need to find out if there are other concerns that could hold them back
    
4.  To do this, you ask **The Suppose Question**
    

---

## The Suppose Question

> *"Sally, let's suppose that we were able to resolve that issue with you. Now I know it's not resolved right now, but let's just pretend we could — are there any other issues you might have that you would want to see resolved?"*

**Delivery:** Slow down. Pause. Keep it conversational.

**The outcome is binary:**

-   They tell you the first concern is **their only concern**, OR
    
-   They reveal **additional concerns** you need to address
    

---

## The Suppose Question Formula — Three Parts

### Part 1: *"Let's suppose that we were able to resolve that issue with you..."*

**Purpose:** Gets your customer to envision **both of you working together** to resolve the concern.

**Key word: "with you"** — not "for you." This frames it as a **team effort**, not you doing something to them or for them.

---

### Part 2: *"Now I know it's not resolved right now, but let's just pretend we could..."*

**Purpose:**

-   **Acknowledges and respects** their concern — you're not dismissing it
    
-   Lets them know their first concern **will be addressed** — you're not sweeping it under the rug
    
-   Shows you are **fully present** in the conversation
    

---

### Part 3: *"Are there any other issues you might have that you would want to see resolved?"*

**Purpose:** Helps you and your prospect **separate the first concern from any additional concerns** they might have. This gives you the complete picture before you attempt to resolve anything.

---

## Why This Matters

If you try to resolve the first concern without knowing about others, you may:

-   Successfully address one issue only to have another surface immediately
    
-   Lose momentum and credibility
    
-   Miss the real blocker entirely
    

The Suppose Question ensures you have **all concerns on the table** before you start resolving any of them.

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Always find out if there are multiple concerns** — never assume the first one is the only one
    
2.  **Outside influences create hidden concerns** — CEOs, spouses, stakeholders may have objections you haven't heard
    
3.  **"With you" not "for you"** — frame resolution as a team effort
    
4.  **Acknowledge before moving past** — respect their concern, don't sweep it aside
    
5.  **Get the full picture first** — separate and identify all concerns before resolving any
    
6.  **Practice the formula** — delivery and pacing matter as much as the words
    

---

---

### 4: How to Handle Demands That You Cannot Meet

https://youtu.be/4EqzCbbm_ls

# Lecture Notes: How to Handle Demands You Cannot Meet

---

## The Reality

There will be times when your prospects have demands that are **beyond your authority or control**. You need a formula to handle these situations professionally without losing the sale or the trust you've built.

---

## The Three-Step Formula

### Step 1: Repeat What They Want

Show them you're listening and that you understand their request.

> *"John, let me make sure I understand what you want..."*

Then **include their specific objection or interest**.

---

### Step 2: Take a Moment to Frame Your Response

> *"Give me a second while I put this into your perspective."*

This signals that you're thoughtfully considering their request, not dismissing it.

---

### Step 3: Turn Down the Request with a Neutral Explanation

> *"I want you to know why that's further than I can actually go."*

**Key phrase: "I want you to know..."** — This is a very neutral way of saying "no." It:

-   **Respects your prospect** — you're not being dismissive or confrontational
    
-   **Gets them to think** — rather than triggering defensiveness
    
-   Shows you're **thinking about their concern**, not just rejecting it
    

---

## Why This Works

Because of the trust you've built through your skilled questions and listening throughout the engagement stage, your prospect views you as **The Trusted Authority**. With the right dialogue:

-   You and your prospect remain **open to each other**
    
-   Your neutral explanation, combined with the right follow-up questions, leads them to **understand and agree with you** most of the time
    
-   If they trust you as the authority to help them, **why would they go with someone else?**
    

---

## Follow-Up Questions: Invite Collaboration

After saying no, don't leave it there. Invite them to work through it together:

> *"Would you like to go over with me what I think would work for you?"*

Or:

> *"John, I might have a possible solution to that. Would you like to go over that with me?"*

**Delivery:** Slow down. Watch your tonality. Keep it calm and inviting.

**What these questions accomplish:**

-   You **invite** your prospect to work out their concerns **with you, together**
    
-   Both of you agree to **find a way to get there**
    
-   The prospect feels like a partner, not someone being sold to
    

---

## From Here: Uncover Solutions Together

Once they agree to explore options with you:

-   Briefly discuss their concerns using **questions from the engagement stage**
    
-   Use **Solution Awareness Questions** to explore alternatives
    
-   Ask them to participate in the resolution:
    

> *"How do you see yourself being able to resolve your concern, Sally?"*

This keeps the prospect in the driver's seat — they're solving their own concern with your guidance.

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Repeat their request first** — show you understand before responding
    
2.  **"I want you to know..."** — the neutral way to say no without triggering resistance
    
3.  **Never just say no and stop** — always follow with a collaborative invitation
    
4.  **Invite them to solve it with you** — "Would you like to go over what I think would work?"
    
5.  **Trust is your leverage** — they'll accept your guidance because you've earned authority
    
6.  **Use engagement stage questions** — your earlier skills are powerful tools for resolving concerns too
    
7.  **Tonality and pacing matter** — slow down, stay calm, stay conversational
    

---

---

### 5: Help Your Prospects Overcome Their Own Concerns

https://youtu.be/y-7I89Krib8

# Lecture Notes: Help Your Prospects Overcome Their Own Concerns

---

## Where You Are in the Conversation

At this point, your prospect is **emotionally involved** with their problem and **emotionally invested** in wanting to solve it — all because of the skilled questions you've asked throughout the engagement stage. They're now ready to start telling you how **they** feel they can resolve their concerns.

---

## Diffusing Questions

After you've discussed what the concern is, use **diffusing questions** to get them to resolve the concern themselves:

-   *"John, let's suppose that it wasn't what you thought it was..."*
    
-   *"Sally, suppose you could..."*
    
-   *"John, what if we could..."*
    
-   *"Amy, what if you could..."*
    

Then **plug in an alternative view** to theirs — one that addresses their concern while connecting to what they told you they wanted.

---

## Health Coaching Example

**The concern:** Mr. Jones has tried weight loss programs before but never had success because he lacked support.

**His deeper motivation (from earlier questions):** He wants to live longer to be around for his grandkids.

**The diffusing question:**

> *"Mr. Jones, if there was a way that you could get that support you wanted and have a coach that would assist you weekly to make sure you were actually losing weight — so you can be around for your grandkids — would you feel comfortable working with me on helping you get to that point?"*

**The formula:**

-   Plug in **what they said they wanted** (weekly support)
    
-   Connect it to **their emotional motivation** (grandkids)
    
-   Match your solution to **their desired outcome**
    

---

## Checking for Agreement

Before moving to closing/committing questions, check that your prospect is on the same page:

-   *"Amy, would you feel comfortable with that?"*
    
-   *"Tony, how does that look so far to you?"*
    
-   *"Would that work for you?"*
    
-   *"Would that be appropriate?"*
    
-   *"Would this help you?"*
    
-   *"Is there anything else you'd like to address to me at this point?"*
    

**That last question is critical.** After resolving their first concern, never assume it was their only concern. Always ask if there's anything else.

---

## Handling the #1 Concern: "We Can't Afford It"

This is the most common concern in sales. Variations include:

-   *"We don't have the budget for that."*
    
-   *"Your price is too high."*
    

### The Step-by-Step Process

**Step 1: Isolate the concern — is it money or value?**

> *"Tell me, if you did have the money, would this be something that would work for you?"*

Most of the time they'll say: *"Yeah, I think it would. We just don't have the money."*

**Step 2: Get them to articulate the value**

> *"Hold on — why do you feel like it would work for you though?"*

They explain the benefits in their own words, reinforcing their desire for the solution.

**Step 3: Put the funding challenge back on them**

> *"I can appreciate that money might be an issue. From what you told me, how do you think you can resolve that — where you can find the money so that you can \[plug in what they said they wanted\]?"*

**The key alignment:** You're tying **getting the funding** to **having what they said they wanted**. If they don't find the money, they can't have what they want. This creates internal motivation to problem-solve.

---

### What Happens When You Do This Correctly

Because of the trust they have in you, **they will come up with ways** to fund the purchase themselves:

-   Using a credit card
    
-   Getting a loan
    
-   Refinancing their house
    
-   Borrowing from a 401K or investments
    
-   Getting their boss to reallocate budget from another department
    

**You didn't suggest any of these** — they came up with them on their own.

---

### If They Still Can't Find Funding

Ask:

> *"I see. Well, what other avenues do you have to find the funding so that you can \[what they said they wanted\]?"*

**Industry examples:**

-   **Home security:** *"John, what other avenues do you have to find the funding to protect your home and family?"*
    
-   **Health coaching/supplements:** *"Sally, what other avenues do you have to find the funding to get your health back for you and your family?"*
    
-   **Rental/commercial property:** *"John, what other avenues do you have to find the funding to purchase the rental property so you can get a better return on your money?"*
    

**Every version plugs in their stated desire** — you're always connecting money to their emotional outcome.

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Diffusing questions let them resolve their own concerns** — "What if you could..." and "Suppose that..."
    
2.  **Always plug in what they told you they wanted** — connect your solution to their emotional motivation
    
3.  **Check for agreement before closing** — and always ask if there are additional concerns
    
4.  **"We can't afford it" is rarely the real objection** — isolate whether it's a money problem or a value problem first
    
5.  **Put the funding challenge back on them** — they'll come up with solutions you'd never suggest
    
6.  **Tie funding to their desire** — no money = no outcome; this creates urgency to solve the funding problem
    
7.  **Never assume one concern is the only concern** — always ask "Is there anything else?"
    

---

---

## Chapter 9

### 1: Words that Trigger Sales Pressure

https://youtu.be/kxOb9FF62fo

# Lecture Notes: Words That Trigger Sales Pressure — The Commitment Stage

---

## Welcome to the Commitment Stage

You've made it through the Connecting Stage, the Engagement Stage, and the Transitional Stage. Now you're entering the final phase: **The Commitment Stage** — where the prospect makes the decision to move forward.

But before you learn how to close, you need to understand what **not** to say.

---

## Pressure-Triggering Phrases

Look at these common sales statements and questions:

-   *"I'm just calling to see if you would be interested in..."*
    
-   *"Can I come by and show you what we can do for you?"*
    
-   *"How about we schedule another call to move this forward for you?"*
    
-   *"I can meet you tomorrow at noon or Friday at 3 — which time works for you, Mr. Jones?"*
    
-   *"Do you still want to move this forward?"*
    
-   *"When should I follow up with you again to make sure we meet your needs?"*
    

---

## What Do These All Have in Common?

These phrases all **create sales pressure**. They:

-   Put the focus on **you and your agenda**, not the prospect
    
-   Push the prospect toward a decision **they may not be ready to make**
    
-   Use language that signals "I'm trying to close you" — which triggers resistance
    
-   Make the prospect feel like they're being **managed or manipulated** rather than helped
    

This is exactly the kind of language that causes prospects to pull back and give you the objections you've already learned about: *"Let me think about it," "I'll get back to you," "I need to talk to my boss."*

---

## Key Takeaway

The words you use in the commitment stage matter enormously. **Pressure-triggering language undoes all the trust you've built** through the connecting and engagement stages. The next sections will teach you how to ask for commitment in a way that feels natural, pressure-free, and consistent with the new model of selling.

---

---

### 2: Traditional Closing Questions

https://youtu.be/LG0gg4fGtlU

# Lecture Notes: Traditional Closing Questions — Why They No Longer Work

---

## The Problem with Traditional Selling

Traditional selling techniques cause salespeople to play **the numbers game** because they work **against human nature**. These techniques are designed to **push the prospect forward** rather than getting the prospect to **pull you in**.

Ask yourself:

-   Are you "going in for the kill" with your closing techniques?
    
-   Are you following the "Always Be Closing" mantra?
    

---

## The Post-Trust Era Reality

In today's world, trust is at an all-time low. Your potential customers **can feel when you're trying to push and close them**. Old closing techniques do nothing more than **put pressure on your potential customer**.

**Think about it from your own experience:** How do you feel when a salesperson pressures you? You feel that pressure — and you fight back. If you feel it, your prospect feels it too.

When prospects feel chased, they do one of two things:

1.  **Get defensive** — throw out objections and reject what you're offering
    
2.  **Withdraw** — disappear, stop returning calls, emails, and texts
    

**If you're getting constant objections, ask yourself: who's actually causing them?** You are — because of the way you've been taught to communicate.

---

## Assumptive Selling Is Dead

The old training says "always assume the sale." In today's era, **assumptive selling is dead**. Any training that teaches this is outdated — created by gurus who haven't been in sales for decades, or newer trainers who simply copied the old material.

**Example of assumptive selling gone wrong:**

> **Salesperson:** *"Whose name will the contract be in?"* **Prospect:** *(hesitantly)* *"I guess my name..."* **Salesperson:** *"Great. Phone number? Address? Bank details for the EFT?"* **Prospect:** *"Wait — I never said I was ready to buy. Leave me a brochure. I'll talk to my spouse and call you back."*

**The irony:** The very techniques that teach you to assume the sale — which trigger sales resistance — then require you to learn objection handling techniques to overcome the objections **you caused**. Why not learn how to avoid getting the objection in the first place?

---

## The ABCs of Closing — Outdated Techniques

These are among the first things salespeople are taught. None of them work effectively in the post-trust era:

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 75px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Technique</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Example</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Problem</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Option Close</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Do you want the red one or the blue one?"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Assumes the decision is made</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Invitational Close</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Why don't you give it a try?"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Minimizes the decision's importance</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Assumptive Close</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Do you want delivery Tuesday or Wednesday?"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Skips consent entirely</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Choice Close</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Contract in your name or the company's?"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Forces a decision they haven't made</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Demonstration Close</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"If I could show you the best investment, would you want to see it?"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Puts focus on you, forces you to prove yourself</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

**The "If I could show you" trap:** This language immediately shifts focus onto you. Now you're in a weak position — having to prove your product with stats, facts, and features. But people bond on **emotion, not logic**. And every other salesperson is already claiming to be the best — your prospect is completely desensitized to these claims.

---

## The New Model Language

Instead of: *"If I could show you the very best investment, would you want to see that?"*

Say: *"John, if there was an investment out there that could get you the returns you're looking for, would that be of interest to you?"*

**The difference:**

-   **Neutral position** — no resistance created
    
-   **No need to prove yourself** — the focus stays on their desired outcome
    
-   Applies to **any industry, product, or service**
    

**Key distinction:** New model salespeople are always **neutral**. Average salespeople are always **one-sided** — focused on themselves instead of their prospect's world.

---

## The Fundamental Shift

In the new model of selling, the salesperson **doesn't even use the word "closing."** It has a negative stigma and can be dehumanizing toward your potential customer.

**Consider:** If you overheard a salesperson say they "just closed you," how would that make you feel? That mentality comes through in your communication style whether you realize it or not.

**Write this down:** Today's consumer doesn't want to be talked at and sold to. They want to be **asked, heard, and understood.**

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Traditional closes trigger the very objections you're trying to overcome** — you're causing your own problems
    
2.  **Assumptive selling is dead** — prospects feel it immediately and resist
    
3.  **"If I could show you" puts you in a weak position** — you now have to prove yourself
    
4.  **Use neutral language** — "If there was a way..." keeps focus on them, not you
    
5.  **New model = neutral; old model = one-sided** — this is the core distinction
    
6.  **Stop using the word "closing"** — it's dehumanizing and shapes your mindset negatively
    
7.  **People want to be asked, heard, and understood** — not talked at and sold to
    
8.  **If you're getting objections, look at your own approach first** — you may be the one causing them
    

---

---

### 3: Commiting Questions

https://youtu.be/MvL_abH3WnE

# Lecture Notes: Committing Questions — The New Model of Closing

---

## Defining Commitment

What most people call "closing" is actually **commitment** — and it's the last of your prospect's many **logical conclusions** throughout the sales conversation. It's not a trick or a pressure moment; it's the natural endpoint of everything you've built together.

By this point, you've:

-   Demonstrated your understanding of their problems
    
-   Found the correct solution to solve those problems
    
-   You're now putting the proposed solution into effect
    

---

## Two Forms of Commitment

### Form 1: Commitment to Smaller Action Steps

A series of **intermediate action steps** that lead to the final sale. This is appropriate when the prospect needs additional steps in the discovery process before making the final decision.

### Form 2: Commitment to Purchase

A direct commitment to **purchase your solution** and do business with you and your company.

---

## The Decision You Must Make

At this point, you need to decide which path is appropriate:

1.  **Ask for the commitment to purchase** — if they're ready
    
2.  **Suggest another step in the discovery process** — to move them forward toward the final decision
    

**The key question:** What moves the prospect forward at this specific point in the process?

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Commitment, not closing** — reframe your mindset and language
    
2.  **It's a logical conclusion** — not a pressure moment; it's the natural result of the entire conversation
    
3.  **Two paths exist** — direct purchase or intermediate steps; read the situation to choose correctly
    
4.  **You've earned this moment** — through skilled questions, listening, and genuine understanding
    
5.  **The prospect leads themselves here** — if you've followed the process, commitment feels natural, not forced
    

---

---

### 4: Catapult Your Commissions

https://youtu.be/Td0Xvucdf_4

# Lecture Notes: Catapult Your Commissions — Committing Questions in Action

---

## The Power Shift

When you give up on what you thought was power — assuming the sale, using old-school pushy closing techniques — and replace it with committing questions, something transformative happens:

Your prospects will view you **completely differently** than any other salesperson they've ever dealt with. In fact, they won't view you as a salesperson at all. You become:

-   A **Trusted Authority**
    
-   An **advisor**
    
-   A **friend** in your market
    

**You are the authority in their mind.** Why would they go anywhere else?

---

## The Committing Question Formula

The core committing question follows this structure:

> *"Do you feel like this is something you can \[do/have\] to \[benefit they stated\]?"*

After they say yes, **always follow with a probing question** to bring out more emotion:

> *"Well, hold on — why do you feel like it would though?"*

---

## Industry Examples

### Coaching:

> *"John, do you feel like this is something you can do to get you where you're wanting to go in your life?"* → *"Yeah, I do."* → *"Well, why though?"*

### Financial Services:

> *"Do you feel like this is something you can do to get you where you're wanting to go with your investing?"* → *"Yeah, I do."* → *"Hold on — why do you feel like it is though?"*

### Insurance:

> *"Sally, do you feel like this is something you can have to protect your family in case something happened to you?"* → *"Yeah, I think it would."* → *"Hold on — why do you feel like it would though?"*

### Real Estate:

> *"Do you feel like this is the home for you and your family?"* → *"Yeah, I really like it."* → *"Well, why though?"*

**The pattern:** Always plug in the **specific benefits they told you they wanted** at the end of the committing question.

---

## The Calendar Statement Commitment

This is where you ask for the commitment **at a specific point in time**. By this stage:

-   Prior agreements are in place and carried through the conversation
    
-   The prospect feels their momentum
    
-   They've bought into you as the trusted authority
    
-   Agreeing to do business is simply **a logical conclusion**
    

**The Calendar Statement:**

> *"Amy, in order for us to do this for you, would it be appropriate for us to get out our calendars and schedule the next steps?"*

**Key phrase: "In order to do this for you"** — this shows you want to carry out their wishes to solve their issues. It's about them, not you.

---

## The Mindset

There are **no tricks, no fancy moves, no manipulation techniques**. The commitment is the natural conclusion of a well-run sales conversation.

**The critical mindset shift:**

> Always be **detached from the expectation** of making the sale. Instead, focus on **whether or not there's a sale to be made** in the first place.

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Committing questions replace closing techniques** — they feel natural, not forced
    
2.  **The formula:** "Do you feel like this is something you can \[do/have\] to \[their stated benefit\]?"
    
3.  **Always probe after they say yes** — "Why do you feel like it would though?" deepens emotional commitment
    
4.  **Plug in their words** — the benefit at the end must be what they told you they wanted
    
5.  **The Calendar Statement** — "In order to do this for you, would it be appropriate to schedule next steps?"
    
6.  **"In order to do this for you"** — frames everything as fulfilling their wishes
    
7.  **Detach from the sale** — focus on whether a sale should be made, not on forcing one
    
8.  **No tricks needed** — if you've followed the process, commitment is the logical conclusion
    

---

---

### 5: Commitment Stage Summary

https://youtu.be/I_H-X7yzQg8

# Lecture Notes: Commitment Stage Summary — Final Review & The Path Forward

---

## Congratulations

You've completed the entire training program — all stages of the new model of selling:

1.  **Connecting Stage** — building rapport and trust
    
2.  **Engagement Stage** — skilled questioning, active listening, uncovering needs
    
3.  **Transitional Stage** — bridging to your presentation
    
4.  **Commitment Stage** — earning the decision naturally
    

---

## The Critical Reminder: Training Is Not a One-Time Event

Going through the material once is just the beginning. **Training isn't something you do once — it's something you do daily.**

It's what you do **continually** to:

-   Improve and develop your skills
    
-   Become a far more effective salesperson
    
-   Be more connected and invested in truly helping prospects solve their problems
    

---

## The Repetition Formula

**Repetition is key.** The recommended practice:

-   Go through **3 to 5 videos every single day**
    
-   **7 days a week**
    
-   Continuously revisit and reinforce the material
    

**The promise:** If you commit to daily training, you will be more successful than you ever thought possible as a sales professional. You'll help more people solve their problems — and you'll make a lot of money doing it.

---

## Key Takeaways from the Entire Program

1.  **Telling is not selling** — ask skilled questions, don't lecture
    
2.  **People buy on emotion, not logic** — always uncover feelings, not just facts
    
3.  **Be The Trusted Authority** — not just another salesperson
    
4.  **Keep the focus on them** — use neutral language, avoid pressure-triggering words
    
5.  **Let them sell themselves** — your questions guide them to their own conclusions
    
6.  **Commitment is a logical conclusion** — not a pressure tactic
    
7.  **Train daily** — mastery comes from repetition, not one-time exposure
    

---

---

## Chapter 10

### 1: Live Training with Marco Cortesi - Video 1

https://youtu.be/DHopuA3kkkQ

# Lecture Notes: Live Training with Marco Cortesi (Video 1) — Finding the Pain Point Through Problem Awareness

---

## The Core Challenge: "My Prospects Seem to Have No Problems"

When selling high-ticket offers (e.g., $30-60K Amazon business, coaching programs) to prospects with money, a common struggle is: **"How do I extract problems from people who seem to have no problems?"**

**The answer:** Everyone who gets on a call has a problem. Your job is to **make them aware of it**.

---

## The Sequence You Must Understand

The problem awareness section of the script follows a specific chain:

**Problem → Impact → Pain Point → Consequence**

Your job is to unveil a problem because unveiling a problem brings you to a **pain point**. Without reaching the pain point, the sale never moves forward.

---

## Step 1: Know What You Sell and What It Solves

Before you can find problems, you need clarity on what your product actually solves:

**Example — Amazon Business:**

-   Solves for: **Time, Money, Freedom** (time + money combined = freedom)
    

**So what problems might prospects have?**

-   Not enough time
    
-   Not enough money
    
-   Not enough freedom
    
-   Current investments aren't yielding enough
    
-   No retirement plan
    
-   No future financial plans
    
-   Want to diversify assets
    

**Key insight:** The problem awareness stage is **not fixed — it's variable** depending on what the prospect tells you they're looking for.

---

## Step 2: Master Situation Questions First

Before you can do problem awareness, you often need to **master situation questions** to understand where the prospect currently stands.

**Situation question examples:**

-   *"What are you currently doing now to generate passive income?"*
    
-   *"How long have you been doing it that way?"*
    
-   *"What other types of investments do you have right now?"*
    
-   *"What got you into \[crypto/property/etc.\]?"*
    
-   *"What's the rationale for putting money into Amazon rather than more into crypto?"*
    

**Example dialogue flow:**

> **You:** *"What kind of investments do you have right now?"* **Prospect:** *"Property and some crypto."* **You:** *"How long have you been doing that?"* **Prospect:** *"6 years."* **You:** *"What's the rationale for putting money into Amazon rather than more into crypto?"* **Prospect:** *"Diversifying my assets."*

Now you're ready to transition into problem awareness: **"What are you feeling that's making you want to do that?"**

---

## Step 3: Drive to the Pain Point

**The critical skill:** You must be mentally flexible and articulate enough to take what the prospect tells you and **drive it toward a tangible pain point** — where they say things like *"I'm frustrated"* or *"I'm worried about getting fired."*

**The mistake most salespeople make:** They ask problem questions and **hope** the answer is good. They're not landing safely — they're not steering the conversation toward pain.

**Instead:** Add questions that **make them feel**, getting you closer to the pain point rather than just asking surface-level problem questions.

---

## Live Example: Selling Sales Training (Sales Structure Topic)

**Starting point:** Prospect mentions their sales structure is "okay."

**Situation questions:**

-   *"What current sales structure do you have in place?"*
    
-   *"How long have you been using it?"*
    
-   *"What caused you to use that sales structure? Where did you get it from?"*
    
-   *"Do you like the current sales structure you have in place?"*
    

**Driving to pain — the power questions:**

> *"How many deals do you close a week?"* → *"Zero at the moment."* **That's one question — straight to the pain point.**

Or:

> *"Out of 10 good leads, how many do you feel you can close?"* → *"I'd like to say two."* *"What's happening in the other eight?"* → *"My process... my sales structure."* **Pain point reached.**

---

## The Big Lesson: Listen and Be Mentally Flexible

**Most average salespeople don't listen.** They follow a script mechanically without processing what the prospect is actually telling them.

To be effective at problem awareness:

-   **Listen to what they're saying** — the clues to pain are in their words
    
-   **Be mentally articulate** — know how to take any topic and steer it toward a pain point
    
-   **Know your niche** — the better you know your industry, the easier it is to find the right questions
    
-   **Don't just hope for good answers** — design questions that **land** on pain points
    

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Everyone on a call has a problem** — your job is to make them aware of it
    
2.  **Problem → Impact → Pain Point → Consequence** — this is the chain you must follow
    
3.  **The problem awareness stage is variable** — it changes based on what the prospect is looking for
    
4.  **Master situation questions first** — you need context before you can find problems
    
5.  **Drive to tangible pain points** — don't ask and hope; steer the conversation intentionally
    
6.  **One powerful question can get you there** — "How many deals do you close a week?" → "Zero" = instant pain point
    
7.  **Listen** — the biggest differentiator between average and top salespeople
    
8.  **Mental flexibility is everything** — you must be able to adapt your questions to whatever the prospect tells you
    

---

---

### 2: Live Training with Marco Cortesi - Video 2

https://youtu.be/B7OEYcC9cTc

# Lecture Notes: Live Training with Marco Cortesi (Video 2) — Live Role-Play Breakdown: Finding Pain Points and Handling Objections

---

## Live Demonstration: Selling a Car to an Unprepared Prospect

Marco demonstrates the entire sales process live with "Ambush" (Bush), a videographer with **zero sales training** who has never been on a sales call. This proves the methodology works on real, unrehearsed people.

---

### The Mental Mapping Process

When the prospect says something, **mentally map it immediately**. When Bush mentions "sound" as his reason for wanting a new car, Marco's brain maps it in three words and starts building the path to a pain point.

> **Bush:** *"My car doesn't sound good — it's a low, squeaky noise instead of a loud, deep sound."*

**Marco's internal process:**

-   He hears "sound" but needs to **differentiate** — is it about the car not functioning well, or about how the car makes him **feel**?
    
-   Answer: Bush wants to **feel good** about his car. The car functions fine.
    

> **Marco:** *"What is it about the sound you don't like?"* **Bush:** *"It reminds me of a different car I used to drive."* **Marco:** *"Hold on — bad memories or good memories?"* **Bush:** *"Bad memories."* **Marco:** *"Has that had an impact on you?"* **Bush:** *"Not an impact, but judgment — negative emotional impact."*

**Result:** Started from "sound" → ended at **"negative emotional impact."** Marco knew where he was going the entire time.

---

### Using the Pain Point for the Rest of the Sale

Once you have the pain point, it becomes your **leverage for the entire conversation** — including objection handling.

> **Marco:** *"You've been driving this car every day. How many hours do you spend in it?"* **Bush:** *"About 1 hour each way. More on weekends."* **Marco:** *"How much do you value your time?"* **Bush:** *"I value it, but not enough."* **Marco:** *"The problem is you're spending 2 hours a day in something you don't want. You don't appreciate yourself. Would you be open to doing something to make yourself appreciated?"*

**Bush is sold.** The exhaust fan option he originally wanted is now irrelevant — who cares about modifying the old car when the real issue is how he feels every day?

---

### Handling the Money Objection — Live

> **Bush:** *"I don't have the money."* **Marco:** *"Money aside, would you do it though?"* **Bush:** *"Oh yeah."* **Marco:** *"Why?"* **Bush:** *"To feel better."*

**Marco's reframe:**

> *"How much good feeling have you lost by driving a car every day that you don't like?"*

Then collaboratively work through the numbers:

-   Current car value: $35K trade-in
    
-   Cash available: $18K
    
-   Remaining: ~$30K needed
    
-   **"Which other ways do we have to find that $30K?"** (Notice: "we" — working together)
    

Bush suggests shares and property. Marco ties it back to the pain:

> *"Are you willing to sell your shares so you can fix the problem with the squeaky car that doesn't make you feel good? Your shares — are they going to fix your daily problems?"*

**Bush:** *"No."* **Marco:** *"So where would you like to go from here?"*

Then the final reframe when Bush hesitates about using his savings for the future:

> *"You're worried about the future, brother, but you don't respect yourself in the present."*

**Bush:** *"That makes sense."* — **Sold.**

---

## Jake's Practice Round: Amazon Business Sales Call

Jake (Marco's trainee) practices the same methodology selling Amazon automation. Key observations:

### What Jake Did Right:

-   Set the frame early: *"The first part of this call is for us to understand your background..."*
    
-   Qualified honestly: *"There are some people we just can't help"*
    
-   Good situation questions: *"What do you do for a living?" "How long?" "Do you like what you do?"*
    
-   Used a probing question: *"Why is getting out of cleaning so important to you now though?"*
    

### What Jake Needs to Improve:

**The #1 Issue: Tonality**

Marco's feedback is blunt: Jake's tonality is like **"cardboard"** — rigid, schematic, stiff. He's concentrating so hard on what to say next that he can't be creative or present in the conversation.

**The difference:**

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Jake's Approach</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Marco's Approach</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Asking questions to <strong>get answers</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Asking questions to <strong>get to know the person</strong></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Schematic, structured, robotic</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Conversational, like talking to a friend</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Focused on the script</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Mentally flexible, knows where to go</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Creates sales resistance</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Creates connection and openness</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

**Bush (the prospect) confirmed it:** *"When he said he wants to get to know me more, that actually felt good."*

---

## The Mindset Shift: Money vs. Helping

Jake asks: *"How do you shift from thinking about money when you haven't made much?"*

**Marco's answer:**

-   If thinking about money hasn't produced money, **change the equation**
    
-   Product interest vs. helping people — **totally different energy**
    
-   When you focus on helping: you're chill, no stress, no pressure
    
-   Marco has been selling since age 15 and made $1M+ — *"Even if I mess up, who cares?"*
    
-   The problem on sales calls: people just want to make sure you're not selling them
    

**The shift:** Stop going in trying to sell. Go in trying to understand what's going on with this person.

---

## Key Takeaways

1.  **Mentally map in real-time** — when they give you a word, build the path to pain immediately
    
2.  **Differentiate the real issue** — is it functional or emotional? It's almost always emotional
    
3.  **One pain point fuels the entire sale** — including objection handling
    
4.  **"Money aside, would you do it?"** — isolates whether it's a value problem or a funding problem
    
5.  **Work the numbers together** — "Which other ways do WE have to find that money?"
    
6.  **Tie funding back to pain** — "Are your shares going to fix your daily problems?"
    
7.  **"You worry about the future but don't respect yourself in the present"** — a devastating reframe
    
8.  **Tonality is mastery** — if you sound like a script reader, nothing else matters
    
9.  **Ask questions to get to know them, not to get answers** — the prospect can feel the difference
    
10.  **Shift from selling to helping** — the energy changes everything
     

---

# Cold Calling: Getting Through the Gatekeeper to the Prospect

---

## Overview

Cold calling doesn't have to be stressful — it only takes **preparation**. The goal is never to pitch. The goal is to **open a two-way conversation** and create enough curiosity that the prospect wants to engage.

---

## Core Mindset Shift

Before anything else, ask yourself two questions:

1.  **What words or behaviors make me look like a stereotypical salesperson?** → Eliminate those.
    
2.  **What problems does my prospect go to bed thinking about?** → Lead with those.
    

---

## The 3 Steps

### Step 1 — Understand How You're Coming Across

**Exercise: Self-Audit Your Delivery**

-   Practice your opening in front of a mirror, multiple times a day
    
-   Record yourself on a call → listen for tone, delivery, and language
    
-   Record yourself talking to a friend or family member → compare the two
    

**Ask yourself:**

-   Do I sound scripted or natural?
    
-   Am I talking too fast?
    
-   Do I sound nervous or overly excited?
    
-   Would I want to keep talking to this version of me?
    

The goal: sound the same whether you're talking to a prospect or a friend.

---

### Step 2 — Prepare Your Problem Statement

**Exercise: Build Your Problem Statement**

1.  List the **top 3 problems** your solution solves for prospects
    
2.  Identify which one your prospects relate to **most** — the one they're thinking about when they wake up in the morning
    
3.  Craft a short statement around that problem and how you solve it
    

> This is what you use to fill in the blanks of your cold call opening.

---

### Step 3 — Make the Call

**Your agenda on every call:**

-   Open a two-way conversation
    
-   Create curiosity so they *want* to engage
    
-   Avoid all sales pressure
    
-   Discover their problems, the root cause, and how it's affecting them
    

---

## The 4-Part Call Opening Framework

### Part 1 — The Opener

Use a disarming, low-pressure opener:

> *"Hi, is John there? … Hey John, this is just Alex — I was wondering if you could possibly help me out for a moment?"*

> *"Hi, this is just Alex — we haven't met yet. I was wondering if you could possibly help me out for a moment?"*

**Key word: "just"** — it signals you're not a threat. You're asking for help, not pitching.

---

### Part 2 — Wait for Their Response

They will almost always say one of:

-   *"Sure, how can I help you?"*
    
-   *"Who is this?"*
    
-   *"What's this about?"*
    

Wait for it. Don't rush past it.

---

### Part 3 — The Problem-Based Opening Statement

Reply with one of these structures:

> *"Well, I'm not quite sure I could yet… I called to see if your company would be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in your \[blank\] that could be causing you to \[blank\]."*

> *"I called to see if you'd be open to looking at a different perspective on \[blank\]."*

> *"I'm just calling to see if you'd be open to some different ideas around \[blank\]."*

> *"I called to see if your company is grappling with issues around \[blank\]."*

**Key phrase: "I'm not quite sure I could yet"** — this removes pressure and positions you as someone checking fit, not pushing a sale.

---

### Part 4 — Connecting Phrases

Use these to bridge your opener to the specific problem:

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Connecting Phrase</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Example Use</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>…that could be causing you to…</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>hidden gaps in your pipeline <strong>that could be causing you to</strong> lose deals</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>…that might be affecting…</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>issues <strong>that might be affecting</strong> your team's retention</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>…that are preventing you from…</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>gaps <strong>that are preventing you from</strong> scaling</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>…to eliminate…</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>ideas <strong>to eliminate</strong> recurring compliance issues</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>…related to / regarding…</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>questions <strong>regarding</strong> your current coverage</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>…for improving…</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>different perspectives <strong>for improving</strong> your cash flow</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>…surrounding…</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>challenges <strong>surrounding</strong> your protection strategy</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## What Happens Next

After your opening, the prospect will likely say:

-   *"Who is this again?"*
    
-   *"What do you mean by hidden gaps?"*
    
-   *"What's this about?"*
    

**This is exactly what you want.** That response = curiosity = they're now engaged instead of hanging up.

→ This is your cue to deliver your **Personalized Introduction** — how what you do has helped others solve those same problems. *(Covered in a separate module.)*

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Purpose = conversation, not pitch</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Pitching triggers rejection; curiosity triggers engagement</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Tone &gt; Script</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Prospects pick up on nervousness and scripted delivery immediately</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Lead with their problem</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>People engage when they feel understood, not when they feel sold to</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"I'm not sure I could yet"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Removes pressure; signals you're qualifying them, not chasing them</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>No pressure, ever</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The moment you push, you lose them</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Record yourself doing a mock cold call and a casual conversation — compare both
    
-   \[ \] Write out your top 3 prospect problems
    
-   \[ \] Identify which problem your prospects feel most urgently
    
-   \[ \] Draft your problem statement using the connecting phrase framework
    
-   \[ \] Practice the 4-part opening until it sounds completely natural

---

### 02 Cold Calling Examples

https://youtu.be/SI4B5GtGgmY

# Cold Calling Examples: Industry Role-Plays

---

## Overview

This module walks through live cold call examples across different industries so you can see exactly how the framework applies — and adapt it to your own product or service. The structure is always the same. Only the **problem statement** changes.

---

## Why the Opening Works (Quick Recap)

**"Just \[Name\]"** — the word *just* implies familiarity, as if they should already know you. It lowers the guard.

**"I was wondering if you could possibly help me out for a moment"** — human nature almost automatically triggers *"Sure, how can I help?"* It's a conversation starter, not a sales opener.

**"I'm not quite sure you could yet"** — signals detachment. You're not desperate. You're not even sure if this is the right fit. That tone alone changes the entire dynamic.

---

## Full Role-Play Examples by Industry

---

### Example 1 — Accounts Payable / Finance Services

> *"Hey Amy, this is just Bart Red — I was wondering if you could possibly help me out for a moment."* *"Sure, how can I help you?"* *"Well, I'm not quite sure you could yet… I called your office to see if your company would be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in your accounts payables that could be causing you to lose unnecessary time and money."*

---

### Example 2 — Vendor Management / Cost Reduction Services

> *"Hey Barbara, this is just Chris Delaney — I was wondering if you could possibly help me out for a moment."* *"Sure Chris, how can I help?"* *"Well, I'm not quite sure you could yet… I called to see if your company would be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps with some of your vendors that could be causing you to overpay each month."*

**Trainer note:** Notice the slow, relaxed delivery. You're not rushing. You're asking if they'd be *open to looking* — not asking them to buy anything.

---

### Example 3 — Technology / Data Analytics (Gatekeeper Scenario)

> *"Hey Joanne, this is just Zachary Taylor — I was wondering if you could possibly help me out for a moment."* *"Sure Zack, what can I do for you?"* *"I'm looking for Bob Freely — is he available?"* *"He's pretty busy. What's this about?"* *"I apologize, I didn't mean to offend you Joanne… the reason I called was to see if Bob would be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in your claims adjustment process that could be causing your company to lose customers each month — or lose money each month."*

**Key takeaway:** When the gatekeeper pushes back, don't panic. Apologize, stay calm, and redirect with your problem statement. The curiosity it creates often gets you transferred.

---

### Example 4 — Pharmaceuticals / Healthcare (In-Person / Walk-In)

> *"Hey Amy — I'm just Brett Simpson. I was wondering if you could possibly help me out for a moment."* *"Sure Brett, how can I help?"* *"I'm not quite sure you could yet… I stopped by the office to see if your practice would be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in your flu and strep testing that could be causing you to lose some of your patients each month."*

**Key takeaway:** The framework works in person too. Spot the name tag, use their name, stay relaxed, act slightly confused — same energy as the phone.

---

### Example 5 — Legal Services

> *"Hey John, this is just Marian Monroe — I was wondering if you could possibly help me out for a moment."* *"Sure — I don't think I know you. How can I help?"* *"I apologize — I'm not quite sure you could yet… I called to see if you'd be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in your contract review process that could be possibly putting the company at more risk."*

**Key takeaway:** Use neutral, non-accusatory language. Don't say *"your company IS at risk"* — say *"possibly putting the company at more risk."* Assuming the worst triggers defensiveness, especially with Type A personalities.

---

### Example 6 — Employee Benefits / Health Insurance

> *"Hi, this is just Alison — John, I was wondering if you could possibly help me out for a moment."* *"Sure Alison, what's this about?"* *"Well, I'm not quite sure you could yet… I called to see if your company would be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in how you're providing health insurance for some of your employees that could be causing you to overpay every month."*

**When they ask "who is this again?":**

> *"Oh, I apologize — I didn't mean to offend you. What I do is, you know how a lot of companies… \[→ Personalized Introduction\]"*

**Key takeaway:** If they ask who you are or what this is about mid-opener, don't panic. Simply apologize and bridge straight into your Personalized Introduction.

---

### Example 7 — Lead Generation / Advertising / Marketing Services

> *"Hey John, this is just Andy Simpson — I was wondering if you could possibly help me out for a moment."* *"Sure Andy, how can I help?"* *"I'm not quite sure you could yet… I called to see if your company would be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in your lead generation process that might be causing you to lose potential jobs every week."*

Best suited for: contractors, plumbers, electricians, service businesses that depend on leads to book work.

---

### Example 8 — Fundraising Services (In-Person / Walk-In)

> *"Hey — is John in? Oh, you're John! I'm just Scott Lerro — I was wondering if you could possibly help me out for a moment."* *"Sure Scott, how can I help?"* *"I'm not quite sure you could yet… I came by your school to see if you guys would be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in your fundraising that could be causing the school to lose money on some of their activities."*

---

### Example 9 — Financial Services (High Net Worth / Pension Funds)

**Version A — Pension Fund:**

> *"Hey Sally, this is John Smith — I was wondering if you could possibly help me out for a moment."* *"Sure John, what's going on?"* *"I'm not quite sure you could yet… I called to see if you'd be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in your pension fund that could be putting some of your capital at risk."*

**Version B — Investment Portfolio:**

> *"I'm not quite sure you could yet… I was calling to see if you'd be open to looking at any possible hidden gaps in your portfolio that could be causing some of your capital to be at risk."*

**Key takeaway:** Both versions create the same reaction — *"What do you mean at risk? Who are you?"* — which is exactly the curiosity you want before bridging to your Personalized Introduction.

---

## The Universal Bridge (When They Ask "Who Is This?")

No matter which industry or version you use, when they push back with *"What's this about?"* or *"Who are you again?"*, always bridge the same way:

> *"Oh, I apologize — I didn't mean to offend you. What I do is, you know how a lot of \[companies / practices / schools / investors\]… \[→ Personalized Introduction\]"*

This phrase:

-   Disarms without collapsing
    
-   Acknowledges without over-explaining
    
-   Moves straight into your value story
    

---

## Formula Summary

Pace yourself like you're talking to a friend</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Neutral language</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"Could be causing" not "is causing" — you're not accusing, you're exploring</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Curiosity is the goal</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>If they ask <em>"what do you mean?"</em> — you've won the opener</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>The apology bridge</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>When challenged, apologize calmly and pivot immediately into your Personalized Intro</p></td></tr></tbody></table>
