### 03 Time Management

https://youtu.be/zu621CspvKY

# Time Management: Prospect Time Blocking (PTB)

---

## Overview

Time management is one of the most overlooked skills in sales — yet it's one of the clearest separators between top performers and average ones. This module introduces **Prospect Time Blocking (PTB)**: a structured approach to protecting and maximizing your selling hours every day.

---

## The Core Idea

You have 24 hours in a day. Only a handful of those are actual **selling hours**. How you manage those hours determines whether you're average or at the top of your organization.

> **Top salespeople organize their day into distinct time blocks for specific activities — and they eliminate distractions during those blocks.**

---

## The Two Types of Time

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 75px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Time Type</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Label</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Definition</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Prospect Time Blocking</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>PTB</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Hours dedicated <em>exclusively</em> to prospecting</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Non-Sales Activities</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>NSA</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Everything else — admin, CRM, emails, meetings</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

The biggest mistake salespeople make: **letting NSA bleed into PTB.**

---

## Why NSA Kills Your Pipeline

Here's what will try to steal your prospecting time:

1.  **Sales managers** who want to talk all day
    
2.  **Co-workers** who want coffee, gossip, or to show you YouTube videos
    
3.  **HR/Marketing** sending requests for reports, data entry, or other admin tasks
    
4.  **Your own habits** — checking email, scrolling social media, responding to texts
    

This is called **busy work** — and it becomes an excuse for not prospecting.

> *"You get paid to sell. Nothing else. I've never seen a sales rep fired for bad data entry — but I've seen plenty fired for not selling enough."*

---

## How to Structure Your PTB

**Target: 3 hours of pure prospecting per day**, broken into blocks:

-   1 hour in the morning
    
-   1 hour before lunch
    
-   1 hour in the late afternoon
    

**During PTB, you do ONE thing only: prospect.**

### What you do NOT do during PTB:

-   Check social media
    
-   Do follow-ups
    
-   Attend meetings
    
-   Write proposals
    
-   Check email or texts
    
-   Update your CRM *(take notes, log in the CRM after PTB ends)*
    

This still leaves **5–6 hours** in your day for:

-   Crafting proposals
    
-   Closing sales
    
-   Calling back older leads
    
-   CRM updates
    
-   Meetings and admin
    

---

## The 3 Biggest Distractions (Kill These First)

1.  **Social media**
    
2.  **Email**
    
3.  **Text messages**
    

Every time you hear a notification ping, your focus is broken. 15 minutes later you've scrolled through a Facebook feed and forgotten what you were doing.

### Practical rules:

-   Turn off email notifications during PTB
    
-   Silence your phone — put it where you can't see it
    
-   Log out of all social media on your computer
    

### Recommended email schedule:

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Time</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Action</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>20 min before work starts</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Clear emails before the day begins</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Just before lunch (~3 hrs later)</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Check again</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Before leaving the office</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Final check</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Optional: before bed</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>One more if needed</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

> Outside of those windows — don't look at it.

---

## Revenue Generating Activities (RGA) vs. NSA

During your **golden hours**, only focus on RGAs.

### Revenue Generating Activities (RGA):

-   Calling leads
    
-   Getting back to prospects to close
    
-   Running demos
    
-   Going over proposals with a prospect
    

### Non-Sales Activities (NSA) — important, but always secondary:

-   Preparing proposals and presentations
    
-   Researching prospects
    
-   Admin and reporting
    
-   CRM management
    
-   Planning your week
    
-   Contracts
    
-   Emailing
    

> NSA must be accomplished — but **never at the expense of selling time.**

---

## The PTB Mindset

Treat your prospecting blocks the way you'd treat the most important meeting on your calendar — one you would never miss.

-   Schedule PTB into your calendar like an appointment
    
-   Put up a "do not disturb" signal if you need to
    
-   Say **no** to co-workers, managers, and distractions during those hours
    
-   No multitasking (e.g., emailing while calling) — it splits your focus and makes you less effective on the phone
    

> *"Professional salespeople protect their prospecting time like it's their life — because it actually is. It's their lifeblood."*

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>PTB = sacred hours</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Nothing moves your income like consistent prospecting time</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>NSA is not your job</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You're paid to sell — admin is secondary</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Distractions compound</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>One ping = 15 min lost. Multiply that by 10 a day</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Single-tasking wins</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Multitasking during PTB splits focus and slows you down</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Discipline = results</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Same hours as everyone else, but 2x the output</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Map out your current daily schedule — identify how many true selling hours you actually have
    
-   \[ \] Design your 3-hour PTB structure (morning / pre-lunch / afternoon blocks)
    
-   \[ \] Block those hours in your calendar today, recurring daily
    
-   \[ \] Identify your top 3 NSA time-wasters and set rules to contain them
    
-   \[ \] Set up your email schedule (3x per day max) and stick to it for one week
    
-   \[ \] Track your prospecting calls within PTB for 5 days and compare output vs. unstructured days

### 04 Delegation

https://youtu.be/FRXpTaK5WwU

# Delegation: Your Key to Time Management

---

## Overview

Delegation is not a luxury — it's a **requirement** for reaching the top in sales. If you're spending your day on admin, reports, and busy work, you're not selling. And if you're not selling, you're not making money.

---

## The Core Idea

Your ability to delegate **Non-Sales Activities (NSA)** to salaried or hourly staff is what frees you up to focus exclusively on **Revenue Generating Activities (RGA)**.

> *"Without learning how to delegate, you'll always end up bogged down in non-sales activities — and you'll never reach your full potential as a sales professional."*

---

## What to Delegate

Hand off anything that doesn't directly move a sale forward:

-   CRM management and data entry
    
-   Reports and admin tasks
    
-   Preparing slides and presentations
    
-   Drafting and preparing agreements
    
-   Prospect research and information gathering
    
-   Decision-making on routine operational problems
    

These are tasks that **consume your selling time** but can be handled by someone on a salary or hourly wage.

---

## How to Delegate Effectively

It doesn't have to be complicated. Two things are all you need:

1.  **A clear deadline** — when does this need to be done?
    
2.  **A performance standard** — what does "done well" look like?
    

That's it. Set the expectation, hand it off, and get back to selling.

---

## Why This Matters

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Without Delegation</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>With Delegation</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Hours lost to admin and busy work</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Hours freed up for pure selling</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Constantly reactive and distracted</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Focused on prospects and their problems</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Stuck at average performance</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Free to reach full earning potential</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Working hard on the wrong things</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Working smart on what actually pays</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>You only get paid to sell</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Every hour on NSA is an hour not earning</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Delegation is a learnable skill</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>It's not about rank — it's about habit and clarity</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Busy work feels productive</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>But it's the #1 reason salespeople stay average</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Free your time = grow your income</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>More selling hours = more pipeline = more results</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] List every task you did this week — mark each one as RGA or NSA
    
-   \[ \] Identify which NSA tasks can be handed off to someone else in your team or office
    
-   \[ \] For each delegated task, define the deadline and the performance standard
    
-   \[ \] Track how many extra selling hours you recover in the first week of delegating

### 05 Procrastination

https://youtu.be/Ob9sD3jmyIo

# Procrastination: How to Control It in Sales

---

## Overview

Everyone procrastinates. The question isn't *whether* you procrastinate — it's **what** you procrastinate on. Top producers make a deliberate choice about which tasks get delayed and which get done immediately.

---

## The Core Distinction

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Performer Level</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>What They Procrastinate On</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Top Producers</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Low-value tasks — NSA, admin, busy work</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Average / Bottom Producers</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>High-value tasks — prospecting, closing, revenue-generating activities</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

> The habit is the same. The choice is different. That choice determines your income.

---

## The Rule

**Never procrastinate on high-value, Revenue Generating Activities (RGA).**

When you look at your daily task list, always ask: *which of these items leads to a sale?* Do those first. Everything else can wait.

---

## Practical Application

1.  Start each day by reviewing your task list
    
2.  Identify which tasks are **RGA** (prospecting, demos, follow-ups to close)
    
3.  Do those **first** — before anything else
    
4.  Everything else (reports, CRM, emails, admin) gets done after your selling work is done
    

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Procrastination is a choice</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You decide what gets delayed — make it the right things</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Busy work feels safer</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Admin is comfortable; prospecting is uncomfortable — that's why people avoid it</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>RGA always comes first</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The order of your tasks determines the outcome of your day</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Recommended Resource

**Brian Tracy** — books on time management and productivity. Highly recommended for going deeper on this subject.

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Write out tomorrow's task list tonight
    
-   \[ \] Highlight every RGA item on the list
    
-   \[ \] Commit to completing all RGA tasks before touching any NSA
    
-   \[ \] At the end of the week, review: did you protect your high-value time?

### 06 How To Get It Done

https://youtu.be/vB642sOOqBE

# How to Get It Done: Programming Your Mind for Execution

---

## Overview

Knowing what to do isn't enough — you have to train your mind to actually do it. This module covers two practical techniques: a mental reprogramming phrase to kill "someday" thinking, and a task-breakdown method for tackling large projects without getting overwhelmed.

---

## Part 1 — Kill "One Day" Thinking

The phrase *"one day I'm going to do this"* or *"someday I'll get to that"* is the path to nowhere. It's a delay disguised as intention.

**Replace it with:**

> *"I'm going to get this done NOW."* *"I will get this done NOW."*

Repeat it to yourself **10–20 times**. This isn't just motivation — it's unconsciously programming your mind to stay on course and complete the task before moving on to other things.

> The word *now* is the trigger. It removes the escape hatch that "someday" gives you.

---

## Part 2 — How to Finish Large Tasks

Large tasks feel overwhelming because we treat them as one giant thing. They're not — they're a series of small steps.

### The Method:

1.  Take a notepad or tablet and **write down every small part** of the task or project
    
2.  Put them **in the correct order** — from the very first step to completion
    
3.  **Start with #1** and do nothing else until it's done
    
4.  **Cross it off** and move to #2
    
5.  Repeat until the project is complete
    

### Why it works:

Finishing step #1 creates momentum. It becomes significantly easier to start step #2, then #3, and so on. You don't need motivation for the whole project — just enough to start the first step.

> *"Just start working on the first part. You'll find it becomes much easier to complete the second, then the third, then the fourth — until the whole project is done and on time."*

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Someday" = never</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Vague intention creates zero action — replace it with "now"</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Repetition programs the mind</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Saying "I will do this now" 10–20x keeps you locked on the task</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Break it down</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>No large task is too big when it's a numbered list of small steps</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Start with #1</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The first completed step creates the momentum for everything that follows</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Cross it off</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Visible progress reinforces the habit of finishing</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] The next time you catch yourself saying "one day" or "someday" — stop, and replace it with "I will get this done now" repeated 10–20 times
    
-   \[ \] Pick one large task or project you've been avoiding — break it into every small step in order
    
-   \[ \] Start step #1 today, even if you only have 15 minutes
    
-   \[ \] Use a physical notepad or list app and cross off each step as you complete it

# Sense of Urgency: The Edge That Separates Top Performers

---

## Overview

Sense of urgency isn't about rushing — it's about consistently doing what others aren't willing to do. In sales, that one habit compounds into a career-defining advantage.

---

## The Core Idea

> *"I simply out-learned other salespeople in my industry — and I continue to do that even now. That's the secret."*

To be the best in sales, you have to do two things better than everyone else:

1.  **Out-learn your competition** — constantly
    
2.  **Manage your time more effectively** than they do
    

That's it. Not talent. Not luck. Out-learning and out-executing.

---

## What Sense of Urgency Looks Like in Practice

-   Start your **Revenue Generating Activities first** — every single day
    
-   Complete them **quickly and without delay**
    
-   Build a reputation for **getting things done and taking action fast**
    
-   Never wait for the "right time" — move now
    

---

## The Compounding Effect

Once you develop a reputation for speed and follow-through, opportunities start finding you. Your career accelerates. You create choices for yourself that average performers never have access to.

> *"You'll be surprised at the opportunities you'll have for the rest of your life — and you will fast-track your sales career extremely quickly."*

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Do what others won't</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>That gap is where top performers live</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Out-learn constantly</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Knowledge compounds — the more you learn, the wider your edge</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Speed builds reputation</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>People notice who executes fast and trust them with more</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>RGA first, always</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Urgency applied to the wrong tasks is just busy work done faster</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Ask yourself honestly: am I out-learning my peers right now?
    
-   \[ \] Identify one area — product knowledge, sales skills, time management — where you can accelerate your learning this week
    
-   \[ \] Start tomorrow's RGA tasks the moment your day begins — no warm-up, no delay
    
-   \[ \] Commit to building a reputation on your team for being the one who always executes

### 08 Interruptions

https://youtu.be/H3qzaTLdGdI

# Controlling Interruptions: Protecting Your Selling Time

---

## Overview

Interruptions are one of the biggest reasons companies consistently miss their sales targets. It's not strategy or product — it's that salespeople simply aren't selling enough of their available time because they're constantly being pulled away from it.

---

## The Scale of the Problem

> Studies report that as much as **53% of time in a typical office** is spent in idle chitchat with co-workers.

Over half the working day — gone. Look around your office right now. How many people are actually working vs. walking around talking?

The fix isn't complicated. As Brian Tracy puts it:

> *"When you're at work — work."*

When you arrive, open your to-do list (written the night before) and start immediately on your most important task. Not Facebook. Not the football recap. Not browsing. Work.

---

## The Three Types of Interruptions (and How to Handle Each)

---

### 1 — The Co-worker Who Walks In

**What to say:**

> *"Hey John — I'd really love to talk with you. However, I'm not available until later today, around 4pm, because I've got to complete this task by then. Just give me a little bit of time and I'll come over to you then, I promise."*

**Why it works:** You're not rejecting them — you're giving them a specific time. They won't be offended, and you protect your prospecting block.

---

### 2 — The Non-Revenue Phone Call

**What to say:**

> *"Hey, nice to hear from you! Can I help you with something real quick? I've got to jump into a meeting soon."*

Get to the point. Keep it short. Don't let casual calls drag into 20-minute conversations during PTB hours.

---

### 3 — The Unscheduled Visitor / Drop-In

**What to say:**

> *"Hey John — so glad you came in! I've got a meeting here in just a little bit, so I can probably spend about 20 minutes with you. I'm sure we can cover everything we need to by then."*

**Why it works:** The moment you set a time limit, the other person knows to get to the point. You're not being rude — you're being efficient. They'll respect it.

---

### 4 — Scheduled Meetings

-   Prepare a **written agenda** before every meeting
    
-   Order agenda items from **highest priority to lowest**
    
-   Follow the agenda strictly to stay on time
    
-   *(More on how to run effective meetings covered in a later module)*
    

---

## The Digital Interruptions (Quick Recap)

Already covered in PTB and Delegation modules — but worth reinforcing:

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Distraction</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Rule</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Email notifications</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Turn off during PTB</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Text messages</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Silence your phone; put it out of sight</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Social media</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Log out on your computer during work hours</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Messaging apps (Skype, etc.)</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Set to Do Not Disturb during PTB blocks</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Key Takeaways

### 09 Proposals

https://youtu.be/XWRzgHIWqjM

# Proposals: How to Build and Present Them to Close More Sales

---

## Overview

Proposals are primarily a B2B tool for complex selling environments. If you're in a one- or two-call close situation (cars, insurance, door-to-door), you likely won't need one. But if you do use proposals, how you build and present them will directly determine how many deals you close.

---

## Rule #1 — Never Send a Proposal Without Discovery First

This is non-negotiable. No exceptions.

Before you write a single word of a proposal, you must know:

1.  **What their problems are** — specifically, in their own words
    
2.  **Whether they have the budget or funding** to actually solve those problems
    

If a prospect asks for a quote or proposal upfront before you've even had a discovery conversation, respond with this:

> *"I'd be open to putting together a proposal for you. To be frank, I'm not quite sure I can even help you yet. Can I ask a few questions about what you're using now and your situation — to put something together that might actually be more useful? Would that be appropriate?"*

They'll say yes. Then you move into your discovery questions. This keeps you in control without being pushy, and it opens them up before you've committed to anything on paper.

---

## Rule #2 — Your Proposal Must Reflect What They Told You

A great proposal isn't a product brochure — it's a mirror of the conversation you already had.

**Every proposal should include:**

**Their Problems (2–3 max)** Restate the specific problems they told you about during discovery. This reminds them of the pain, brings it back to the surface, and shows them you were actually listening.

**Their Objectives (2–3 max)** Restate what they want to accomplish. This demonstrates that you understand not just the problem but where they want to go.

**The Value of Solving Those Problems** Restate the value of achieving their objectives through your solution. This is what frames the price — so that when you reveal your number, it feels small relative to what they're getting.

> **The 10x Rule:** The perceived value of solving the prospect's problem should be at least **10 times the cost of your solution**.
> 
> *Example: If a company is losing $15–20M a year in revenue, a $300K solution looks like a bargain — if you've positioned it correctly.*

---

## Rule #3 — Always Offer Three Options

Never send a one-option proposal. That's shooting yourself in the foot.

Three options work because:

-   You can't read the prospect's mind 100%, even after great discovery
    
-   Options make the decision easier — they're choosing *between your offers*, not between *you and someone else*
    
-   The premium option makes the middle option look like a great deal
    

**The Three-Option Structure:**

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 75px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Option</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Description</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Purpose</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Option 1</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Basic, lower-priced — still profitable</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Gives an entry point for budget-conscious prospects</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Option 2</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Mid-range — your core offering</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Where most prospects land; this is what you want to sell</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Option 3</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Premium, high-priced</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Makes Option 2 feel like a bargain; some prospects will choose it</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

> The power of the premium option isn't just that some will buy it — it's that it anchors perception and makes your core offering look extremely reasonable by comparison.

---

## Rule #4 — Make the Proposal Signable

Don't wait for a formal contract if you don't have to.

Build your proposal so it can double as a **basic signable agreement**. It doesn't need to be legally binding in full — just enough of a commitment to get the process started while the formal contract is being drawn up.

**Why this matters:** Every extra step between *"yes I'm interested"* and *"I've signed something"* is a chance for the deal to fall apart. The prospect can change their mind, go back to the status quo, or explore other options.

> *"The more steps you add to the sales process, the more chances there are of it falling apart. Keep it simple."*

Give the prospect the opportunity to authorize the proposal as the first step in doing business with you — and you protect the deal while everything else catches up.

---

## Proposal Structure Summary

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Section</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>What Goes Here</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Their Problems</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>2–3 problems they told you about in discovery</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Their Objectives</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>2–3 goals they want to achieve</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Value Statement</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The cost of <em>not</em> solving the problem vs. cost of your solution</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Three Options</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Basic / Core / Premium</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Signature Line</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Make it a signable agreement to get the ball rolling</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>No discovery = no proposal</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>A blind proposal is a waste of time and a credibility killer</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Proposals mirror the conversation</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>They show you listened — not just that you have a product to sell</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Value before price</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Always stack perceived value first so the price looks small</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>10x value rule</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Your solution should feel like a bargain relative to the problem's cost</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Three options always</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Anchors perception, simplifies the decision, increases average deal size</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Make it signable</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Reduce steps between interest and commitment</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

-   \[ \] Add a value statement section to your proposal that quantifies what solving the problem is worth
    
-   \[ \] Rebuild your pricing structure into three tiers if you haven't already

### 10 How To Present

https://youtu.be/yc0V929qkPw

# How to Present: By Not Presenting

---

## Overview

The biggest mistake salespeople make during presentations is trying to show everything. The counterintuitive truth is that **the less you present, the more you sell**. Your job isn't to showcase your solution — it's to show the prospect that you understand their problems and can solve them.

---

## Delivery Format: Face-to-Face or Zoom Whenever Possible

Avoid emailing proposals without a live walkthrough. Present face-to-face or over Zoom so you can:

-   Read body language and tone in real time
    
-   Answer concerns as they surface
    
-   Guide the conversation toward a close
    

> The more you can be *present* during the proposal review, the higher your close rate.

---

## Rule #1 — Present Around Their Problems, Not Your Product

Only present what is directly relevant to the challenges the prospect told you about during discovery. Nothing more.

**What most salespeople do wrong:**

-   50-page proposals
    
-   2-hour presentations
    
-   Feature and benefit dumps
    
-   Generic "we're the best" monologues
    

**Why it fails:** Prospects tune out. It goes in one ear and out the other — like a high school chemistry lecture nobody asked for.

**What to do instead:**

-   Customize every presentation to the specific issues they mentioned
    
-   Never do cookie-cutter presentations
    
-   Only cover what moves them closer to solving their problem
    

> *"Your prospect doesn't care about your features. They care about one thing: can you solve my problem?"*

---

## Rule #2 — Use Case Studies to Prove Your Solution Works

Case studies are one of the most powerful tools in a presentation. They show real results from real clients in similar situations.

**A strong case study includes:**

1.  The problem the client faced
    
2.  What your solution did to address it
    
3.  The measurable results — with real, quantifiable numbers
    

> Prospects buy **results**, not features. Show them what happened for someone else in their position.

---

## Rule #3 — Ask Checking for Agreement Questions Throughout

Most presentations are a one-way monologue. That's a disaster. Instead, turn your presentation into a **dialogue** by asking checking for agreement questions every paragraph or so.

**What these questions do:**

-   Keep the prospect engaged and part of the process
    
-   Give you a pulse on how the presentation is landing
    
-   Position you as a trusted authority, not another salesperson
    
-   Create micro-commitments that build toward a yes
    

**Target: 5–10 checking for agreement questions per presentation.**

### Checking for Agreement Question Bank

**General engagement:**

-   *"Does that make sense?"*
    
-   *"Are we on the same page?"*
    
-   *"What are your thoughts on that?"*
    
-   *"Are you with me on this?"*
    
-   *"Do you see how that works?"*
    
-   *"Do you see how that could help you, John?"*
    
-   *"Any questions on that?"*
    
-   *"Is there anything else I should add?"*
    

**Clarifying their priorities:**

-   *"John, what parts of the presentation would be most important to you — where should we spend more time?"*
    
-   *"John, what are you hoping to gain from the proposal today?"*
    
-   *"Amy, what are your thoughts on what we've outlined so far?"*
    
-   *"Shakir, in what ways does this capture what you're trying to accomplish?"*
    

**Uncovering concerns:**

-   *"Amy, what aspects of this possibly concern you?"* *(use when you sense hesitation in tone or body language)*
    
-   *"Sally, I sense you have some hesitation about what we covered — what's behind that?"*
    
-   *"Jane, what's holding you back here?"*
    

**Budget and decision-making:**

-   *"How will your funding to solve this problem be determined — just so I understand?"*
    
-   *"Frank, who besides you would be deciding on which firm to work with?"*
    

**Competitive positioning:**

-   *"John, just so I understand — if you had two vendors that were evenly matched in solving this problem, same price, same experience, how would you make your decision? And why?"*
    

**Final check before closing:**

-   *"Bob, is there anything else you'd like to go over before we finalize our approach?"*
    

> Always ask this last question — even after they've agreed to move forward. You'd be surprised how often one more concern surfaces at the end. You want to be there to handle it.

---

## The Presentation Stage Is Only 10–15% of the Sales Process

This is one of the most important calibrations in sales.

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Stage</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Approximate % of Sales Process</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Discovery / Engagement</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>~60–70%</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Presentation</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>~10–15%</strong></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Closing</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>remainder</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

Most salespeople spend 50% of the process presenting. That's backwards. The heavy lifting was done in discovery. The presentation is just feeding back what they already told you — through the lens of your solution.

> *"The amount of information the prospect needs to know will always be far less than what you think it is."*

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Present less, close more</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Overloading prospects with information kills deals</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Customize every time</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Generic presentations signal you weren't listening</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Case studies over features</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Real results from real clients are far more persuasive</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Dialogue, not monologue</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Checking for agreement questions keep the prospect engaged and moving forward</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Presentation is 10–15% of the process</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Discovery does the real work — presentation just confirms it</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Never sweep hesitation under the rug</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Surface concerns in the room while you can still address them</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Review your current presentation — cut anything that doesn't directly address a problem the prospect stated
    
-   \[ \] Build at least one case study with real numbers for your most common client situation
    
-   \[ \] Memorize 5–8 checking for agreement questions and practice inserting them naturally
    
-   \[ \] Time your next presentation — if you're talking more than you're listening, restructure it
    
-   \[ \] After your next proposal walkthrough, ask: *"Is there anything else you'd like to go over before we finalize our approach?"* — and see what comes up

### 11 Start A Sales Meeting

https://youtu.be/N7nRWGjedNo

# How to Start a Sales Meeting the Right Way

---

## Overview

The first 15–30 seconds of any sales meeting — especially with a boardroom full of decision-makers — sets the tone for everything that follows. Most salespeople blow this by launching straight into their pitch. The right move is the opposite: put the focus on them immediately.

---

## What Most Salespeople Do (And Why It Fails)

The typical pattern:

1.  Ask a few surface-level questions
    
2.  Launch into a pitch about features, benefits, and why they're the best
    

The problem: every salesperson says they're the best. Prospects have heard it a thousand times. It triggers defensiveness before you've even started.

---

## The Right Approach: Establish Yourself as the Trusted Authority

Don't pitch. Ask. The right opening questions do four things at once:

-   Take the focus off you and put it on them
    
-   Show you're there to help, not to sell
    
-   Surface what they actually care about
    
-   Get them to start persuading themselves
    

---

## Opening Question Bank

Use one or two of these to open any sales meeting:

**Understand what they want from the meeting:**

> *"John, from your perspective — what would be a valuable way for us to spend this time together today?"*

> *"Jane, what would you like to discuss today so we can focus on what you guys might be looking for?"*

> *"Can I ask what the important items are you'd like to cover today — just so we can see if we could help you?"*

**Understand why they're there:**

> *"Can I ask what prompted your company to have this meeting with us today?"*

> *"How did you reach the decision to possibly seek outside help — just so I understand?"*

> *"Jamie, what caused you to bring me in — just so I can see if we could possibly help you?"*

---

## Why These Questions Work

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>What the Question Does</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Shifts focus to them immediately</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Shows you're there for them, not your quota</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Surfaces their hot points</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Tells you exactly what to emphasize during the meeting</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Gets them talking first</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>They begin persuading themselves before you say a word</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Signals you're a trusted advisor</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Not another salesperson running a pitch</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Key Takeaways

-   Never open a meeting with a pitch — open with a question
    
-   The goal of the first 30 seconds is to make them feel heard, not sold to
    
-   One or two well-placed opening questions is enough — don't overload
    
-   Tone and body language matter as much as the words — stay calm, curious, and present
    

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Pick two opening questions from the list above and memorize them
    
-   \[ \] Practice them out loud until the delivery feels natural, not scripted
    
-   \[ \] Use one at the start of your next meeting and observe how the energy of the room shifts
    
-   \[ \] Note what hot points they surface — and build your presentation around those

### 12 Budgeting Questions

https://youtu.be/vbrmgUDuW4A

# Budgeting Questions: How to Uncover Funding in Complex Sales

---

## Overview

In B2B and complex selling environments — where your solution is high-ticket, requires multiple visits, and involves multiple decision makers — budgeting questions are essential. According to Forbes, the average company has **6.8 decision makers** involved in a purchase. If you don't understand how funding gets approved, you're winging it.

> **Key principle:** The more pain the prospect experiences, the more they will pay to solve it. Your skilled questions are what surface that pain — and those same questions open the door to the budget conversation.

---

## The Budgeting Question Bank

### Understanding Their Budget Process

> *"John, just so I understand — could you go over with me your budgetary process in the company?"*

Helps you understand how they allocate funding to solve problems — before you've named a price.

---

> *"Charity, just to see if I could help you — what budgetary parameters are you working with, just so I have a better understanding?"*

Positions you as the expert who needs to know if funding is even possible. If they can't get the funding together, nothing changes for them. Delivered with calm detachment — not desperation.

---

> *"Isaac, can you tell me how the funding for this project would be determined?"*

Reveals the internal funding process and flags whether there are other decision makers you haven't met yet who need to be brought on board.

---

### Identifying Other Decision Makers

> *"Carlos, who else besides you would be involved in approving the budget to solve this?"*

A far more effective way to surface other decision makers than asking directly *"who are the decision makers here?"* — which often gets a deflecting *"that's me"* response.

---

> *"Frank, who besides you would be deciding on which firm to work with?"*

Another angle to uncover influencers or approvers who haven't been mentioned yet — especially important in complex B2B deals.

---

### Uncovering Internal Obstacles

> *"Max, what obstacles would you possibly encounter when trying to get financial approval from the board to solve this problem?"*

This is one of the most important questions in the sequence. It helps you understand what your contact is going to face internally when they advocate for your solution — so you can help them make the case, instead of being blindsided later.

> Without asking this, you're hoping and praying. You're not in a position of strength.

---

### Tying Budget to Their Stated Goals

> *"Mr. Jones, what type of budget do you have to put into this so that you can \[plug in what they said they wanted\]?"*

Anchors the budget question directly to the outcome they already told you they want. It's not about your price — it's about what it takes to get them what they asked for.

---

### Challenging Whether the Budget Is Enough

> *"Do you feel the budget you've been given is sufficient to actually solve this problem?"* *(delivered with calm skepticism)*

A powerful question that prompts them to reconsider whether their current budget is adequate — and potentially go back to the board for more. This works because your earlier discovery questions helped them see the full scope of the problem themselves.

---

## Why These Questions Work

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Question Type</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>What It Does</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Budget process questions</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Reveals how decisions get funded internally</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Budget parameters questions</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Qualifies whether a deal is even possible</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Decision maker questions</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Surfaces hidden approvers before they derail the deal</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Obstacle questions</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Prepares your contact to advocate for you internally</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Sufficiency question</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Prompts them to consider increasing the budget</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Never assume one decision maker</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Average B2B deal has 6.8 people involved</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>More pain = more willingness to pay</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Your discovery questions create the urgency that justifies the budget</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Budget questions = qualification</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>If they can't fund a solution, nothing you do will close the deal</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Ask indirectly about decision makers</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Direct questions get deflected; indirect ones reveal the truth</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Calm detachment is your tone</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You're not chasing the budget — you're assessing whether you can help</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Memorize at least three budgeting questions from the list above
    
-   \[ \] Practice delivering the sufficiency question *("do you feel the budget you've been given is sufficient…")* with genuine skepticism in your tone — not accusation
    
-   \[ \] In your next discovery call, identify all potential decision makers before the proposal stage
    
-   \[ \] Map out the internal approval process for your last three prospects — did you know how the budget was being decided?

### 13 Qualifying Questions

https://youtu.be/Soo0_7YJlhE

# Qualifying Questions: Probing for Urgency and Commitment

---

## Overview

Qualifying questions aren't just about checking if someone can afford your solution — they're about understanding **how urgently the prospect needs to solve their problem**. If the need isn't important to them, they won't buy. Your questions are what reveal that urgency — and create it.

---

## The Core Principle

> The way a prospect responds to your questions tells you whether there's a genuine sense of urgency to change. No urgency = no sale, no matter how good your solution is.

**The expert's posture:** calm, skeptical, detached. A needy salesperson gets excited when prospects show interest. An expert challenges them — *"well, why now though?"*

---

## Part 1 — Importance and Urgency Questions

These questions probe how seriously the prospect wants to change their situation. Ask them naturally throughout the conversation — not as a checklist.

**Importance questions:**

> *"Jane, how important is this issue to you?"*

> *"How important is it for you to change your situation and start \[repeat back what they said they wanted\]?"*

> *"How important is it for you to do something about this?"* → They say "very important" → *"Well, why now though?"*

> *"Is it important for you to solve this problem?"* → They say yes → *"Well, why?"* *(skeptical tone)*

**Connecting their pain to the benefit:**

> *"It sounds like you're frustrated with \[repeat back what they said they don't like\] — so if you could get what you want, how important is it for you to actually have that?"*

**Visualizing the positive outcome:**

> *"If you could \[fill in the blank\], what would that do for you?"*

> *"Are you prepared to change your situation so that you can \[repeat back what they said they wanted\]?"*

---

## Part 2 — The Two Most Powerful Words in Qualifying

> **"Why now though?"**

Delivered with calm skepticism, these two words create massive urgency. They force the prospect to articulate *why* this problem needs to be solved now — not weeks or months down the road. Once they say it out loud, they've committed to the urgency themselves.

---

## Part 3 — Pre-Commitment Before Investing Your Time

This is critical. Don't waste travel time, demo preparation, or selling hours on prospects who aren't truly qualified to move forward. Before you commit to an in-person visit or demo, test their commitment first.

**Example scenario:** Prospect says *"Can you come to our Dallas office and do a demo for the owner?"*

**Your response:**

> *"Possibly. Let's suppose I do come out to your office for a day and you're able to pull the owner and the other decision makers together for me to do a demo in person — and let's say everyone finds that we can actually solve the problem you've talked to me about. What do you see happening next?"*

**Read their answer:**

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 75px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Their Response</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>What It Means</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Your Move</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"We'd do business with you for sure"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Genuinely qualified</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Book the trip — do the demo</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"We'd have to run it by the committee / corporate"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Too many uncontrolled obstacles</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Do NOT commit yet — remove obstacles first</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"We'd have to check if we have the budget"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Budget not confirmed</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Do NOT commit yet</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"We'd compare it to our current vendor"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Not ready to decide</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Do NOT commit yet</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"I'm not really sure what would happen"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Not a serious prospect</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Do NOT commit yet</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

> If the answer is anything other than a clear commitment to move forward — resolve those obstacles *before* you invest travel time and resources.

---

## Part 4 — The Qualifying Question Framework

Build qualifying questions using a two-part structure: **a hypothetical opener + a forward-looking closer.**

### Hypothetical Openers:

-   *"Ben, let's pretend that we could…"*
    
-   *"Amy, what if we could…"*
    
-   *"Sally, just suppose we could…"*
    
-   *"Frank, imagine for a moment…"*
    
-   *"Assuming that we're able to…"*
    

### Forward-Looking Closers:

-   *"…what do you feel would happen next?"*
    
-   *"…what do you see happening next at that point?"*
    
-   *"…where would we go from there?"*
    
-   *"…what would you want to happen at that point?"*
    
-   *"…what would you want to happen next?"*
    

**Combined example:**

> *"Sally, just suppose we could solve the problem you've described — what do you see happening next?"*

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Qualify for urgency, not just ability to pay</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>A prospect who can afford you but doesn't feel urgency won't buy</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Skepticism signals expertise</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Excitement signals desperation — stay calm and probe</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Why now though?" builds urgency</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Gets them to verbalize why this needs to happen now, not later</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Pre-commit before you travel</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Unqualified demos waste your most valuable resource: selling time</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Hypothetical openers remove pressure</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"Just suppose we could…" lets them answer without feeling committed yet</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Memorize at least three importance/urgency questions and practice with a colleague
    
-   \[ \] Add *"Why now though?"* to your next three prospect conversations and observe the response
    
-   \[ \] Before your next demo or in-person visit, use the *"let's suppose I come out…what do you see happening next?"* pre-commitment test
    
-   \[ \] Review your last three lost deals — was there a lack of urgency you could have probed earlier?
    
-   \[ \] Build your own qualifying question using one opener + one closer from the framework above

### 14 Questions - Vendor Relationships

https://youtu.be/awrDLB-cRZw

# Questions to Disrupt Vendor Relationships

---

## Overview

When a prospect is already happy with their current vendor, most salespeople give up or go straight into pitch mode — both of which fail. This module covers how to use NEPQ questions to open the door, surface dissatisfaction the prospect didn't even know they had, and position yourself as the expert without triggering defensiveness.

These questions are used primarily during the **Problem Awareness** and **Solution Awareness** stages of the engagement process.

---

## The Core Principle

> No one likes 100% of what they currently have. There is always something they would change — if they could. Your job is to find it.

The goal isn't to attack their current vendor. It's to ask questions that cause the prospect to discover the gaps themselves — so they feel the need to change, on their own terms.

---

## The Vendor Disruption Question Bank

### Opening the Door to Dissatisfaction

> *"Can I ask — how does your ideal situation compare to what you have now with the company you're currently using?"*

This is a gentle opener. It doesn't criticize the current vendor — it simply invites the prospect to describe the gap between where they are and where they want to be. Then probe what surfaces.

---

### Challenging Complacency with Future-Focused Questions

> *"So Amy — you've already reached these big numbers and milestones with your salespeople. Where do you want to go from here in terms of future improvements in their sales ability — so that your numbers companywide keep going up rather than just stagnating over time?"*

Use this when the prospect says things are going well. It acknowledges their success while pointing toward what's next — and opens the question of whether their current vendor can take them there.

---

### Revisiting Their Original Selection Criteria

> *"Barry, back when you chose to work with the company you're with now — what was your selection criteria, if I could ask?"*

Wait for their answer. Then follow up with:

> *"In what ways has that possibly changed as you look at your needs today though?"*

**Why it works:** Their needs have almost certainly evolved since they first signed with the vendor. This question helps them see that the criteria that made sense then may not fit what they need now — opening a door you didn't have to force.

---

### Probing What They'd Change

> *"Sally, if you could change one thing about who you're using now — what would that be, if anything?"* *(delivered skeptically)*

> *"Jane, in what ways could your company do better for you than what they're doing now though?"* *(skeptical tone)*

When they give you something they'd change — don't move on. Probe it:

> *"Can I ask — why would you change that though?"*

> *"Why is that important to you now though?"*

These follow-up probes pull out the emotion behind the dissatisfaction and build urgency to change now rather than later.

---

### Letting Them Know They May Be Making a Mistake — Without Offending Them

This is one of the most important questions in this entire module. Use it when a prospect is leaning toward a competitor.

> *"Tony, how would I be able to communicate with you that you might be making the wrong decision — without you getting upset with me?"*

**Why it works:** It disarms them completely. Instead of defending your solution or attacking theirs, you're asking for permission to share a concern. Almost every prospect will say *"go ahead, you're not offending me — what do you have in mind?"* — and now you've earned the floor as the trusted expert.

---

### The Two Truths Question

When the prospect insists everything is fine with their current setup:

> *"Amy, to me it sounds like things are going fairly well with what you're already using. I mean — is there anything you would change about it… if you could?"* *(pause after "if you could")*

**Industry example — life insurance:**

> *"Sally, to me it sounds like things are going fairly well with the policy you already have. Is there anything you would change about the financial protection you have for the family… if you could?"*

When they give you something they'd change — even small — immediately probe:

> *"Hold on though — why is that important to you now though?"*

---

### 15 Questions - Keep Customers

https://youtu.be/Sy0FPWz3sao

# Questions to Keep Your Customers Happy

---

## Overview

Keeping a client is just as important as winning one. These questions help you regularly monitor the health of your existing client relationships — so you can catch problems early, reinforce why they chose you, and eliminate buyers' remorse before it becomes churn.

> **The answers to these questions will tell you one of two things: you're in danger of losing them, or the relationship is strong.** Either way, you need to know.

---

## The Tone That Makes These Work

Before the questions — notice how they're delivered. The trainer's tone throughout is:

-   **Calm**
    
-   **Relaxed**
    
-   **Conversational**
    

This isn't an interrogation or a check-in call with a hidden agenda. It's a genuine dialogue. That tone is what causes clients to open up honestly instead of giving polished non-answers.

---

## The Client Retention Question Bank

### Understanding What Needs to Improve

> *"What type of changes do you feel we could make to help you achieve even greater results?"*

Gets you direct, actionable feedback on what's not quite working — before they start looking elsewhere.

---

> *"In what ways do you feel we could improve that would be able to help you on your end?"*

A softer version of the same probe. Frames improvement as being for their benefit, not your evaluation.

---

> *"Sabrina, would you be open to giving me some advice on how we could possibly do better for your company?"*

Asking for *advice* rather than *feedback* is more disarming. It positions them as the expert and makes them more likely to be candid.

---

> *"How could we make your life easier?"* → They answer → *"Can you tell me more about that?"*

Simple, open-ended, and powerful. The follow-up probe is essential — don't let the first answer be the final answer.

---

### Reinforcing Why They Chose You

> *"How do you feel we were able to help you the most so far?"* → *"Why do you feel that way though?"*

This question does double duty. It surfaces what's working — and it causes the client to actively remind themselves why they made the right decision by working with you. That's how you prevent buyers' remorse.

---

> *"How do you see us being different from the company you used to work with?"* → *"Why do you feel like we are though?"*

Same principle — you're getting them to articulate your value in their own words. That's far more powerful than anything you could say about yourself.

---

> *"What do you feel we're doing the best for you that's helping you get where you're wanting to go?"*

Another reinforcement question. Every time a client says out loud what you're doing well for them, they're reaffirming their own decision to stay with you.

---

### Forward-Looking Questions

> *"What goals would you like to see us help you get to within the next 12 months?"*

Positions you not just as a current vendor but as a long-term partner in their growth. It extends the relationship forward and ties your success to theirs.

---

## What These Questions Do Together

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Monitor relationships proactively</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Don't wait for a client to go quiet before checking in</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Their words &gt; your claims</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Getting clients to say your value out loud is more powerful than any pitch</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Buyers' remorse is preventable</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Regular reinforcement questions keep them confident in their decision</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Calm and conversational always wins</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Formal check-ins feel like audits — relaxed conversations feel like care</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Always probe the first answer</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"Can you tell me more about that?" unlocks what they really mean</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Schedule a check-in with your top 3–5 existing clients this month
    
-   \[ \] Use at least two reinforcement questions and one improvement question per check-in
    
-   \[ \] Always follow up their first answer with *"Can you tell me more about that?"* or *"Why do you feel that way though?"*
    
-   \[ \] After each check-in, note: are they at risk, neutral, or strongly engaged?
    
-   \[ \] Build a recurring quarterly habit of asking the 12-month goals question with every active client

### 16 Questions - External Customers

https://youtu.be/qVe2B21ibVo

# Questions About Your Prospect's External Customers

---

## Overview

These questions are designed for a specific situation: when your prospect's ability to keep *their own customers* is at stake. The goal is to get prospects to feel the consequences of staying in status quo — losing their clients — if they don't adopt your solution.

> **Important disclaimer:** These questions are not for every industry. Use them only when your prospect has significant contact with external customers and your solution directly affects their ability to retain those customers.

*Example: You sell fulfillment services to a social media marketing agency that is struggling to deliver results for their clients and is at risk of losing them.*

---

## The Core Strategy

Most prospects don't feel urgency until they see the real cost of inaction. These questions make that cost vivid — by walking them through what happens to *their* customers if they don't solve the problem. Pain felt on behalf of their own business is far more motivating than any feature you could pitch.

---

## The External Customer Question Bank

### Understanding Their Customer Base

> *"Can you go over who your most valuable customers are for your company?"*

> *"What does your typical customer look like for you — just so we understand on our end?"*

These are scene-setting questions. You're building a picture of who they serve before you help them see what's at risk.

---

### Surfacing Consequence: What Happens If They Do Nothing

> *"What would their customers expect from them as far as results — so they don't go somewhere else?"*

A consequence question. It plants the seed: if you're not delivering what your customers expect, they leave. This sets up the urgency for everything that follows.

---

> *"Amy, what if you don't do anything about this — and you start losing clients because of it?"*

Direct consequence question. Forces them to visualize the outcome of staying in status quo. Don't soften it — the discomfort is the point.

---

### Probing How They Measure and Retain Customers

> *"Can I ask how your clients would measure success from doing business with you?"*

When they answer → probe immediately:

> *"Why is that though?"* or *"Tell me more about that so I understand."*

---

> *"What are you doing now to make sure you keep your clients from going somewhere else?"*

A direct question that reveals whether they have a retention strategy — or are just hoping for the best.

---

> *"How do you see your customers' expectations changing over the next few years?"*

Forward-looking. Opens the gap between where they are now and where they'll need to be — and whether their current solution can get them there.

---

### Finding the Crack: Why Customers Leave Them

> *"John, can you tell me some of the main reasons why your clients would possibly go with your competitors instead of you — just so we understand?"*

When they give you reasons → probe:

> *"Hold on though — why do you feel like they would?"*

When they confirm it's happening → probe further:

> *"How long has that been going on for?"*

Let them sit with how long the problem has existed. Then:

> *"Losing that type of customer base over the last \[X\] years — has that had an impact on you?"* → *"Oh my gosh, you have no idea."* → *"In what way though?"*

**This is where the deep emotion surfaces.** Keep probing until they've fully expressed the impact — not just the problem, but how it has affected them personally and as a business.

---

### Strengths and Weaknesses Sequence

> *"What would you say your biggest strengths are as a company?"*

Immediately follow with:

> *"What are some of the weaknesses though — if you really thought about it?"*

When they share a weakness → probe:

> *"How long has that been going on?"* *"Has that had an impact on you guys?"* → *"In what way though?"*

This sequence — strength then weakness — lowers their guard before surfacing the vulnerability. Probing the weakness reveals the root cause, how the problem developed, and most importantly, how it's affecting them right now.

---

## The Full Probing Sequence at a Glance

-   \[ \] Identify whether your prospects serve external customers — if yes, these questions apply directly to your sales process
    
-   \[ \] Memorize the consequence question: *"What if you don't do anything about this and start losing clients because of it?"* and practice delivering it with calm, genuine concern
    
-   \[ \] Build your own version of the strengths/weaknesses sequence for your industry
    
-   \[ \] In your next discovery call, use the duration probe *("how long has that been going on?")* and the impact probe *("has that had an impact on you — in what way?")* and note how the conversation shifts

### 17 Questions - Expanded Probing

https://youtu.be/oDAvb5kAkRY

# Expanded Probing Questions: Getting Prospects to Open Up Even More

---

## Overview

You've already learned how to clarify and probe. This module goes deeper — **expanded probing questions** are designed to get prospects to elaborate further, surface more emotion, and feel the full weight of their problems. This is where urgency is built.

> *"Without pain, there is no sale. Without the gap, there is no sale."*

Prospects buy on emotion 100% of the time. They only justify with logic afterward. Brain studies confirm this. Expanded probing is how you access that emotion.

---

## The Core Principle

Every time a prospect mentions a problem, your instinct should be: *go deeper*. Don't move on. Don't pivot to your solution. Ask them to expand — because the more they elaborate, the more they feel the pain, and the more urgency they build to solve it.

Think of yourself as their doctor. You don't just want to hear the symptom — you want them to hear themselves describe it. That's what moves them to act.

---

## The Five Leading Phrases for Expanded Probing

These are your building blocks. Every expanded probing question starts with one of these:

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Leading Phrase</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Effect</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Walk me through…"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Gets them to explain step by step — reveals the full picture and who's involved</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Tell me more…"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Simple, powerful, and universally applicable — invites elaboration immediately</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Can you share with me…"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Soft and collaborative — feels like curiosity, not interrogation</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Describe for me…"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Encourages detail and specificity — helps you understand exactly what they want</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Can you explain to me in more detail…"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Signals you're listening carefully and want to fully understand before responding</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## The Expanded Probing Question Bank

### Deepening a Problem They've Just Mentioned

> *"Tell me more about that."*

The simplest and most powerful. Use it immediately after any problem statement. No framing needed — just ask.

---

> *"Can you give me a specific example so I can understand this better?"*

Gets them to move from abstract complaint to concrete story. Specific examples intensify the felt pain — and give you material to reference in your presentation.

---

> *"Can you explain to me in more detail — just so I understand?"*

Signals that you're genuinely trying to understand, not just waiting to pitch. Causes them to go further into the root cause and how the problem is affecting them.

---

### Understanding Their Decision-Making Process

> *"Can you walk me through your company's decision-making process?"*

Far more effective than asking *"Are you the decision maker?"* — which is a closed question that often gets a deflecting yes. This open version reveals the actual org chart of influence and shows you exactly who else needs to be brought on board.

---

> *"Sally, walk me through the criteria you use to make a decision on something like this."*

Reveals how they evaluate solutions — so you can position your offer to match exactly what they're looking for.

---

### Understanding What They're Looking For

> *"Can you go over with me the qualities you look for when choosing a company to work with — just so we understand?"*

Helps them surface what they truly want and gives you a roadmap for the presentation stage — you'll know exactly what to emphasize.

---

> *"Shakir, describe for me what you're possibly looking for — just to see if we could actually help."*

Another open invitation that gets them to define their ideal outcome in their own words — which you then mirror back to them later.

---

### Follow-Up Probes After Any Answer

> *"How did you feel about that?"*

Shifts from the logical/factual to the emotional. Feelings are where buying decisions live.

> *"In what way though?"*

Use after any statement where they hint at impact. Gets them to put the effect of the problem into their own words.

---

## How These Work Together: The Probing Chain

The goal is never a single question — it's a sequence that keeps going deeper:

1.  Prospect mentions a problem
    
2.  *"Tell me more about that."*
    
3.  They elaborate
    
4.  *"Can you give me a specific example?"*
    
5.  They give an example
    
6.  *"How long has that been going on?"*
    
7.  *"Has that had an impact on you — in what way?"*
    

Each question pulls them further into the experience of the problem. By the end, they're not just describing a challenge — they're feeling it. That's when urgency becomes real.

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Emotion drives buying, logic justifies it</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Your job is to surface emotion — not present features</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Never move on from a problem too quickly</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Every problem is an opportunity to probe deeper</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Specific examples intensify pain</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Vague problems feel manageable; concrete stories feel urgent</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Walk me through" reveals the org chart</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Never assume you know who's deciding — ask how decisions get made</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Pain is the gap between now and what they want</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Without that gap, there's no reason to change</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Memorize the five leading phrases: *walk me through, tell me more, can you share with me, describe for me, can you explain to me*
    
-   \[ \] In your next discovery call, practice using at least three expanded probing questions after a prospect mentions a problem
    
-   \[ \] Replace *"Are you the decision maker?"* with *"Can you walk me through your company's decision-making process?"* in every future B2B call
    
-   \[ \] Build a three-step probing chain for the most common problem your prospects face — and rehearse it until it flows naturally
