### 18 Questions - Decision Making

https://youtu.be/_ANTZqxLfUo

# Decision-Making Questions: Shifting from Price to Results-Based Thinking

---

## Overview

In B2B sales, understanding how a company makes decisions is critical — but these questions do more than just map the org chart. Used well, they shift the prospect's mindset from *"who's cheapest?"* to *"who can actually solve our problem?"* That shift is what prevents you from being commoditized and forced to compete on price.

> *"Value always outweighs price — if you can pull out enough emotion from your prospect."*

---

## The Core Principle

Most prospects default to price-based thinking because it's easy and familiar. Your decision-making questions interrupt that pattern and redirect their focus toward **results, outcomes, and what solving the problem is actually worth to them** — personally and professionally.

---

## The Decision-Making Question Bank

### Mapping the Decision Process

> *"Can you walk me through the criteria you use when picking a company to solve these issues you've talked about?"*

Reveals how they evaluate vendors and surfaces other decision makers you may not know about yet — so you can bring them into the process before they derail a deal at the end.

---

> *"How do you measure success with who you're using now?"*

Planted when they already have a current vendor. Gets them to assess whether their existing solution is actually delivering — and opens the door to your alternative.

---

> *"Brunson, let's suppose you're looking at several different companies who all meet your criteria — including on price. How would you then decide what to do?"*

Critical when you know they're also talking to competitors. Forces them to articulate the tiebreaker — and tells you exactly what to emphasize to win.

---

> *"You had mentioned that your company has budgeted \[XYZ\] to fix this problem — can I ask how that was determined?"*

Uncovers the real decision makers behind the budget and gives you a fuller picture of the company's funding process — and whether you need to bring other people into the conversation.

---

### Shifting from Price to Value

> *"You had mentioned that the most important thing to you is cost — can I ask how that compares to actually getting results and being able to solve the problem though?"*

This is one of the most powerful reframes in the entire training. It doesn't argue against their price focus — it simply asks them to weigh price against outcomes. Most prospects, when asked directly, will acknowledge that results matter more than cost. That's the shift you need.

---

> *"Do you feel the budget you've been given is sufficient to actually solve this problem?"* *(skeptical tone)*

Use when their stated budget is smaller than your solution costs. Delivered with calm skepticism — not accusation. The goal is to get them thinking: *maybe we need to go back and get more budget approved*, rather than *how do we find a cheaper option*.

---

### Building Urgency Around Their Future State

> *"Let's suppose we're able to make these changes for you and get you that type of increase in profitability — what would you do with the extra revenue coming in?"*

They'll give you logical answers — reinvest, hire, expand. Then immediately follow up with:

> *"What would that do for you personally — to actually be able to do that?"*

**Why this works:** The first question gets them describing business outcomes. The second question connects those outcomes to personal meaning — ambition, security, legacy, relief. Once they're attached to that future state emotionally, urgency to act now becomes natural.

---

### Checking What Resonates After Your Presentation

> *"Based on what we just went over — what resonates with you the most?"*

When they answer, probe immediately:

> *"Can I ask why that resonates most over \[the other option\]?"*

Shows you exactly what they value about your solution in their own words — which you can then reinforce and mirror back during closing.

---

## Decision-Making Question Summary

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Question</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Purpose</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"Walk me through your criteria for choosing a company"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Maps the decision process and surfaces hidden decision makers</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"How do you measure success with your current vendor?"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Opens doubt about whether their current solution is working</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"If several companies met your criteria, how would you decide?"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Reveals the real tiebreaker — tells you what to win on</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"How was your budget for this determined?"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Uncovers who controls funding and who else is deciding</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"How does cost compare to actually getting results?"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Shifts price-based thinking to value-based thinking</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"Is the budget sufficient to actually solve the problem?"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Prompts them to seek more budget rather than a cheaper solution</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"What would you do with the extra revenue?" + "What would that do for you personally?"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Connects outcomes to personal emotion and builds urgency</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"What resonates most — and why?"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Surfaces what they value so you can reinforce it at close</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Price-based thinking = commoditization</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>If they're comparing on price, you've lost control of the conversation</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Results-based thinking = your competitive advantage</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Shift focus to outcomes and you're no longer competing on price</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Personal stakes trump business outcomes</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"What would that do for <em>you</em> personally?" is where the real urgency lives</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Budget skepticism isn't aggression</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Calmly questioning whether their budget is enough prompts them to find more, not go cheaper</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Always probe what resonates</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Their answer tells you exactly how to close</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Memorize the price-to-results reframe: *"How does cost compare to actually getting results and solving the problem?"* — and practice delivering it without apology
    
-   \[ \] Add the future-state sequence to your next discovery call: *"What would you do with the extra revenue?"* followed by *"What would that do for you personally?"*
    
-   \[ \] Replace *"Are you the decision maker?"* with *"Can you walk me through your company's decision-making process?"* permanently
    
-   \[ \] After your next presentation, ask *"What resonates with you the most?"* and note what they say — that's your closing angle

### 19 Questions - Customer Service

https://youtu.be/UFALO9O17yo

# Customer Service Questions: How to Resolve Complaints Without Arguing

---

## Overview

When a customer complains, the instinct for most salespeople is to defend themselves and prove they're right. That's the wrong move. This module covers how to use NEPQ-style questions to let the customer vent, feel heard, and open up to solutions — without a single argument.

> *"Your goal is to win the relationship — not the argument."*

---

## The Core Principle

When a client is upset, their emotions are facts to them. Arguing with those emotions doesn't resolve them — it inflames them. What every upset customer actually wants is simple:

1.  **To be heard**
    
2.  **To be understood**
    

Questions give them both. Once they've gotten it all off their chest, they naturally calm down and become open to solutions.

---

## Phase 1 — Let Them Get It Out

These questions open the conversation and allow the customer to fully express what happened. Don't rush this phase.

> *"What happened exactly — just so I know?"*

Invites them to tell the full story. The act of telling it is itself relieving.

---

> *"Can you say more about that so we understand on our end?"*

A softer rephrasing of the same intent. Signals that you're listening, not defending.

---

> *"Amy, I can appreciate you bringing this to my attention — can you tell me everything you know about what happened?"*

Acknowledges their concern before asking for details. The phrase *"I can appreciate you bringing this to my attention"* does a lot of relational work — it reframes the complaint as a gift rather than an attack.

---

> *"What else can you tell me about this?"*

> *"How do you think it got to that point?"*

Follow-up probes for after their first explanation. Keep going until they've fully vented — the more complete the picture, the better equipped you are to fix it.

---

## Phase 2 — Acknowledge and Move Toward Resolution

Once they've fully expressed themselves, shift into problem-solving mode.

> *"Sally, I apologize if this happened — what would you like to see done at this point so we can correct this for you?"*

**Key sequence:** Apologize first, then ask what they want. This disarms them and hands them some control over the resolution — which makes them far more likely to accept whatever you offer.

---

> *"Shakir, this is upsetting to me — how soon can we meet to discuss this in person?"*

Use this for larger accounts or serious complaints. Showing that *you* are upset signals that you take it seriously. Requesting an in-person meeting shows you're committed to resolving it, not brushing it off.

*Note: Match the meeting format to the size of the account — phone for transactional situations, Zoom or in-person for high-value accounts at risk.*

---

> *"Would it help you if I did some fact-finding and then got back together with you to discuss possible actions that could take care of this for you?"*

Buys you time to investigate while keeping the client engaged and feeling cared for. The phrase *"would it help you"* keeps the focus on their benefit — not your process.

---

> *"Jamie, if anything else happens in the meantime — can you come to me so I can help you?"*

Positions you as their advocate going forward. Builds trust and keeps the relationship open even while the issue is being resolved.

---

## The Customer Service Question Sequence at a Glance

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 75px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Phase</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Question</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Purpose</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Open</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"What happened exactly — just so I know?"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Get the full story</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Expand</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"Can you say more about that?" / "What else can you tell me?"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Let them fully vent</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Acknowledge</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"I can appreciate you bringing this to my attention…"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Show you're listening and you care</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Probe root cause</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"How do you think it got to that point?"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Understand what actually went wrong</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Apologize + ask</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"I apologize — what would you like to see done?"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Disarm and hand them some control</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Commit to action</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"Would it help if I did some fact-finding and got back to you?"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Show follow-through</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Keep the door open</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"If anything else happens, can you come to me?"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Rebuild trust going forward</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Win the relationship, not the argument</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Arguments damage trust permanently — questions rebuild it</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Emotions are facts to the client</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Don't challenge how they feel — acknowledge it</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Let them vent completely</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Partial venting = partial resolution. Let them finish</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Apologize before asking for what they want</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The apology opens them up to your solution</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Would it help you if…"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Frames everything as being for them — not convenient for you</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Memorize the opening question: *"What happened exactly — just so I know?"* and practice delivering it with calm, genuine concern
    
-   \[ \] Replace any defensive response habits with *"Can you say more about that so I understand?"*
    
-   \[ \] Practice the apologize-then-ask sequence: *"I apologize if this happened — what would you like to see done?"*
    
-   \[ \] For your top accounts, identify who to call if a complaint arises — and reach out proactively before problems surface using the questions from Module 15

### 20 Partner Objections

https://youtu.be/ZBJXfrSY-kE

# Handling the "I Need to Talk to My Spouse / Partner" Objection

---

## Overview

The spouse or partner objection is one of the most common in sales. Before you can handle it, you need to understand what it actually means — because it almost never means what it sounds like on the surface.

---

## The Two Real Reasons Behind This Objection

**Reason 1 — Smoke Screen (You Haven't Built Enough Emotion)** The prospect is using it to get rid of you. They don't feel enough urgency or pain to move forward. This is a sales process problem — not a spouse problem. It means your discovery questions didn't go deep enough.

**Reason 2 — Logistical (They Genuinely Need to Discuss)** The prospect is actually interested but habitually makes major purchases with their spouse or partner. In B2B, it means other decision makers need to be involved.

> How do you tell the difference? Read their tonality, body language, and how they've been engaging throughout the call. The answer will usually be obvious.

**Either way — your initial response is similar.** Acknowledge, probe the consequence, then lock in a specific follow-up time.

---

## The Response Framework (Generic Template)

**Step 1 — Acknowledge without caving**

> *"That's not a problem — how does your \[spouse/partner\] feel about you \[repeat back what they said they wanted\]?"*

**Step 2 — Consequence question if they say the spouse is supportive**

> *"I understand — what are you going to do if you go to them and they don't want you to \[repeat back the benefit they said they wanted\]?"*

**Step 3 — Let them sit with that** They'll usually say something like *"I'd have to figure something out"* or *"I'm not sure."*

**Step 4 — Follow-up consequence if needed**

> *"Well, how will you \[repeat back their desired outcome\] if they don't let you \[take action\]?"*

**Step 5 — Book a specific time**

> *"I understand — what's your time frame on getting back to me? The next day or so, just to see if I'd be available for you?"*

If they say *"I'll call you in the next few days"* — that's too vague. Use the counter-commitment:

> *"What I can do, if you have your calendar handy — I can pull up mine so you can book a specific time with me in the next day or so, just so you don't have to chase me down and vice versa. Would that be appropriate?"*

**Never leave without a specific booked time. A vague "I'll call you back" almost never results in a callback.**

---

## Industry Examples

### B2C — Amazon / E-Commerce Coaching

> Prospect: *"I like this but I need to talk to my spouse."* You: *"That's not a problem — how does your spouse feel about you starting your own business so you can make more money for your family?"* Prospect: *"They'd probably support it."* You: *"What would you do if you go to them and they don't want you to put the funding into your business — how would you even get started so you can get out of your job and have more time with your kids?"*

*(Consequence question — ties back to both benefits they mentioned: more money + more time with family)*

---

### B2C — Life Insurance

> Prospect: *"I just need to talk to my spouse."* You: *"That's not a problem — how does your spouse feel about you having stronger financial protection to pay for the house and the expenses you mentioned, in case something does actually happen to you?"* → Probe consequence: *"What would you do if they don't want you to put the extra funds into the policy — and then something did happen?"* → Further consequence: *"What will she have to do if you did pass away early and she gets stuck with the mortgage and all those bills but doesn't have the money to cover them?"* → Challenge: *"Are you willing to settle for that and take that risk?"* *(skeptical tone)* → If they still want to talk to spouse: book a specific time.

---

### B2B — Lead Generation / Marketing Services

> Prospect: *"This sounds great but I need to talk to my business partner."* You: *"That's not a problem — how does your business partner feel about getting higher quality leads to your salespeople so they make more sales?"* → Consequence: *"What are you going to do if they don't want to put the extra funding into getting those higher quality leads — and your sales stay the same for the next 3, 6, or even 12 months?"* → If they're still committed to involving the partner: *"What I can do is set up a call with you and your business partner to discuss how we might be able to solve that together — would that help you?"* → Then book the calendar commitment.

---

## The Counter-Commitment Script (Always Use This)

When they give a vague *"I'll call you back in a few days"*:

> *"What I can do — if you have your calendar handy, I can pull up mine so you can book a specific time with me in the next day or so, just so you don't have to chase me down and vice versa. Would that be appropriate?"*

**Why the language matters:**

-   *"Just to see if I'd be available for you"* — positions you as busy and in demand, not desperate
    
-   *"You don't have to chase me down and vice versa"* — frames it as mutual convenience, not you chasing a sale
    
-   Experts are busy. Salespeople are always available. Don't be always available.
    

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Diagnose before responding</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Is it a smoke screen or logistical? The question approach differs slightly</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Repeat back their desired outcome</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Makes the consequence of not acting feel personal and specific</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Consequence questions do the work</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Gets them to realize that staying in status quo means giving up what they said they wanted</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Always book a specific time</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Vague callbacks almost never happen — a booked slot protects the deal</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Position as the expert, not the chaser</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"Just to see if I'm available for you" reframes who has the power in the relationship</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Write out the response framework in your own words for your specific product/service
    
-   \[ \] Identify the top 2 benefits your prospects most commonly want — these are what you'll repeat back when this objection comes up
    
-   \[ \] Build your own consequence question using the template: *"What would you do if you go to them and they don't want you to \[benefit\] — and you end up \[negative outcome\]?"*
    
-   \[ \] Memorize the counter-commitment script and practice it until it sounds completely natural
    
-   \[ \] Never end another call with a vague "I'll call you back" — always leave with a booked time

### 21 Overcome - Think It Over

https://youtu.be/qpg3TIywiwU

# Handling the "I Need to Think It Over" Objection

---

## Overview

"I need to think it over" is one of the most common objections in sales — and most of the time, it's a signal that something went wrong earlier in the conversation, not at the close.

---

## Why This Objection Happens

When a prospect says they want to think it over, it almost always means one of two things:

1.  **You didn't ask the right questions** — they can't clearly see their problem, its root cause, or how it's affecting them. Because you couldn't help them see the gap, the problem doesn't feel urgent enough to act on now.
    
2.  **They don't believe you can get them the result** — your questioning didn't build enough emotional connection to your solution.
    

> *"You can't tell them they have a problem. You have to ask the right questions that allow them to tell themselves — and when they do, they persuade themselves."*

**The Gap Model:** The larger the gap between their **current situation** and their **desired objective state** — and the clearer the problems blocking that gap — the more urgency they feel to act now rather than later.

*Note for B2B / complex sales: longer sales cycles of 3–6 months are normal. This objection framework still applies — it just plays out over more touchpoints.*

---

## The Response Framework

**Step 1 — Acknowledge without panic**

> *"Yeah, that's not a problem — what's your time frame on getting back to me? The next day or two, just to see if I'd be available for you?"*

*(Positions you as busy, not desperate.)*

---

**Step 2 — If they say "I'll call you in a few days" — don't accept vagueness**

> *"What I can do — if you have your calendar handy — I can pull up mine and have you book a specific time with me. That way you don't have to chase me down and vice versa. Would that be appropriate?"*

Book the time before you leave or end the call.

---

**Step 3 — The key move: ask what's really on their mind**

As you're wrapping up, say this:

> *"Before I go — what were you wanting to go over in your mind, just so I'd be prepared to answer your questions when we talk tomorrow?"*

**Why this works:** It's softer than *"what do you want to think about?"* — and because you're about to leave, they're far more likely to tell you what the real concern actually is. That's your opening to address it directly.

---

**Step 4 — Once the real concern surfaces**

Don't argue. Clarify, ask a diffusing question, and work through it together as two people trying to find a solution — not a salesperson trying to overcome a roadblock.

> *"Selling is not combative. It's not adversarial. It's supposed to be collaborative."*

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Think it over" = insufficient pain surfaced</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Go back to your discovery questions — the objection was created earlier, not at the close</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>The gap drives urgency</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>No gap = no urgency = "I'll think about it"</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Always book a specific time</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Vague callbacks don't happen — a scheduled slot protects the deal</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Position as busy, not desperate</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"Just to see if I'd be available for you" flips the power dynamic</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"What were you wanting to go over?"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The exit question that surfaces the real concern right before you leave</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Selling is collaborative</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You're working <em>with</em> them to find a solution — not fighting to close a sale</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] The next time you get this objection, resist the urge to immediately defend your solution — acknowledge it and book the time first
    
-   \[ \] Memorize the exit question: *"Before I go — what were you wanting to go over in your mind, just so I'd be prepared?"*
    
-   \[ \] After your last three "think it over" situations — identify at which point in the conversation the gap wasn't built clearly enough
    
-   \[ \] Practice the counter-commitment script until it sounds natural and confident, not scripted

---

### 22 Overcome - Money

https://youtu.be/P9ZcDMIASk0

# Handling the "We Don't Have the Money / Budget" Objection

---

## Overview

The money objection is one of the most common closes in sales. The key insight: when a prospect says they can't afford it, your job isn't to discount or back down — it's to reconnect them to what they said they wanted and help them find a way to fund it themselves.

> *"No money = no result. Tie the funding to the outcome they told you they wanted — and let them figure out how to get it."*

---

## Why This Framework Works

If you've asked the right questions throughout the conversation, the prospect already believes your solution can solve their problem. The money objection isn't about your price — it's about their perceived inability to access funds. Your questions help them discover options they hadn't considered.

---

## The Response Framework (Generic Template)

**Step 1 — Test if the money is the only barrier**

> *"John, tell me — if you did have the funding, is this something that would work for you?"*

If your discovery was solid, they'll say yes. If they say no, you have a different problem — go back to uncovering pain.

---

**Step 2 — Make them articulate why it would work**

> *"Why do you feel like it would though?"* *(skeptical tone)*

Gets them to restate the value of your solution in their own words — reinforcing their own desire before you address the funding.

---

**Step 3 — Acknowledge and redirect to solutions**

> *"I can appreciate that money might be an issue from what you told me — how do you think you can resolve that, so that you can \[repeat back what they said they wanted\]?"*

Hand the problem back to them. Most prospects — when they trust you and genuinely want the result — will start generating solutions themselves:

-   *"I could use a credit card"*
    
-   *"I could get a loan"*
    
-   *"I could pull from savings / 401k / stocks"*
    
-   *"I could go back to the boss and request a bigger budget"*
    

---

**Step 4 — If they can't come up with ideas, probe further**

> *"What other avenues do you have to find the funding so that you can \[repeat back their desired outcome\]?"*

---

**Step 5 — If they still can't, make a suggestion**

> *"Can I make a suggestion? Have you ever considered putting it on a credit card and paying it off once you start getting results?"*

Only suggest this after they've exhausted their own ideas. Offering it too early removes the valuable step of them discovering it themselves.

---

## Industry Examples — Filling in the Blank

The formula is always: *"What other avenues do you have to find the funding to \[their desired outcome\]?"*

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Industry</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Fill-in Example</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Home security</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"…to protect your home and family from intruders in this area"</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Life insurance</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"…to get stronger protection for your family and kids in case something happens to you"</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Real estate</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"…to use as a down payment so you can get your family the dream home you mentioned"</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Lead generation</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"…to start getting higher quality leads so your salespeople can make more sales"</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Health coaching</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"…to get rid of the skin problem so it doesn't keep coming back"</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>E-commerce training</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"…to start the business so you can start making more money"</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Rental property</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"…to purchase this rental so you can get a greater return on your money than last year"</em></p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Bonus: When They Ask About Pricing Too Early

If a prospect asks for price before you've done proper discovery, don't just quote a number. Instead, qualify their budget first:

> *"I'm assuming you understand that to \[get their desired result\] it's going to require some type of funding or investment. With your situation — how much do you have to put into this to make sure you actually get \[their outcome\]?"*

If their answer aligns with your pricing — confirm it:

> *"You're going to need around \[X\] to make sure \[outcome\]. Is that something you can manage if it fits what you're looking for?"*

If they have no budget at all — you've saved yourself the time of pursuing an unqualified prospect.

---

## The Complete Generic Script

> *"John, tell me — if you did have the funding, is this something that would work for you?"* → *"Yes, I think it would."* *"Why do you feel like it would though?"* → *"I like \[this, this, and this\] — we just don't have the money right now."* *"I can appreciate that money might be an issue from what you told me. How do you think you can resolve that so that you can \[their desired outcome\]?"* → They offer ideas, or they can't. *"What other avenues do you have to find the funding so that you can \[their desired outcome\]?"* → Still stuck: *"Can I make a suggestion? Have you ever considered \[option other clients have used\]?"*

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Test if money is the real objection first</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"If you did have the funding, would this work?" — if no, you have a bigger problem</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Make them articulate the value</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"Why do you feel like it would?" gets them to sell themselves</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Let them generate solutions</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>People commit more strongly to ideas they came up with themselves</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Tie funding to their outcome</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>No money = no result. The equation makes the case without pressure</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Suggest only as a last resort</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Offering credit cards too early removes the self-discovery moment</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Early price questions = budget qualification</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Flip it — ask how much they have before you reveal your price</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Write your own version of the money objection script with your specific product/service outcome filled in
    
-   \[ \] Memorize the core sequence: test → why → how to resolve → avenues → suggest
    
-   \[ \] Identify 2–3 funding options your past clients have used — these become your suggestion examples
    
-   \[ \] Practice the early pricing response: *"How much do you have to put into this to make sure you get \[outcome\]?"*
    
-   \[ \] Never discount as a first response to the money objection — use this framework first

### 23 References

https://youtu.be/MUaHtgcAEg8

# Handling the "Can You Send Me References?" Objection

---

## Overview

References from satisfied clients can be a genuine tool to help a hesitant prospect move forward — but they can also be a polite way to get rid of you. Your job is to find out which one it is before you send anyone's contact details.

> **Warning: If you send references without securing a commitment on what happens next, you will almost never hear from that prospect again.**

---

## The Two Reasons Behind This Request

1.  **They have a genuine concern** — something specific is holding them back and they want to hear from real clients before deciding
    
2.  **It's a smoke screen** — they're not serious and are using references as a way to stall or exit the conversation
    

NEPQ qualifying questions reveal which one it is — without confrontation.

---

## The Response Framework

**Step 1 — Agree, then immediately probe what they actually want to know**

> *"Yeah, that's not a problem — I'm curious, what would you like to ask them when you call?"*

or:

> *"That's not a problem — just so I can send you the right people, what specifically would you like to discuss with them?"*

**Why this works:** If it's a real concern, they'll tell you exactly what they want to ask — which tells you what their actual objection is. If it's a smoke screen, they'll struggle to give you a specific answer.

---

**Step 2 — Understand their timeline**

> *"That makes sense — when do you plan on calling them so I can let them know to see if they'd be available for you?"*

This does two things: it shows you're actively helping them move forward, and it creates a specific time frame you can follow up against.

---

**Step 3 — Pre-commit to the next step before references are sent**

Once they've given you a timeline:

> *"What I can do is reach out to them to see if that works for their schedules. Now — let's pretend for a moment that the clients you talk to say good things about how we were able to solve the same type of problems your company is having. Where do you think we should go from there?"*

**This is the critical step.** You're asking for a conditional commitment — if the references confirm everything checks out, what happens next? This separates the serious prospect from the time-waster before you invest in the reference call.

---

## What Their Answer Tells You

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Their Response</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>What It Means</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"We'd move forward with you"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Genuinely qualified — send the references</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"We'd still need to compare other options"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Other obstacles exist — address those first</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"I'm not sure / we'd have to see"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Low urgency — more discovery needed before sending anyone</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>Vague, non-committal answer</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Likely a smoke screen — qualify further</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>References without a next step = a dead end</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Always secure a conditional commitment before sending contacts</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"What would you like to ask them?"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Surfaces the real concern hiding behind the request</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Timeline creates accountability</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>A specific call date makes the conversation real, not theoretical</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Pre-commit to the next step</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"If they say good things — where do we go from there?" locks in progress</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Never send references again without first asking *"What specifically would you like to discuss with them?"*
    
-   \[ \] Practice the conditional commitment question: *"Let's pretend they say great things — where do you think we should go from there?"*
    
-   \[ \] Build a short list of 2–3 reference clients you can reach out to quickly when this comes up
    
-   \[ \] Always confirm the prospect's timeline before contacting your references — *"When do you plan on calling them?"*

### 24 Quote

https://youtu.be/B3UcPYjmI1U

# Handling the "Just Send Me a Quote" Request

---

## Overview

When a prospect asks for a quote in the first few minutes of a conversation, before you've done any discovery, it's almost always a reflex — not a genuine buying signal. Sending a blind quote is a waste of time. This module covers how to redirect that request into a real conversation.

---

## The Two Risks of Sending a Quote Too Early

1.  **They're fishing for information** — comparing prices with no real intention to buy
    
2.  **They don't have the budget** — using the quote to confirm they can't afford you, rather than to move forward
    

Either way, a quote without discovery does nothing to advance the sale.

---

## The Response Framework

**Step 1 — Acknowledge, then probe what they actually want from it**

> *"Yeah, that's not a problem — what is it that you're hoping to see from the quote, just so I understand?"*

They'll usually say something like: *"I just want to see if we have the budget for it."*

---

**Step 2 — Agree and redirect into discovery**

> *"I understand — that makes sense. It might make sense, before I send a quote, if I understood a little bit more about your situation, just to see if we could even help you in the first place. For example, what type of \[first situation question\]…?"*

This transitions you smoothly into discovery without making them feel interrogated. You're framing it as being in *their* interest — to make sure the quote is actually relevant to their situation.

---

**Step 3 — After full discovery, revisit the quote with a commitment question**

At the *end* of the discovery conversation — not at the beginning — come back to it:

> *"So Amy — what I can do is get to work on putting together a quote for you if you'd like. Now, let's assume for a minute that we get you the quote and we're able to meet your needs and solve that challenge you mentioned. What do you see as the next step from there?"*

**If they can clearly articulate a next step** — they're qualified. Build the quote.

**If they can't answer** — they're likely fishing for information or don't have the budget. Don't invest the time.

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Never send a blind quote</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Without discovery, a quote is just a number with no context or value</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"What are you hoping to see from it?"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Surfaces their real intent before you invest in building anything</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Discovery first, quote second</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The quote should reflect their specific situation — not a generic price list</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Always pre-commit before sending</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"If the quote works, what's the next step?"</em> separates serious buyers from tire-kickers</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] The next time a prospect asks for a quote in the first few minutes, use: *"What is it you're hoping to see from the quote, just so I understand?"*
    
-   \[ \] Practice the redirect into discovery: *"It might make sense if I understood a little more about your situation first…"*
    
-   \[ \] After your next discovery call, use the conditional commitment question before building any quote
    
-   \[ \] If a prospect can't tell you what happens after they see the quote — qualify them further before spending time on it

---

### 25 Information

https://youtu.be/7pEEssLpDJs

# Handling the "Just Send Me Some Information" Request

---

## Overview

"Just send me some information" is one of the most common deflection tactics prospects use when they're busy — or when they want to get off the phone. Like the quote request, sending information blindly almost never leads anywhere. This module covers how to turn that request into a real discovery conversation.

---

## The Core Rule

> **Never send information to a prospect without first finding out if they're serious about changing their situation. Otherwise you're wasting your selling time.**

---

## The Response Framework

**Step 1 — Agree, then probe what they actually need**

> *"Yeah, that's not a problem — just so I can put together the best information for you, what exactly are you looking for?"*

They'll tell you something like: *"I want to see how your product could help us in our department."*

---

**Step 2 — Bridge naturally into discovery**

> *"Oh okay — and what do you use now for your \[situation\]?"*

That one question transitions you straight into the engagement stage — situation questions, problem awareness, consequence questions — without it feeling like an interrogation. You're just following the thread they gave you.

---

**Step 3 — After full discovery, revisit their request with a commitment question**

At the end of the conversation, come back to the information request:

> *"Here's what I can do — I can go ahead and send you more information like you asked about, on how we can solve those challenges you brought up. Now, let's suppose you go through that information and it fits into what you're looking for — what would you want the next step to be?"*

If they can articulate a clear next step — they're a real prospect. Send the information.

If they can't — they're not serious. Qualify further before investing any more time.

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"What exactly are you looking for?"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Turns a vague request into a specific conversation</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>One situation question bridges everything</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"What do you use now for…?"</em> naturally opens the discovery stage</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Information comes after discovery</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Targeted information beats generic brochures every time</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Always pre-commit before sending</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"If it fits, what's the next step?"</em> filters serious prospects from time-wasters</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Next time a prospect asks for information, respond with: *"Just so I can put together the best information for you — what exactly are you looking for?"*
    
-   \[ \] Practice the bridge into discovery: *"And what do you use now for…?"*
    
-   \[ \] After discovery, use the conditional commitment before sending anything: *"If this fits what you're looking for — what would you want the next step to be?"*
    
-   \[ \] If they can't answer the next step question — don't send anything yet. Qualify first.

---

### 26 Call Me Back

https://youtu.be/bEPwhEPQPKI

# Handling the "Call Me Back Later" Brush-Off

---

## Overview

When a prospect answers the phone and immediately says *"I'm busy, call me back"* — most salespeople fold and say *"Sure, when should I call?"* That response is desperate and hands all the power to the prospect. Here's how to flip it.

---

## The Core Principle

> Experts are busy. Salespeople are always available. Never position yourself as the one who will chase them — position yourself as someone whose time is valuable and in demand.

---

## The Response Framework

**Step 1 — Don't offer to call them back. Give them your number instead.**

> *"That's not a problem — what I can do is give you my number and have you call me back, to see if I'd be available for you at that time. Would that be appropriate?"*

Give them the number. Then immediately:

> *"What's your time frame on getting back to me later today — just to see if I'd be available for you?"*

**Why this works:** Asking *when they'll call you* — rather than asking when you can call them — signals that your time is valuable. You're not sitting around waiting for their call. You might not even be available.

---

**Step 2 — If they give a vague time frame, don't accept it**

> Prospect: *"I can probably get back to you sometime later this week."*

> *"I'm not sure if I'd be available for you then — I do have my calendar handy if you have yours. We can book a specific time so you don't have to chase me down and vice versa. Would that be appropriate?"*

Same counter-commitment script as always. A vague callback is no callback. Lock in a specific time.

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Give your number — don't offer to call them</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Flips the power dynamic immediately</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Just to see if I'd be available"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Signals you're busy, not desperate</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Never accept a vague time frame</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"Sometime this week" almost never results in a call</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Book a specific time every time</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>A calendared slot is the only real commitment</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Stop saying *"Sure, when can I call you back?"* — replace it with giving your number and asking when they'll reach you
    
-   \[ \] Memorize: *"Just to see if I'd be available for you at that time"* — and practice until it sounds natural
    
-   \[ \] Use the counter-commitment calendar close any time they give a vague timeframe

---

### 28 Jeremy Miner Video 1

https://youtu.be/wgSUSTXQs20

# Top 5 Traits of Highly Successful Salespeople — Jeremy Miner

---

## Overview

Before diving into sales process mechanics, this session covers the internal traits — the mindset and habits — that separate legends from savages, and savages from average performers. Tactics without these foundations will never reach their full potential.

> *"All selling is change. It's about how good you are at getting your prospects to see — through your questioning — that where they are now is far more risky than making the change."*

---

## The Foundation: What Really Matters on a Call

Morning routines, meditation, journaling — they're fine. But when the prospect picks up the phone, none of that matters. What matters is:

-   **What you say**
    
-   **What you ask**
    
-   **How you deliver it** — so the prospect opens up to you
    

The entire sales process from first word to signed agreement is about one thing: **disarming the prospect — every 30 seconds.**

---

## Trait 1 — Resign from the Company Club

**What is the Company Club?** It's the group of salespeople — in every organization — who blame everything but themselves for poor results:

-   *"The leads are bad."*
    
-   *"The manager is terrible."*
    
-   *"The comps aren't fair."*
    
-   *"Prospects are broke."*
    
-   *"Prospects just have too much fear."*
    

**The truth about fear:** Every prospect has fear. That's human behavior 101. All selling is change — and people fear change. It is your job as a sales professional to help them overcome that fear. That's the whole role.

**Who's in the Company Club?** Average performers. Bottom performers. Always. The top performers are too busy calling leads and solving problems to join.

**The rule:** When you get off a bad call, the first question you ask is not *"why was this prospect so difficult?"* — it's *"what did I do wrong? Where did the call go sideways? What didn't I ask?"*

> Blame yourself, not them. That's the only path to getting better.

---

## Trait 2 — Be 1,000% Committed

There's a difference between 100% committed and 1,000% committed. Most people are 100% — that's the baseline. That just means you showed up. The question is: what are you doing *daily* to show yourself you're all in?

**Daily habits of the committed:**

-   **Write your to-do list the night before — not the morning of.** When you write it the night before, your subconscious processes it while you sleep. You wake up already knowing what to do. Writing it in the morning wastes time and mental energy.
    
-   **Study sales every single day.** Not a few weeks ago. Not once a month. Daily.
    
-   **Ask yourself: what does discipline mean to me?** Real discipline means working backwards from your outcome and doing whatever it takes to get there — consistently, not just when it's convenient.
    

**The Kobe Bryant model:** After winning an NBA title, Kobe was back in the gym at 4am — not 7am. Not 8am. He out-disciplined everyone. Tom Brady isn't the most physically gifted quarterback — he's the most disciplined. That's the model.

> *"If you want to be great, you have to do what others are not willing to do."*

---

## Trait 3 — Be Prepared

You get the same objections every day. Every week. If you're still surprised by them — or hoping they don't come up — that's a preparation problem, not a prospect problem.

**The prepared salesperson:**

-   Knows exactly what to say on every objection before the call starts
    
-   Has memorized their questions — not reading from a wall
    
-   Has rehearsed their lines until they sound completely natural
    

**The actor analogy:** Every word Megan Fox, Adam Sandler, or George Clooney says in a film is scripted. Yet it doesn't sound scripted — because they've rehearsed it until it becomes natural. When it sounds natural, you believe the character. When it sounds scripted, you feel manipulated.

**The same is true on a sales call.** If you're reading questions off a wall or a sheet, the prospect picks up on your tonality and knows it. They disengage — or get defensive. When your questions sound natural and conversational, they open up.

> The moment a prospect feels they're being sold — they put up sales resistance. Your job is to never trigger that.

**When a call goes wrong:** Don't blame the prospect. Ask what *you* did wrong. What question did you miss? Where did the conversation turn? That reflection is what makes you better on the next call.

---

## Trait 4 — Treat Every Prospect with Respect

Even if they don't buy.

There's a difference between **challenging** a prospect's thinking and **talking down** to them. Challenge their way of thinking — the way that's kept them stuck in their current situation. But never make them feel stupid for not buying.

**Why this matters:**

-   Prospects who respect you trust you
    
-   Prospects who trust you come back — even months later when circumstances change
    
-   If you never get a callback from someone who didn't buy, that's a signal you're making them feel put down, not challenged
    

> *"How many sales have you gotten because someone respected you, couldn't get the funding at the time, then messaged you three months later? That's how you know you're doing it right."*

One additional principle: how you treat people who can't do anything for you reveals your character. Never talk down to anyone — prospect, waiter, assistant, junior colleague. It's always a red flag to the people watching.

---

## Trait 5 — Invest in Your Skill Level Daily

Not weekly. Not quarterly. **Daily.**

**What are you putting into your brain every day?**

-   Political news? It's not making you any money.
    
-   Music playlists? Same.
    
-   Business, sales, behavioral science, psychology? Now you're building something.
    

**Make your car a university on wheels.** Brian Tracy's principle: learn while you drive. You can become more educated in the car than in a classroom — if you're intentional about what you listen to.

**The ignorance tax:**

> *"Your biggest expense in life is your lack of knowledge."*

Write down what you made last year. Write down what you want to make in the next 12 months. Subtract the first from the second. That gap — that number — is what you paid in ignorance tax. You paid life that amount because you haven't yet learned the right skills, or committed fully to developing them.

**The key question:** Are you willing to pay that same amount again next year?

**Invest in yourself — not just when the company pays for it.** When hiring, one of the first questions asked is: *"How much did you personally invest in your own sales training last year?"* If the answer is *"the company paid for it"* — that's a red flag. Top performers invest in themselves regardless.

> *"You have to become so good that people simply cannot ignore you."* — Steve Martin

Background doesn't matter. Connections don't matter. Age doesn't matter. What matters is whether you out-learn, out-discipline, and out-commit everyone around you. That's what creates a reputation that can't be ignored.

---

## The Income Gap Exercise

1.  Write down what you made last year
    
2.  Write down what you want to make in the next 12 months
    
3.  Subtract — circle the difference
    
4.  That number is your current cost of insufficient skills, commitment, and discipline
    
5.  Ask yourself: *"Am I willing to pay that again?"*
    

---

## Key Takeaways

-   \[ \] Write tonight's to-do list before you go to bed — start this habit now
    
-   \[ \] List the top 5 objections you get every week — do you have a prepared, rehearsed response for every single one?
    
-   \[ \] Calculate your income gap: goal minus last year's income — circle it and keep it visible

# The Connecting Stage: Introduction to Disarming Your Prospect

---

## Overview

Before any discovery, before any questions about problems or budget — you have to do one thing first: **disarm the prospect**. This module introduces the science behind why prospects go defensive the moment they pick up the phone, and how connecting questions solve it.

> *"All sales is change. And it's all about disarming your prospect — from the very first word to the moment the funds hit the bank."*

---

## The 7–12 Second Rule (Behavioral Science 101)

Within the first **7 to 12 seconds** of any interaction, your prospect is already picking up on:

-   Your **tonality**
    
-   Your **body language** (if on Zoom or in person)
    
-   What you **say** — and what you *don't* say
    

Based on those cues, their brain triggers one of three reactions. If you come across as **needy, nervous, or attached** — you trigger one of two defensive states.

---

## The Two Defensive States

### Fight Mode — Type A Personalities

These prospects come out swinging. You'll hear things like:

-   *"I don't have much time — how much is it going to cost?"*
    
-   *"I'm busy, can you call me back later?"*
    
-   *"I'm a straight shooter, just give me the facts."*
    
-   *"I work in sales, I know what you're doing."*
    
-   *"I'm not interested."*
    
-   *"What's this all about?"*
    

**What happens if you don't disarm them immediately:** The fight escalates within the next 10–15 seconds. The call is effectively over — even if they stay on the line. You've wasted 30–45 minutes and you knew it was dead at hello.

---

### Flight Mode — Passive Personalities

These prospects don't push back — they just disengage. You'll notice:

-   Two or three word answers to your questions
    
-   A vague feeling that something is off
    
-   Overly enthusiastic agreement: *"Yes, 100% I'm in — I just need 24 to 48 hours to think it over."*
    

**What happens next:** You follow up. They ghost you. Witness protection mode. They don't call back, don't respond to texts, don't reply to emails.

> You sensed it during the call — something didn't feel right, even though they said all the right things. That's flight mode. They were never really open.

---

## Why This Happens

Both fight mode and flight mode are triggered by the same thing: **the prospect sensing neediness, nervousness, or attachment** from the salesperson in those first few seconds.

The good news: **what you say in the next 3–5 seconds determines whether they stay in defensive mode or get disarmed.**

---

## The Role of Connecting Questions

Connecting questions are the tool that shifts a prospect from defensive to open. They do one thing above all else:

> **Take the focus off you (the salesperson) and put it entirely on them (the prospect).**

When done correctly, connecting questions disarm fight mode prospects before they escalate — and prevent flight mode prospects from disengaging before the conversation even starts.

The connecting stage is not small talk. It is the intentional, skilled process of making the prospect feel like this call is about *them* — not about a sale.

---

## Key Principles from This Module

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Disarm every 30 seconds, not just at the start</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Sales resistance can be triggered at any point — even at the close</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>7–12 seconds sets the tone</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Your opening determines which mode the prospect enters</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Neediness triggers defensiveness</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>If they sense you need the sale, they protect themselves</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Fight mode escalates fast</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>If you don't disarm in 3–5 seconds, it compounds — and the call is lost</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Flight mode is harder to detect</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>They say yes but mean no — you only find out when they ghost you</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Connecting questions are the solution</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>They shift the focus to the prospect and create the safety for them to open up</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Think back to your last 3 calls that went cold — were those prospects in fight mode or flight mode?
    
-   \[ \] Identify what you said in the first 10 seconds of those calls — what might have triggered it?
    
-   \[ \] Start observing your tonality and energy at the very start of calls — are you coming across as relaxed and confident, or needy and attached?
    
-   \[ \] Prepare for the next module: connecting questions — what you actually say to disarm the prospect from word one

# Breakdown of the Engagement Stage

---

## Overview

The Engagement Stage is where the sale is actually made — emotionally, in the prospect's mind. Before you can ask about their problems, you have to understand *why* people resist opening up in the first place — and how the stages of engagement systematically remove that resistance.

---

## The Paradox at the Core of All Selling

> *"All selling is change. But human beings don't like change."*

This is the fundamental tension every salesperson works within. Prospects say they want to change — they booked the call, they're on the line — but deep down, change feels unknown, unsafe, and uncomfortable. Especially when it's being initiated by someone they suspect is trying to sell them something.

The result: prospects become **comfortable with being uncomfortable.** They'd rather stay in a bad situation they know than risk a change they don't trust yet.

**Your job is to make it safe enough for them to open up — continuously, throughout the entire conversation.**

---

## Why Prospects Don't Tell You Their Real Problems

When prospects give you surface-level answers or two-word responses, it's not because they're difficult. It's because they haven't been disarmed enough to trust you with what's really going on.

> *"They feel like if they open up, they'll get sold. Nobody wants to be sold."*

The first problem a prospect tells you is almost never the real problem. **90% of the time it's a surface-level answer** — a test to see if you're safe to talk to. You cannot take it at face value and run with it. If you do, you've built your entire sales process on a false foundation.

They haven't earned enough trust to go deeper yet. Your questions have to create that trust first.

---

## The Three Stages of the Engagement Process

### 1\. Connecting Stage

Gets the prospect curious enough to engage. Takes the focus off you and puts it on them. Creates the initial disarm that opens the door to everything else.

### 2\. Situation Questions

Helps the prospect understand their own current situation — not just you understanding it. This is where the emotional groundwork is laid. The sale is made here, in their mind.

### 3\. Problem Awareness → Solution Awareness

Helps them see the gap between where they are and where they want to be. Creates urgency from the inside out — through their own conclusions, not your pitch.

---

## The Three Levels of Sales Skill

This is one of the most important distinctions in the entire training.

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Level</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>What They Can Do</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Good salespeople</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Help the prospect identify what their problems are</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Great salespeople</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Help them see the problems AND uncover the root cause — how did this problem happen?</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>The very best</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Do all of the above AND help them feel how the problem is affecting them personally</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

**Why the third level matters most:**

Surface problem → *"I can't get enough listings."* Root cause → *"I haven't built a personal brand or marketing system."* How it's affecting them → *Less clients → less money → stress at home → health issues → can't fund their kids' future → feels stuck and trapped*

The deeper you go with them — through skilled questions, not assumptions — the more urgency they feel. You don't tell them those things. Your questions help them discover it themselves.

> *"When you tell them, it goes in one ear and out the other. When your questions allow them to tell themselves — it's the gospel. They persuade themselves."*

---

## The Over-Probing Trap

More probing is not always better. The common mistake is **probing randomly** — chasing tangents that don't build toward anything.

**Effective probing:**

-   Follows a logical thread that deepens the felt impact of a specific problem
    
-   Connects the surface problem → root cause → personal consequence
    
-   Stops when you've built enough emotion to move forward — not when you've exhausted every possible direction
    

**Ineffective probing:**

-   Jumping to the first problem mentioned without checking if it's the real one
    
-   Probing on topics that don't connect to the rest of the conversation
    
-   Going so deep into one tangent that you lose the thread of the whole call
    

---

## The Sales Consistency Formula

One of the clearest signs of skill level: **consistency month over month.**

Salespeople who go up one month and down the next are missing one or more of three things:

1.  **Commitment**
    
2.  **Discipline**
    
3.  **Skill level**
    

Combine all three — and results become consistent and compounding. Kobe Bryant didn't score 30 points one month and 5 the next. Consistency is the proof of mastery.

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>All selling is change — and people fear change</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Your entire approach must account for this resistance at every step</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>The first problem is usually not the real problem</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Probe past surface answers — trust hasn't been built yet</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Good finds problems; great finds root cause; best finds emotional impact</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The deepest level is where urgency lives</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Questions cause self-discovery</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Telling triggers resistance; questioning triggers persuasion</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Disarming is continuous</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Not just at the start — every 30 seconds, all the way to close</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Consistent results = commitment + discipline + skill</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Up-and-down months signal a gap in one of these three</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] For your top 3 products or client situations — map out: surface problem → root cause → how it affects them personally (go 3–4 layers deep)
    
-   \[ \] In your next call, when a prospect gives you their first problem — don't build on it yet. Ask one more question to see if there's something deeper underneath
    
-   \[ \] Practice the three-level framework out loud: *"What's the problem? What caused it? How is it affecting them?"*
    
-   \[ \] Review your last three months of results — are they consistent? If not, identify which of the three (commitment, discipline, skill) is the weakest link
