# Connecting Stage Role-Play: Inbound Leads Breakdown

---

## Overview

This session uses live role-play to break down exactly what the connecting stage looks and sounds like in practice — what works, what triggers fight or flight, and the specific language choices that make the difference between a prospect who opens up and one who ghosts you.

---

## The Overarching Theme: Always Be Disarming

> *"It's not just the opening. You're disarming the prospect every 30 seconds — all the way through. Even at the close. Even when they're walking to get their credit card."*

Objection prevention — not objection handling — is the goal. The more consistently you disarm throughout the call, the fewer objections surface at the end. Most sales burnout comes from constantly fighting or chasing prospects who were never truly open. Disarming prevents that.

---

## Key Language Principles from the Role-Play

### 1\. You're Selling Results — Not the Product

> *"You're not selling yield farming. Nobody cares about yield farming. You're selling what yield farming does for them — bigger returns, stability, time freedom."*

Whatever you sell, the same rule applies. Lead with the result, not the mechanism.

**Wrong:** "Booked a call about yield farming" **Right:** "Booked a call about possibly getting a higher rate of return with yield farming"

The word **"possibly"** is critical — it's neutral, non-guaranteeing, and non-pressuring.

---

### 2\. Frame the Call Correctly — Without Triggering Flight Mode

**Wrong framing (triggers flight):**

> *"At the end of this call it's going to be a yes or a no — either way is fine."*

This sounds reasonable but immediately puts the prospect in defensive mode. They agree on the surface but shut down emotionally — and you get *"I need to think it over"* at the end.

**Right framing:**

> *"The first part of this call is really more for us to find out more about what you've been doing and what you're looking for — just to see if we can actually help. Because there's just some people there's just not much we can do for, you know what I mean by that?"*

**Why it works:**

-   *"For us"* instead of *"for me"* — collaborative, not interrogative
    
-   Admitting you might not be able to help them immediately positions you as an advisor, not a salesperson
    
-   *"You know what I mean by that?"* — delivered as a statement, not a question — gets automatic agreement and further disarms
    
-   Their internal reaction: *"I hope this works for me"* — they're now leaning in, not pulling away
    

**One call close vs. two call close:**

-   One call: *"The first part of this call…"*
    
-   Two call: *"The first call…"*
    

---

### 3\. Watch Your Word Choices — They Trigger Fight or Flight

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Word/Phrase</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Effect</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Print more money"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Triggers skepticism — sounds too good to be true</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Make more money"</em> or <em>"higher rate of return"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Neutral, believable, safe</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Me"</em> or <em>"I"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Puts focus on you — signals it's about the sale</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Us"</em> or <em>"we"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Collaborative — signals it's about them</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"I'm so sorry"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Demotes your status — signals desperation</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"I apologize, I didn't mean to offend you"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Disarms without losing status</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Thank you for your patience"</em> (when late)</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Maintains status + disarms; far better than "I'm so sorry I'm late"</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

### 4\. "You Know What I Mean By That?" — The Disarming Statement

This phrase is delivered as a casual statement, not a genuine question. The prospect automatically agrees — and in agreeing, they're not thinking *"maybe I shouldn't buy"*, they're thinking *"I hope this works for me."*

It's a micro-disarm that resets the tone and keeps them engaged.

---

### 5\. Always Acknowledge What They Say Before Moving On

When a prospect says *"I just want to know how it works and what the cost is"* — you cannot ignore that and jump to your next question. They'll notice. It triggers flight.

**Right response:**

> *"Oh yeah, for sure — we'll go through all of that."*

Short, natural, disarming. Then move on. That brief acknowledgment keeps them feeling heard and prevents them from mentally flagging the concern throughout the rest of the call.

---

### 6\. Status: Start Equal, Finish as the Expert

At the start of a call, you and the prospect are on level ground in their mind. The goal throughout the call is to gradually rise to the level of trusted expert — through your questions, your calm, and your knowledge — so that by the end, they want to buy from someone who clearly knows what they're doing.

**What drops your status instantly:**

-   *"I'm so sorry"* — apologetic, desperate
    
-   Sounding scripted or reading off a wall
    
-   Being reactive to their pushback instead of calm and grounded
    

**What maintains and builds status:**

-   *"I apologize, I didn't mean to offend you"* — confident, disarming, not groveling
    
-   *"Thank you for your patience"* — gracious without shrinking
    
-   Leaning forward while they talk — shows genuine interest
    
-   Calm, curious tonality even when you know exactly what you're asking
    

---

### 7\. On Inbound Calls — Never Ask These Questions

> *"Have you found what you're looking for already?"* *"Is now still a good time?"*

Both are irrelevant on inbound calls. They booked the time — they chose it. Asking these questions signals low confidence and wastes the opening. Save *"Is now a good time?"* for outbound only.

---

### 8\. Body Language Affects Tonality — Even on Phone

Leaning forward while the prospect speaks affects your tone of voice — even when they can't see you. Sitting slouched creates a flat, disengaged delivery. Sitting upright and leaning in creates energy and curiosity in your voice.

**The acting analogy:** Megan Fox and Adam Sandler deliver scripted lines — but they don't sound scripted because they've rehearsed until it becomes natural. Same standard applies to your questions. Memorize them. Then deliver them like you're having a conversation, not reading a script.

---

## Role-Play Feedback Framework

When reviewing your own calls or a colleague's, evaluate on two dimensions:

**What's working:**

-   Does it sound conversational or scripted?
    
-   Is the prospect being disarmed or put on guard?
    
-   Is the language collaborative ("us/we") or salesperson-focused ("I/me")?
    
-   Is the framing creating curiosity or triggering fight/flight?
    

**What to improve:**

-   Any word that sounds too promotional or "salesy"
    
-   Any moment where status was dropped unnecessarily
    
-   Any prospect response that was ignored instead of acknowledged
    
-   Reading off a script or wall instead of speaking naturally
    

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Sell the result, not the product</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Nobody buys the mechanism — they buy what it does for them</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Frame the call as "for us"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Collaborative framing keeps the prospect engaged</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Never frame as yes-or-no upfront</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>It triggers flight mode — you lose them emotionally before you start</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Possibly" neutralizes pressure</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Non-committal language keeps the prospect safe enough to open up</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Acknowledge before pivoting</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Ignoring what they say triggers flight — a brief acknowledgment disarms</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Status starts equal, ends expert</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Earn the authority through your questions, not by claiming it</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Leaning in affects tonality</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Physical posture shapes vocal energy — even on audio-only calls</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Disarming is continuous</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>It never stops — every 30 seconds, all the way to the close</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Review the framing you currently use at the start of your calls — does it include a yes-or-no statement? Replace it with the "first part of this call is really for us to…" structure
    
-   \[ \] Audit your language for "me/I" vs. "us/we" — make the shift
    
-   \[ \] Remove "Is now still a good time?" from all inbound call openers
    
-   \[ \] Practice saying *"I apologize, I didn't mean to offend you"* instead of *"I'm sorry"* when a prospect pushes back early
    
-   \[ \] Record yourself doing the opening of a call — identify any moment where you sound scripted, rushed, or low-status
    
-   \[ \] Memorize your connecting questions until you can deliver them leaning forward, naturally, as if it's a conversation with a friend

---

# Connecting Stage Role-Play: Outbound Calls & No-Show Follow-Ups

---

## Overview

This session covers outbound call scenarios — specifically how to re-engage prospects who didn't show up for a scheduled appointment, and how to handle follow-up sequences when people go quiet after a call. The same disarming principles apply, but the framing and language shift for outbound.

---

## Part 1 — Pre-Call Setup: The DM Strategy (Before They Even Pick Up)

The best outbound calls are easier because you set the right expectations *before* the call happens — in the DMs or booking confirmation messages.

**What works in booking confirmations:**

> *"Should we let you book with one of our team members to go over the details and see if we can help?"*

The phrase **"should we let you"** frames the call as a favor you're doing them — not a sales call they're doing you. Combined with social proof (testimonials, results they've seen), prospects arrive emotionally halfway there before you even open your mouth.

**The show-up commitment trigger:**

> *"\[Name\] will take good care of you — now if you don't show up, the team might have to kick you to the curb 😄"*

Delivered with humor and a smiley face — not as a threat. What it actually does: gets them to respond with *"don't worry, I'll be there!"* — they've now made a verbal commitment. Show-up rates increase significantly.

**What kills show-up rates:**

> *"Just book a call — what's the worst that can happen? You don't have to buy."*

This sets zero psychological or emotional expectation. There's no reason for them to show up. Don't ever frame it this way.

---

## Part 2 — Calling No-Shows

### Calling Priority: Always Call the Most Recent No-Shows First

If someone missed yesterday, call them today — not next week. Recency dramatically increases the quality and success rate of the outreach.

---

### Opening the No-Show Call

**Avoid:** *"The reason for my call today is…"* This phrase is a trigger word associated with sales calls. Even if your methodology is different, they'll immediately pattern-match to every cold call they've ever received.

**Use instead — be specific and natural:**

> *"Hey Road — looks like you had booked on the calendar with Bill Walsh about possibly learning how to sell more of your products and services. Bill asked me to reach out because you didn't show up yesterday — what the heck happened?"*

With salespeople (who your DM already playfully warned about no-showing), you can add humor:

> *"Jeremy's going to smack you around over there — what happened?"*

This works *specifically* because you primed them in the DM. In other industries or with senior executives (C-suite, corporate), don't use this approach — be more professional.

---

### Avoid These Status-Dropping Phrases

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Phrase</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Problem</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"I know I caught you at a bad time…"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Immediately demotes your status — signals you're beneath them</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"I'm so sorry"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Shrinks your authority — they pounce</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"I understand, things happen…"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Filler — sounds like every other salesperson</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"When can we reschedule?"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Puts you in chasing mode</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

**Instead — maintain status with calendar control:**

> *"Yeah, not a problem — I'm completely booked the rest of today and tomorrow. The soonest I'd be available for you would be Thursday afternoon or Friday morning. Would one of those work?"*

This communicates: *your time is valuable too.* You're doing them a favor by fitting them in — not chasing them for another shot.

---

### What to Ask After Reconnecting

Once you've acknowledged the missed call:

> *"Are you still looking at how to sell more — or have you found what you're looking for?"*

Open-ended. Non-pressuring. Gets them talking without triggering fight or flight.

---

## Part 3 — The Follow-Up Email / DM Sequence (After a No-Response)

When a prospect ghosts after a call — don't send long emails. Nobody reads them. Use a three-touch sequence, short and direct.

### Touch 1 (Same day or next day):

> *"Hey John — just left you a message. I'm available here for a bit if you'd like to reach me. \[Number\]"*

### Touch 2 (Following day):

> *"Hey John — left you a second message, didn't hear back yesterday. I'm available here for a bit today if you'd like to reach me."*

### Touch 3 (Day after — the pattern interrupt):

> *"Hey John — left you two messages but didn't hear back. How do you want to proceed from here?"*

**Why Touch 3 works:** A short, direct question demands a short, direct answer. 80–90% of people respond to this — often with a real reason (*"I'm so sorry, I had a family emergency"*) that you can now actually address. Long emails get ignored. A five-word question gets answered.

### Touch 4 (Final attempt if still no response):

> *"Hey John — tried to reach you a few times but never heard back. This will be my last attempt. If you'd like to reach me: \[number\]."*

---

## Key Language Principles from This Session

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Application</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Set expectations before the call</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>DM framing creates emotional investment before they even pick up</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Humor works with the right demographic</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Salespeople respond to playful pressure; executives don't</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Avoid "the reason for my call"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>It's a trigger phrase — sounds like every cold call they've ever received</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Soonest I'd be available for you"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Signals your time is valuable — you're not chasing them</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Short follow-ups &gt; long emails</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Nobody reads paragraphs; everyone answers a direct question</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"How do you want to proceed from here?"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Surfaces the real reason for silence on the third touch</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Status Dynamics — Outbound Context

At the start of any outbound call:

-   You and the prospect start at **equal status**
    
-   Saying *"I'm so sorry"* or *"I know I caught you at a bad time"* instantly drops you **below** their status
    
-   Saying *"the soonest I'd be available for you"* keeps you **at or above** their level
    

People buy from experts. They don't buy from people who are beneath them in the interaction. Maintain your status through calm, confident, non-chasing language — especially in the first 30 seconds.

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Call no-shows the same or next day</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Recency dramatically improves response and re-engagement</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Avoid trigger phrases</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"The reason for my call" = immediate sales resistance</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Frame callbacks as a favor</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"The soonest I'd be available" positions you as in-demand</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Three-touch email sequence</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Short, specific, escalating — culminates in "how do you want to proceed?"</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Pre-call DM setup changes everything</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Emotional investment and show-up commitment happen before the call starts</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Review your current booking confirmation messages — do they frame the call as a favor or as a generic appointment?
    
-   \[ \] Add the playful commitment line (*"if you don't show up we might have to kick you to the curb 😄"*) to your booking confirmations and track show-up rate
    
-   \[ \] Replace *"the reason for my call"* with a specific, natural reference to what they booked for
    
-   \[ \] Build your three-touch email sequence — short, direct, and culminating in *"how do you want to proceed from here?"*
    
-   \[ \] Practice the no-availability response: *"I'm booked the rest of today and tomorrow — the soonest I'd be available would be \[day\]"* until it sounds natural

# Situation Questions Role-Play: Finding the Current State

---

## Overview

Situation questions are the first stage of the engagement process. Their job is simple: find out the prospect's **current state** — who they are, what they're doing now, and what's actually going on in their world. This sets up everything that follows in problem awareness and solution awareness.

---

## Where Situation Questions Fit in the Process

```
Connecting → Situation Questions → Problem Awareness → Solution Awareness → Consequence → Close
```

> *"The sale is not made in connecting. It's not made at closing. It's made right here — in problem awareness and solution awareness. But situation questions give you permission to get there."*

Situation questions are **fact-gathering** — not emotion-pulling. The heavy emotional work happens in problem awareness and beyond. Don't confuse the two.

---

## The Key Rules for Situation Questions

### 1\. Don't Stay in Connecting Too Long

Connecting questions are meant to open the door — not become the whole conversation. **60–90 seconds maximum.** Then transition.

Common mistakes in connecting:

-   Talking about the weather
    
-   Asking *"How are you doing today?"* — sounds like every other salesperson
    
-   Letting the prospect waffle and going down rabbit holes for 5+ minutes
    

> Every salesperson asks "how are you doing today?" — the moment you say it, you're grouped with everyone else they've ever wanted to get off the phone with.

---

### 2\. Keep Situation Questions to 3–4 Maximum

Any more and it starts to feel like an interrogation. In complex B2B environments you might stretch to 5–6, but only if the questions flow conversationally. The moment it sounds scripted or clinical:

→ Fight mode: *"Can you just get to the point?"* → Flight mode: They give two-word answers and disengage

---

### 3\. The Tone: Curious, Not Skeptical

Situation questions are not the place for skepticism. That comes later in problem awareness. Here you want the tone of a **confused old man at a gas station** — genuinely curious, just trying to understand, no agenda.

> *"You're curious and confused. You're just trying to figure out what's going on. Not interrogating — exploring."*

Skepticism signals judgment. Curiosity signals genuine interest. In situation questions, you need curiosity.

---

### 4\. Control the Conversation — Don't Let Them Waffle

If the prospect starts going off on tangents:

-   Acknowledge briefly
    
-   Ask a specific follow-up to redirect
    
-   Keep moving through the situation picture you need to build
    

They hired you (as the expert) to run the call — not to follow wherever their story takes them.

---

### 5\. Clarify Vague Answers Before Moving On

When a prospect uses a general term like *"cold calling"* — don't assume you know what they mean.

> *"How do you mean by cold calling? Because it sometimes means different things to different people."*

This does two things:

-   Gets you accurate information
    
-   Causes the prospect to start thinking about their own situation more clearly than they ever have — because nobody ever asked
    

> *"Your questions help them figure out what their current situation actually is. Most of the time they're just in it every day — they're not stepping back and seeing it clearly."*

---

### 6\. Use "We" Instead of "I" Where Possible

*"Just so I understand"* → *"Just so we understand"*

Small language shift. Makes the conversation feel collaborative rather than one-sided.

---

## The Purpose: Building the Gap

Situation questions are the foundation. They establish where the prospect is **now** — their current state. Everything after this builds the gap between where they are and where they want to be.

**The bigger the gap your questions help them see → the more urgency they feel → the smaller your price looks → the fewer objections you get.**

> *"If the gap is massive in their mind — it's very hard for them to say 'I need to think it over.' When the gap is that big, the reaction is: I needed to do this yesterday."*

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Gap Size</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Prospect Reaction</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Large gap (problems fully felt)</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"When can we start?"</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Medium gap</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Mild hesitation, some objections</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Small gap (problems barely surfaced)</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"$15,000? That's a lot. Let me think about it."</em></p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Verbal Technique: The Pause

When asking situation questions, use verbal pauses (*"um"*, *"I guess"*) to slow yourself down and sound more natural.

> *"What were you hoping to get out of the call with us — just so we have a better understanding?"*

The pause also signals you're thinking, not reading from a script.

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Situation questions = facts, not emotion</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Save emotional probing for problem awareness and beyond</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>3–4 questions max</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>More feels like an interrogation</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Curious, not skeptical</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Curiosity opens; skepticism closes</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Clarify vague terms</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"Cold calling" means something different to everyone</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Control the conversation</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Don't let them waffle — redirect with a specific question</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"We" not "I"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Collaborative language keeps them in dialogue mode</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Situation questions build the foundation for the gap</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>No current state = no gap = no urgency</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Time your next connecting stage — if you're spending more than 90 seconds there, you're staying too long
    
-   \[ \] Remove *"How are you doing today?"* and *"How's the weather?"* from your opening permanently
    
-   \[ \] Identify the 3 most important situation questions for your prospect — the ones that tell you the most about their current state
    
-   \[ \] Practice clarifying vague terms: *"How do you mean by \[term\]? Because it can mean different things to different people"*
    
-   \[ \] Record a call and count your situation questions — are you staying under 4? Are they conversational or interrogative?

# Situation Questions Role-Play Part 2: Adapting to Different Prospect Types

---

## Overview

This session goes deeper into situation questions through live role-play — covering how to adapt your questions based on who the prospect is (business owner, employee, retired), how to financially qualify without directly asking about money, and how to rescue a conversation when someone is in flight mode.

---

## The Core Reminder: Preparation Beats Hopium

> *"Hopium is a drug that most salespeople take — where they hope and pray the prospect won't bring up an objection, or hope that something they say will magically get them to buy. You never want to be on hopium."*

You hear the same things from prospects every day. Every week. Every month. If you're still not prepared to handle them — you're winging it. And winging it keeps you average.

**Preparation means:** knowing exactly what to do no matter which direction the prospect takes the conversation — north, south, east, or west.

---

## The "Confused Old Man" Technique — Applied to Situation Questions

The confused old man energy isn't just for opening questions. It applies throughout situation questions.

When they say something vague:

> *"Oh — like a gym, or what do you mean by a fitness business?"*

When they use a term that could mean multiple things:

> *"How do you mean by cold calling? Because that can mean different things to different people."*

**Why it works:** A confused person asking for help triggers human nature to want to help. They lean in. They pull you in rather than you pushing them. That's disarming — and it's genuine, not manipulative.

> *"If I show confusion and empathy, they feel: this person is really here to see if they can help me. And people open up to that."*

---

## Adapting Situation Questions to Prospect Type

The same situation questions don't work for everyone. Your questions need to shift based on who you're talking to.

### Business Owner

Key questions:

-   *"What type of business do you have?"*
    
-   *"How long have you been running it?"*
    
-   *"Are you on your own or do you have a family you're taking care of?"*
    
-   *"What does your spouse do for work?"* *(indirect financial qualification)*
    

### Employee / Job-Holder

-   *"What do you do for work?"*
    
-   *"How long have you been doing that?"*
    
-   *"Are you on your own or do you have a family you're taking care of?"*
    

### Retired Prospect

**If recently retired (under 6 months):** Focus on what they did before — they're still emotionally attached to their career identity.

-   *"What did you used to do for a living?"*
    
-   *"How long did you do that for?"*
    

**If retired for several years:** Focus on the retirement itself — that's their current reality.

-   *"Do you like being retired?"* → then probe what they like or don't like
    

> **Stability signal:** If they held a steady job for 20+ years, that signals financial stability. If they had a series of short odd jobs, that signals potential financial instability — and you need to qualify them more carefully before investing time.

---

## Financial Qualification Without Asking Directly

You need to understand their financial picture — but you can't just ask *"how much do you make?"*

**The indirect approach:**

> *"Are you on your own or do you have like a family you're taking care of?"* → *"I'm married."* *"And what does your spouse do for work?"*

**Why the framing matters:**

❌ *"Are you married? What does your wife do for a living?"* — Sounds nosy. Can trigger *"Why does that matter?"*

✅ *"Are you on your own, or do you have like a family you're taking care of?"* — Feels natural. Nobody pushes back on this. It's the confused old man again — just trying to understand the picture.

---

## Don't Ask Questions That Don't Serve the Sale

One clear mistake: asking *"What caused you to start the gym?"* when it doesn't connect to anything downstream.

If the answer doesn't move you closer to understanding their problem or qualifying them — don't ask it. Every question in the situation stage should have a purpose:

-   Understand their current state
    
-   Financially qualify them
    
-   Set up the gap you'll build in problem awareness
    

Random curiosity questions break the flow and can accidentally trigger defensive responses.

---

## "I Just Want Passive Income" — What It Actually Means

Prospects will often put up a tough front:

-   *"I'm just looking to diversify my portfolio."*
    
-   *"I just want some passive income."*
    
-   *"I'm always looking for more ways to make money."*
    

**Translation:** They want to make more money. That's it.

Nobody books a 30–45 minute call with a company they've never heard of just to *add to their portfolio*. They booked because something isn't working. Your job is to find out what.

When they give you the generic answer, probe past it:

> *"More money — why is that so important to you now though?"* → *"It's always important."* *"Sure, I mean everybody wants that of course — but specifically for you, if you really thought about it, why is it important for you right now?"*

Delivered with calm empathy — not pressure. You're not challenging them aggressively. You're genuinely curious about *why*, because the generic answer tells you nothing useful.

---

## Rescuing a Flight-Mode Prospect Mid-Situation

If you didn't disarm well enough in connecting, the prospect may give flat two-word answers in situation questions. Don't panic. You can bring them back.

**The rescue move:**

> *"Yeah — I mean everybody wants to make more money, of course. But specifically for you — if you really thought about it — why is it important for you?"*

Said with empathy, leaning in slightly, genuine curiosity in your tone. You're not repeating yourself robotically — you're shifting your energy to pull them in.

> *"I need to take them from flight mode back to disarmed. And I can do that mid-conversation if I know what I'm doing."*

---

## Question-Answer-Question vs. Conversational Flow

A common mistake: making situation questions feel like a checklist.

**Robotic pattern:** Question → Answer → Question → Answer → Question...

The prospect feels interrogated. Two-word answers follow.

**Conversational pattern:** Question → Answer → Natural reaction to their answer → Clarify if needed → Next question that flows from what they just said

The difference is reacting to what they say — not just moving to the next item on your list.

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Adapt questions to prospect type</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Business owner, employee, and retired prospects all need different situation questions</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Financially qualify indirectly</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Ask about family and spouse's work — not income directly</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Confused old man = disarming</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Genuine confusion triggers the prospect's desire to help and open up</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Don't ask purposeless questions</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Every situation question should serve discovery or qualification</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Passive income" = they want more money</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Cut through surface positioning and focus on the real motivation</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Probe past generic answers</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Why is that important to you specifically — right now?"</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Rescue flight mode mid-call</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Empathetic re-engagement pulls them back — repetition with a different energy</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Preparation eliminates hopium</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Know every direction the call can go — and know what to do in each one</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Map out your situation questions for each prospect type you commonly encounter — business owner, employee, retired — and make sure they're different
    
-   \[ \] Identify how you currently ask about financial situation — is it direct or indirect? Rewrite it using the family/spouse framing
    
-   \[ \] Practice the *"why is that important to you specifically — right now?"* probe until it sounds natural, not pushy
    
-   \[ \] Record a situation question sequence — count how many questions you're asking, and check whether they flow conversationally or feel like a checklist
    
-   \[ \] Remove any situation question from your script that doesn't directly serve discovery or qualification

# Problem Awareness Questions Role-Play: Getting to the Emotion

---

## Overview

This session uses live role-play to break down the problem awareness stage — the most emotionally critical part of the sales process. Situation questions establish where the prospect is now. Problem awareness questions help them *feel* how bad that situation actually is.

---

## Script Fixes Before We Start: What to Remove

Before even getting to problem awareness, two common script mistakes were identified that trigger fight or flight before the call even gets going:

**1\. Announcing the call length:**

> *"We've carved out a 40-minute slot for you today."*

There is no upside to saying this. If the prospect is busy, they immediately think *"I didn't know it was going to be 40 minutes — can I reschedule?"* If they're engaged, the call will run as long as it needs to. Remove it.

**2\. "Is now still a good time?" on inbound calls:** They booked the time. They chose it. Asking this opens the door for them to say no — and there's no upside. Save it for outbound only.

> *"There's no upside in saying those things — only downside."*

---

## The Purpose of Problem Awareness Questions

Situation questions gather facts. Problem awareness questions gather **emotion**.

The goal: help the prospect feel the full weight of their problem — the problem itself, the root cause, and how it's affecting them personally. The deeper they feel it, the larger the gap becomes. The larger the gap, the more urgency they feel to change now.

> *"The sale is made right here — in problem awareness and solution awareness. Not in connecting, not at closing. Here."*

---

## The "Do You Like..." Entry Point

The standard entry into problem awareness is a simple question that assesses how the prospect feels about their current situation:

> *"With what you're currently doing — do you like the results you've been getting?"*

or:

> *"Do you like what you have in place right now?"*

**Critical rule:** Include what they already told you they don't like before asking this question.

**Wrong:**

> *"So do you like the results you've been getting?"* *(They just told you the diet is hard to stick to — you sound like you weren't listening.)*

**Right:**

> *"So besides it being hard to stick to those diets — do you like the results you've been getting?"*

This small change does something powerful: it disarms them. They can't say *"I just told you"* because you already acknowledged it. They simply respond to the question.

---

## The Two Truths Problem Awareness Version

When they say things are going *okay* or *a little better*:

> *"To me it sounds like things are going fairly well — is there anything you would change about the results you've been getting, if you could?"*

The verbal pause at the end (*"if you could"*) is essential. It softens the question and almost always surfaces something they'd want different.

When they give you that something — probe it immediately:

> *"What do you mean by that? That means different things to different people."* *"Walk me through what you mean by a little better."*

---

## The 1–10 Scale (With Caution)

A scale question can work to quantify pain:

> *"On a scale of 1 to 10 — 1 being no pain at all, 10 being extreme — where would you say you are on a given day?"*

**But avoid purely logical scale questions like:**

> *"On a scale of 1 to 10, what positive results have come from your treatment?"*

This generates only a number — no emotion. The goal is to surface feeling, not data.

When they give you a range (*"a 4 to 7 depending on the day"*) → follow immediately with:

> *"And how long has that been going on?"* *"Has that had an impact on you — in what way though?"*

---

## The "Push the Gas" Principle — And When to Let Off

Problem awareness is where you push the gas on emotion. Each question should build on the last, taking the prospect deeper into feeling the impact of their problem.

**The common mistake:** Getting to a certain emotional depth and then releasing — moving to the next scripted question before the pain has been fully felt.

> *"You're getting them to here, here, here — and then you let off the gas and they start going back down. You've got to keep pushing a little more."*

**But there's a fine line.** Over-probing is real. If you've already established that they don't want to keep living with the problem and why — don't ask the same question a different way. That feels dismissive, like you weren't listening. It triggers defensiveness.

**The rule:** Push the gas until the emotion is genuinely present. Then read their body language and tonality to know when to move on.

---

## The Rationale Question: Preventing the "I'll Figure It Out Myself" Objection

After establishing the problem, use the rationale question to get them to verbalize why they can't keep doing what they've been doing:

> *"Besides wanting to \[repeat their goal\] — what's the main reason you're looking for outside help from \[solution/expert\] rather than just figuring it out on your own?"*

**Why this matters:** If they tell you why they can't keep doing it themselves, they can't give you that objection later. They've already eliminated it — out of their own mouth.

**What to add if they're still on the fence:**

> *"So — time to make a change, possibly?"*

If they're still uncertain:

> *"What if you don't though?"*

This mini-consequence question forces them to see what staying in status quo actually means.

---

## Slowing Down the Probing Questions

A fast question gets a knee-jerk answer. A slow question forces genuine reflection.

**Too fast:**

> *"How long has that been going on? Has that had an impact on you?"*

**Right pace:**

> *"And how long has that... been going on for?"* *(pause)* *"Has that... had an impact on you?"*

The pause between questions forces the brain to process — and processing leads to deeper, more emotional answers.

---

## Pacing Responses: Have 10–12 Memorized

When the prospect is talking, your vocal reactions signal engagement. But repeating the same word (*"sure... sure... sure"*) starts to sound like a tactic — not genuine listening.

Build a varied set of natural pacing responses:

-   *"Uh-huh..."*
    
-   *"Hmm."*
    
-   *"Really..."*
    
-   *"Interesting."*
    
-   *"Oh okay."*
    
-   *"I see."*
    
-   *"Got it."*
    

Rotate them naturally so no single one becomes a pattern.

---

## Trusted Advisor vs. Being a Dick

There is a clear line between *challenging a prospect's thinking* and coming across as dismissive or superior. Problem awareness requires emotional push — but never at the expense of the relationship.

Signs you've crossed the line:

-   Asking the same probing question twice in different words
    
-   Pushing them to articulate something they've already answered
    
-   Tone that signals judgment rather than genuine concern
    

> *"There's a difference between being a trusted adviser and being a dick. You want them to feel your concern — not your pressure."*

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Include what they said before asking "do you like..."</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Prevents them from feeling unheard — keeps them disarmed</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Push the gas on emotion — but read when to stop</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Depth of pain = depth of urgency; over-probing triggers resistance</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Slow down the probing questions</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Fast questions get knee-jerk answers; slow questions get real ones</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>The rationale question prevents end-of-call objections</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>They can't say "I'll figure it out myself" if they already told you why they can't</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Vary your pacing responses</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Repetition sounds like a script — rotate 10–12 natural reactions</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Never ask the same question twice</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>If they already answered it, probing it again feels dismissive</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Remove all time announcements (*"40-minute slot"*, *"45 minutes"*) and inbound *"is now still a good time?"* from your opening
    
-   \[ \] Rewrite your first problem awareness question to include what the prospect already told you they don't like: *"Besides \[their stated problem\] — do you like the results you've been getting?"*
    
-   \[ \] Build the rationale question for your product: *"Besides wanting \[their goal\] — what's the main reason you're looking for outside help rather than figuring it out on your own?"*
    
-   \[ \] Practice slowing down your probing sequence — insert a genuine pause before *"has that had an impact on you?"*
    
-   \[ \] Write out 10–12 pacing responses and practice rotating through them naturally on your next 5 calls

### 36 Role Play Exercise 6

https://youtu.be/jPUsVKwr8SY

# Solution Awareness Questions Role-Play: Getting Prospects to Envision Their Future State

---

## Overview

Solution awareness questions are the bridge between the prospect's painful current situation and their desired future. This session uses live role-play to break down what works, what to avoid, and how to get prospects to genuinely visualize and commit to the outcome they want.

---

## What Solution Awareness Questions Do

After problem awareness has established the pain, solution awareness questions shift the conversation forward — from *"here's how bad things are"* to *"here's where you want to be."*

The goal: get the prospect to articulate their ideal future state in specific, emotional terms — so that your solution becomes the obvious next step to get them there.

---

## Part 1 — Before the Call Gets Going: Script Fixes

**Never announce the call length:**

> *"We've carved out a 40-minute slot…"*

This has no upside. A busy prospect immediately thinks *"I didn't know it would be this long"* — and you lose them before you've started.

**Never ask "is now still a good time?" on inbound calls.** They booked it. Asking implies doubt. Save it for outbound only.

---

## Part 2 — The Research-Mode Trap

Many prospects say they've been *"just looking"* or *"doing research"* for months or years without taking any action. This is a specific pattern you need to address directly — because if you don't, it will become an objection at the end of the call (*"I just need to do more research"*).

**How to handle it:**

> *"How long have you been in research mode though?"* → *"About a year."* *"Okay — so that seems like a lot of research. What's prevented you from actually doing something so you can change your situation?"*

If they keep defaulting to research as a reason for inaction, use the "have you ever considered" challenge:

> *"Can I make a suggestion? Have you ever considered that always doing research — year after year — without making a decision has prevented you from ever actually starting a business where you could make more money?"* → *"Yeah, probably."* *"How are you going to overcome that way of thinking so you can actually make a decision and start a business where you have more time with your family?"*

**Why this works:** If they tell you themselves that the research pattern has kept them stuck, they can't give you that objection at the end. They've already eliminated it in their own words.

---

## Part 3 — Clarifying Vague Answers in Solution Awareness

When prospects say things like *"I've just been looking on YouTube"* or *"I tried a program before"* — don't accept the vague answer. Probe it.

> *"YouTube — like specifically what were you looking at?"*

This matters because the same phrase means different things to different people. Finding out which companies or content they looked at tells you:

-   Who your competition is
    
-   What they liked and didn't like
    
-   What objections might surface later
    

If they tried a program that didn't work — dig into why before moving on:

> *"What part of that process do you feel didn't work for you?"* *"Were there other people in the program actually getting results?"* *"What do you think held you back from getting the same results?"*

This uncovers the real objection before it becomes an end-of-call problem — and often surfaces what they actually need (e.g., more support, clearer structure) which you can address in your presentation.

**Important:** When they tell you what they needed but didn't get — don't immediately say *"don't worry, we have that."* That sounds like every other salesperson. Instead just acknowledge it:

> *"So the support is important to you then."*

That simple statement implies you have it — without triggering the defensive *"I've heard that before"* reaction.

---

## Part 4 — The Boardroom Visualization Question

One of the most powerful additions to the solution awareness stage:

> *"Let's say we had you come into our office — sitting down in the boardroom with me, you, and \[owner/expert name\] — and we're designing your training specifically for you. What do you feel like you'd need to make sure we get you to where you want to go?"*

**Why it works:**

-   The prospect begins to *visualize* themselves already working with you
    
-   They mentally enter the relationship before any commitment is made
    
-   It's personalized — they're designing their own solution, which means they're more bought in to it
    
-   You're one step ahead of everyone else they've talked to, because nobody else got them this far
    

---

## Part 5 — The Ideal Criteria Question

After establishing what they've tried and what held them back, ask:

> *"Just to make sure what we're doing would actually work for you — besides \[pain or problem they mentioned\], what's your ideal criteria when it comes to \[what you sell\]?"*

Let them answer fully. Then ask:

> *"Is there anything else?"*

Keep going until they've exhausted their criteria. Everything they give you here becomes the roadmap for your presentation.

**Word choice matters:**

-   **Avoid** *"coaching"* — it's overused, and prospects associate it with programs that didn't deliver
    
-   **Use** *"training"* or *"specialized training"* instead
    
-   For experienced prospects: *"advanced training"*
    
-   For complete beginners: just *"training"* — *"advanced"* may feel intimidating or out of reach
    

---

## Part 6 — The Income / Future State Question

Once you've established their criteria, move into their future state — specifically what they want to achieve financially and personally.

> *"Tell me about the income you mentioned — as you know, when you work for someone else, you're often limited in what they'll pay you. If you didn't have that limit, what would your ideal income be — let's say annually?"*

**Calibrating their income goal:**

The prospect needs to believe the number they say is achievable. If they're earning $60K and say they want $1M — they probably don't believe that's real. Don't push it higher; bring it back to something credible:

> *"Yeah, a million is definitely possible — maybe year two or three. What would you realistically want to make in your first year?"*

If they give a number that's the same as what they currently earn combined with their spouse — push gently:

> *"Between what you and your spouse make together right now — how close are you to that number?"* → *"We actually make that already."* *"So you're really saying you want to make that amount on top of what you already make — so closer to \[2x number\]? Is that what you're saying?"*

**The key principle:** The prospect has to want to make meaningfully more than they currently earn — or they won't justify the investment. *"Just replacing my income"* is not enough motivation to spend $15–25K. Help them think bigger — but keep it believable.

---

## Part 7 — If You Interject, Make It About Them

You're allowed to cut into the prospect's monologue — but only to ask a clarifying question about *them*. Never to pivot to yourself or your solution.

**Triggers fight or flight:**

> *\[Prospect talks\] → "Let me tell you what we do for that..."*

**Keeps them disarmed:**

> *\[Prospect talks\] → "Hold on — when you say \[X\], what do you mean by that specifically?"*

The rule: if you're interrupting to talk about you — stop. If you're interrupting to go deeper on them — proceed.

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Challenge the research-mode pattern early</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>If you don't, "I need to do more research" will be your closing objection</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Clarify vague answers about past programs</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Surfaces real objections before they become closing problems</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Never say "we have that" when they name a gap</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Just reflect it back — "so that's important to you" implies it without sounding like a pitch</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>The boardroom visualization question</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Gets them mentally into the relationship before any commitment</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Calibrate income goals to what they believe</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Unrealistic goals create disbelief — credible goals create urgency</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Never interrupt to talk about yourself</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Only interject to go deeper on them</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Avoid "coaching" — use "training"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Overused word triggers association with disappointing past experiences</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Identify whether any of your common prospects are "research-mode" types — build the challenge question for your industry
    
-   \[ \] Rewrite your solution awareness ideal criteria question using the boardroom visualization structure
    
-   \[ \] Review your current language — are you using *"coaching"*? Replace it with *"training"* or *"specialized training"*
    
-   \[ \] Build your income calibration sequence for your product: what's the minimum income goal a prospect needs to justify your price?
    
-   \[ \] Practice the income goal expansion: *"Between you and your spouse — how close are you to that number already?"*
    
-   \[ \] Record yourself in solution awareness and count how many times you interrupt to talk about your solution — eliminate those entirely

### 37 Role Play Exercise 7

https://youtu.be/8B2g4Olmzw4

# Solution Awareness Questions Role-Play Part 2: The Future State & Disarming to the Close

---

## Overview

This session completes the solution awareness stage and introduces several advanced principles — including how to help prospects emotionally attach to their future state, how to avoid over-probing, and why you must continue disarming even with the easiest prospects all the way to the moment the funds hit the bank.

---

## Part 1 — Getting Prospects to Feel Their Future State

After establishing income goals, the next step is connecting those goals to personal meaning — not just logic.

**The sequence:**

> *"Let's say we were able to train you step by step, like we do with our other clients, and we're able to give you the skills to get up to that $250K range — what would you do with the extra money coming in?"*

They'll give you logical answers — college fund, pay off debt, retire from job.

Then:

> *"Being able to actually do those things — rather than just wanting to do them, because those are two different things — what would that do for you personally?"*

And when they answer:

> *"How would your life be different than it is now though?"*

**Why this works:** The first question gets them describing outcomes. The second gets them into feeling the emotional reality of achieving those outcomes. The third takes them even deeper — contrasting the future with their current uncomfortable reality.

---

## Part 2 — The "Like We Do With Our Other Clients" Seed

A subtle but powerful addition to the solution awareness setup:

> *"Let's say we were able to train you step by step — like we do with our other clients..."*

**Why include this:**

-   It plants the belief that this outcome is normal and expected — not a big stretch
    
-   It references social proof without a hard sell
    
-   It reduces skepticism before you even address it
    
-   It begins to disarm them about whether the solution will actually work
    

> *"By saying 'like we do with our other clients,' I'm seeding in their mind that this is just what happens. It makes the outcome feel achievable because everyone else is achieving it."*

---

## Part 3 — Vary Your Probing Language or It Becomes a Tactic

If you use the same probing phrase over and over (*"why is that important to you now?"*, *"in what way?"*, *"how do you mean?"*), the prospect starts to feel like they're being processed — not heard.

**The solution:** Build a varied vocabulary of probing responses so the conversation feels natural.

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Instead of always saying...</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Try...</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"In what way?"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Would you?"</em> (repeat back an emotional word with a rising tone)</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Why is that important to you now?"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Happy?"</em> <em>(one-word emotional echo)</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"How do you mean?"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Different though?"</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Tell me more about that"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Amazing?"</em></p></td></tr></tbody></table>

**The emotional echo technique:** When a prospect uses an emotional word, simply repeat it back — curious, not judgmental — and they'll expand on it automatically.

> *Prospect: "It would just make me feel so free."* *You: "Free?"* *Prospect: "Yeah, because right now I feel trapped in this job..."*

One word. No structure. Pure curiosity. And they go deeper on their own.

> *"If it feels like a tactic, they disengage. If it feels like a conversation between two people working out a solution together — they stay open."*

---

## Part 4 — Listening vs. Making Them Feel Heard

These are two different things.

**Listening** = processing what they say **Making them feel heard** = making them *experience* that you understand

The second requires active body language (leaning in, facial expressions, genuine reactions), vocal pacing responses that match their emotional state, and probing questions that show you not only heard the words — but understood the meaning behind them.

> *"When they feel heard — not just listened to — they open up at a deeper level. That's when the real problems and motivations surface."*

---

## Part 5 — Expanding the Map

By the end of solution awareness, the prospect should feel like they have *more* problems than they realized when they got on the call — not fewer.

> *"I want them to not just feel like they have one problem. By the time I'm done with solution awareness, they might have two or three additional things they didn't even know were connected."*

**Why this matters:**

-   More problems = bigger gap = more urgency = smaller price objection
    
-   It positions you as someone who helped them *see* their situation more clearly than anyone else ever has
    
-   It creates a sense of: *"This person really understands what's going on with me"*
    

Think of the best therapist vs. an average one. The best therapist doesn't just treat the symptom you walked in with — they help you see the pattern underneath. That's why they get paid 10x more.

---

## Part 6 — ALWAYS Run the Full Process, Even With "Easy" Prospects

One of the most important principles in the entire training:

> *"If someone gets on the call and says 'I'm already ready to buy — my best friend told me about you, where do I send the funds?' — do NOT skip the process."*

**Why:** Every time you short-circuit the process, you're handing control of the outcome to them. If something shifts between the call and when they try to pay — their grandmother gets sick, their spouse pushes back, they start second-guessing — you have no foundation built.

**The right response to an "easy" prospect:**

> *"Well, I'm not sure we could even help you yet — I'd have to know a little bit more about what's going on, because there are some people we just can't do much for."*

You bring them back into the process. You build the same foundation as the most skeptical prospect on your calendar.

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Who controls the outcome?</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p></p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Skip the process → Let them stay "easy"</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>They control it</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Run the full process → Build the foundation</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You control it</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

> *"If I run the full process, I make that sale 100% of the time. If I don't — who controls it? They do."*

---

## Part 7 — ABDS: Always Be Disarming

This isn't just an opening strategy. It's the entire game.

> *"It's all about disarming them — from the first word out of your mouth, all the way through problem awareness, presentation, commitment, and even after they've wired the funds and they're past the cancellation period. Every 30 seconds."*

Deals are lost at every stage — including at the very end. You can run a perfect call for 45 minutes and then say one wrong thing at the close that triggers fight or flight. They were getting their credit card. They don't come back.

**The solution:** Never stop disarming. Every question, every transition, every response — run it through the same filter.

*Does this keep them open or does it close them down?*

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Connect outcomes to personal meaning</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Logic starts the conversation; emotion makes the decision</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Like we do with our other clients"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Plants social proof and reduces skepticism without a pitch</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Vary your probing language</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Repetition sounds like a tactic; variation sounds like a conversation</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Emotional echo technique</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>One word repeated back creates deep, natural expansion</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Listening ≠ making them feel heard</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The feeling of being heard is what opens people up</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Expand the map</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>More problems discovered = bigger gap = less price resistance</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Always run the full process</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"Easy" prospects who skip the process ghost you when it matters</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>ABDS — Always Be Disarming</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The job never ends until the funds clear and the cancellation period passes</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Build a list of 10+ varied probing responses so you never repeat the same phrase more than twice in a call
    
-   \[ \] Practice the emotional echo technique in everyday conversations — one word, curious tone, watch what happens
    
-   \[ \] Add *"like we do with our other clients"* to your solution awareness setup and observe how it lands
    
-   \[ \] Write out the personal meaning questions: *"What would that do for you personally?"* and *"How would your life be different than it is now?"*
    
-   \[ \] Commit to running the full process on every call — including the easiest ones
    
-   \[ \] Review your last 3 "near-close" situations where the deal fell apart at the end — identify the moment disarming stopped

### 38 Role Play Exercise 8

https://youtu.be/9NHILHyI3UA

# Consequence Questions Role-Play: Jerking Away the Future State

---

## Overview

Consequence questions come after solution awareness — right when the prospect is on an emotional high about where they want to be. The job of consequence questions is to jerk that vision away temporarily, forcing them to defend why they need to change *now* rather than later.

> *"We've gotten them to a place where they're feeling it — they'd feel amazing, they'd have more time, they'd send their kids to college. Now we take that away and make them fight for it."*

---

## Where Consequence Questions Fit

```
Situation → Problem Awareness → Solution Awareness → [Emotional High] → Consequence Questions → Transition to Presentation
```

The prospect is at their peak emotional investment. Consequence questions use that investment to build urgency — by making them articulate what happens if they do *nothing*.

This applies in every selling environment — B2C one-call close, B2B multi-call — the goal just shifts:

-   **One-call close:** Close on the call
    
-   **Multi-call / B2B:** Get them to commit to a demo, proposal, or second call
    

It's always about disarming them enough to take the next step.

---

## The Core Consequence Question Framework

### The Standard Opening

> *"So — time to make a change, possibly?"* → *"Yeah, definitely."* *"Okay — but what happens if you don't do anything about \[their specific problem\] and things stay the same over the next 3, 6, or even 12 months?"*

**Key:** Fill in their specific problem — not a vague "the situation." The more specific and visual you make it, the more real it becomes in their mind.

**Wrong:**

> *"What happens if you don't do anything and things get worse?"*

**Right:**

> *"What happens if you don't do anything about growing your following and the business keeps stagnating — and your salespeople keep getting low-quality leads — over the next 3, 6, or even 12 months?"*

> *"Paint the picture. Specific images in their mind make the consequence feel real — not abstract."*

---

## Reading Their Response: Three Paths

### Path 1 — They Push Back Confidently (*"That's not going to happen"*)

> *"Are you willing to settle for that?"* → *"No."* *"Whose choice is it — if it happens or not?"* → *"Mine."* *"So time to make a change, possibly?"*

When a prospect says *"I'm not going to let that happen"* — that's actually what you want. It means they're committed to change. They just told you themselves.

> *"In my mind, they're saying: I'm not going to fail — I'm going to do something about this. That's the answer I'm looking for."*

**The simple sequence for beginners:**

> *"Okay so it's important for you to do something then."* → *"Yes."* *"But what happens if you don't though?"*

This is the one-two punch that works more than 80% of the time — without requiring you to read the room as precisely.

---

### Path 2 — They're Nonchalant (*"I hadn't really thought about that..."*)

Don't panic. Slow down and help them see it:

> *"Well, if you really thought about it — what would happen?"* → They start to describe the negative consequences. *"Are you willing to settle for that?"* → *"No."* *"Whose choice is it?"* → *"Mine."* *"So what do you think you should do?"*

---

### Path 3 — They Deflect with Positivity (*"I'll still be happy"* / *"Everything happens for a reason"*)

Don't argue with their frame. Agree with it — then redirect:

> *"Yeah, I mean, honestly — getting more leads, building a brand, making more money... that doesn't necessarily make a person happy. But when you have those things, what does it give you that you don't have now?"*

Or:

> *"Yeah, I totally agree — money doesn't make you more spiritual. But not having those things — what's it preventing you from being able to do?"*

**Why this works:** You're not dismissing their values. You're using their values as a bridge to get them back to the outcome. You agree, then redirect.

---

## The "Which Is More Risky?" Frame

Another powerful consequence question format — especially when a prospect is weighing action against inaction:

> *"Which is more risky — getting the funding together to build your business and make $250K, or doing nothing at all, the situation stays the same, and nothing ever changes? Which is more risky?"*

This reframe inverts the prospect's default thinking. Most people fear making a purchase. This question makes *not* purchasing feel like the riskier choice.

---

## Handling the "Too Good to Be True" Objection Mid-Consequence

If a prospect says *"this sounds too good to be true"*:

> *"The saying 'if it sounds too good to be true it probably is' — who do you think came up with that way of thinking? Someone who was extremely wealthy, or someone who was poor?"* → *"Someone poor."* *"So if we think the same way poor people think — what type of financial results do we get?"* → They say it themselves.

You never told them they were thinking like a poor person. They told themselves.

**Alternative approach:**

> *"We don't have to do it that way — we can make it harder if you want."*

When they immediately say *"no, no — I didn't mean that"* — you've moved them back from defensive to open. It's a pattern interrupt.

---

## Key Principles for Consequence Questions

### 1\. Always Be Specific — Paint the Picture

Generic consequence questions get generic answers. Specific, vivid questions create real emotional responses.

> *"Lower quality leads coming to your salespeople, salespeople complaining, salespeople quitting, company stagnating — over the next 3, 6, or 12 months — what does that look like?"*

The time frame matters too — use **real time language** (3 months, 6 months, 12 months) — not *"5 weeks, 5 months, 5 years."* Nobody thinks in those intervals. Real people think in quarters, months, a year out.

### 2\. Tone and Pace

Too fast = knee-jerk answer. Too slow = awkward.

Pause before the consequence question. Let the emotional high from solution awareness settle. Then ask it with calm, genuine concern — not interrogation.

### 3\. "You're Right" vs. "That's Right"

Watch for this distinction (from Chris Voss):

-   *"You're right"* = they're agreeing with you, but it's your idea — they may not act on it
    
-   *"That's right"* = it's coming from them — they own it
    

When a prospect says *"you're right"* — don't just move on. Redirect it back to them:

> *"Well — what do you think?"*

Make them say *"that's right"* — because then the conclusion is theirs.

### 4\. The Consequence Can't Save a Weak Engagement

> *"The consequence question won't save terrible engagement that happened before it. Everything that came before has to be solid for this to land."*

If problem awareness and solution awareness were thin, the prospect won't feel enough to be moved by consequence questions. This is why the whole process matters — not just the closing stages.

---

## The "Always Be Disarming" Reminder

Even at the consequence stage, disarming never stops. If a prospect starts going into fight or flight at any point:

1.  Acknowledge their position
    
2.  Agree with the surface feeling
    
3.  Redirect with a question that brings them back to their own stated goals
    

> *"I'm just continually bringing them back up here. They keep dipping down into fight or flight — I loop them back. By the time they send the funds, they're still right here — open, not defensive."*

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Paint specific pictures</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Generic consequences feel hypothetical; specific ones feel real</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Use real time frames</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"3, 6, or 12 months" — not artificial intervals</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"It's important for you to do something then"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Captures their own ego and commitment before the follow-up punch</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"What happens if you don't though?"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The follow-up that closes the box even for resistant prospects</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Agree before redirecting</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Never argue with their frame — agree, then ask what they're missing</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"That's right" &gt; "You're right"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Their conclusion creates ownership; your conclusion creates resistance</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Consequence can't save weak engagement</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Everything before this stage has to be built properly</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Write out your specific consequence question — replace vague language with the exact problem words your prospect used earlier in the call
    
-   \[ \] Memorize the simple sequence: *"So it's important for you to do something then — yes? But what happens if you don't though?"*
    
-   \[ \] Practice the "which is more risky?" frame for your product
    
-   \[ \] Build two or three "agree and redirect" responses for common deflections (*"I'll be fine"*, *"everything happens for a reason"*, *"money doesn't make me happy"*)
    
-   \[ \] Watch Tony Robbins on YouTube — specifically when he's down in the audience — observe how he uses questions, pauses, and body language to change someone's state in minutes
