# Qualifying Questions & The Transition Stage: Financial Qualification and Reading the Room

---

## Overview

This session covers two interconnected skills: how to use qualifying questions strategically (not robotically), and how to financially qualify prospects in a way that ties money to their desired outcome — so the price never feels like an objection waiting to happen.

---

## Part 1 — Qualifying Questions: Use Them Only When Needed

Qualifying questions are not a mandatory step in the sequence. When you've done your engagement stage well — situation, problem awareness, solution awareness, consequence — the prospect has usually already qualified themselves through their own answers.

> *"Most of the time when you learn the process, qualifying questions are only needed about 5% of the time — because they've already qualified themselves."*

**When you DO need them:**

-   When the prospect gives a vague or uncommitted consequence answer (*"I guess I'd just figure something out"*)
    
-   When financial signals early in the call suggest potential barriers
    
-   When you need to confirm their level of urgency before moving to the presentation
    

**When you DON'T need them:**

-   When they've already shown clear urgency, emotion, and commitment throughout the call
    
-   When their financial situation has already been made clear through earlier questions
    

---

## Part 2 — The Transition Stage

After consequence questions, you're transitioning the prospect into the next stage of the process. What that looks like depends on your sales model:

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Sales Model</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You're Transitioning To</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>One-call close</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Presentation and pillars</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Two-call close</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Second appointment, demo, or proposal</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Complex B2B (multi-call)</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Next decision maker, demo, board meeting</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

In every case, the same principle applies: **you're always disarming them to get them to take the next small step — which leads to the large commitment.**

> *"In B2B, you might need six calls before a decision. Each call you're disarming them and getting a smaller commitment — show up to the next call, agree to a demo, bring in the board — that leads to the final yes."*

---

## Part 3 — Financial Qualification: The Right Way

### Never Just Name Your Price

Most salespeople, when asked *"is this going to cost me money?"* panic and either dodge or blurt out the number. Both are wrong.

**The Framework: Tie the Money to the Outcome**

> *"I'm assuming you understand — when you start your own business, it's going to require some capital or credit to invest in it, to make more money. With your situation, what type of funding do you have or can you get access to, so that you can \[repeat back their desired outcome\]?"*

**Why this works:**

-   It frames money as the *means to get what they want* — not a price you're extracting
    
-   It shifts them from price-based thinking to results-based thinking
    
-   *"What type of funding do you have"* sounds like you're checking if you can help — not selling
    

---

### The Funding Sequence (Step by Step)

**Step 1 — Set the Context**

> *"I'm assuming you understand that starting your own business is going to require some capital or credit to invest in it, to make more money."*

*"I'm assuming you understand"* is the key phrase. It's not a question — it's a given. You're stating it as fact, not surprising them.

**Step 2 — Ask What They Have Access To**

> *"With your situation — what type of funding do you have or can you get access to put into the business, so you can actually make more money?"*

**Step 3 — If They Don't Know How Much Is Needed**

Get them to come up with the number themselves:

> *"How much money do you think most people put into a business to be able to make \[their stated income goal\] in their first year — realistically?"*

When they give you a range — you know what they believe is reasonable. If your price is within or below that range, you don't need to fight for it.

**Step 4 — If They Give an Unrealistic Income Goal**

If they say *"I want to make $1M"* when they currently earn $60K:

> *"Yeah, making a million dollars is something people are doing more in year two or three. Realistically, what do you want to make your first year?"*

Bring them to a credible number — not because you're lowering their ambition, but because they have to *believe* the goal is achievable. Unbelievable goals don't create urgency; they create skepticism.

---

### Asking About Income Without Making It Their Fault

When you need to find out what they currently earn:

❌ *"How much do you make?"* — feels like it's their fault they don't earn enough

✅ *"How much are they paying you at your job?"* — frames it as the employer's limitation, not theirs

> *"I want them to feel like it's not their fault they're in their current situation. It's the vehicle they're in. I need to help them see they need a different vehicle."*

---

### The Gap Must Exist

If someone says they want to make $200K and they're already making $200K combined with their spouse — there's no gap. No gap = no reason to buy.

When this happens, reframe:

> *"So you're already making $200K between you and your spouse — so when you say you want to make $200K, you're talking about making that yourself, on top of what you already earn? So you're really looking at $400K?"*

If they say yes — you've just doubled the gap. Now there's a reason to act.

---

## Part 4 — Reading Financial Signals Without Assuming

**The rule:** Don't qualify based on profile alone. A college student could have a wealthy aunt. A FedEx driver with seven kids might have more access to capital than their title suggests. Always give the person the opportunity to surface solutions themselves before concluding they're unqualified.

**But:** If every signal points the same direction — part-time work, $1K in savings, 600 credit score, friend who's only ever loaned them $100, and no ability to find $10K — don't waste 45 minutes on someone you can't help. Refer them back when they're in a better position.

> *"Keep my information — when you get closer to having that type of funding, come back to me and we can go through the details to see if we can help. Would that help you?"*

This is respectful, not dismissive. You're not closing the door — you're being honest about the timing.

---

## Part 5 — Always Be on the Yellow Brick Road

The prospect will take you off the path. They'll say things that are unexpected, confusing, or tangential. Your job is to resolve the detour with a question — not a speech — and bring them back.

> *"If he takes me off the path, I ask something to help him resolve his concern, and then I bring him back on the path. Continually. You have to do that with some prospects."*

**The difference between top performers and average performers:** Top performers know what to do in every scenario — north, south, east, west — because they've memorized their questions and internalized the process. Average performers wing it and get stuck.

**The solution:** Know your questions so well that when the conversation goes somewhere unexpected, you have the tools to pivot without losing the thread.

---

## Part 6 — Never Assume

> *"First seek to understand — then be understood."* — Stephen Covey

Before drawing conclusions about what a prospect means, believes, or can afford — ask. Tone you can't hear in text. Context you don't have from a job title. A prospect who says *"I just couldn't afford it"* about a previous program — they couldn't afford *that* program. If yours costs $5K and theirs was $35K, you may not need to financially qualify at all.

Neutral language protects you. Assumption gets you in trouble.

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Qualifying questions are conditional, not mandatory</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Use them when needed; skip them when the prospect has already self-qualified</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Tie money to their desired outcome</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Transforms price objection into a means-to-an-end conversation</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"I'm assuming you understand"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>States investment as a given — no surprise, no pressure</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Get them to name the number</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>When they come up with the figure themselves, they own it</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Blame the employer, not the person</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>"How much are they paying you?" vs. "How much do you make?"</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Create or confirm the gap</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>If there's no gap between current and desired state, there's no urgency to buy</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Never assume — always ask</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>One prospect's "I can't afford it" may mean something completely different</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Know the Yellow Brick Road</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Be prepared to pivot in any direction and bring them back to the path</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Write your financial qualification sequence: context-setting statement + access-to-funding question + outcome tie-in, all in your own words for your product
    
-   \[ \] Practice asking *"how much are they paying you?"* instead of *"how much do you make?"* — note the difference in how people respond
    
-   \[ \] Identify your income gap threshold — what's the minimum gap between current and desired income that justifies your price? Build your questions around creating or confirming that gap
    
-   \[ \] Map out the most common ways prospects take you off the Yellow Brick Road on your calls — and write out what you'd say to resolve it and bring them back
    
-   \[ \] Memorize the "I'm assuming you understand" framework until you can deliver it naturally without notes

# The Transition Stage Role-Play Part 2: Bridging to the Presentation

---

## Overview

The transition is the bridge between consequence questions and your presentation. It's a short, precise statement that mirrors back what the prospect told you — their desired outcome, their current problem, and how it's making them feel — before moving into your solution. Done right, it positions you as the expert who truly listened and understands exactly where they need to go.

---

## The Transition Formula

> *"Based on what you've told me — what we're doing \[would / could\] actually work for you — because you know how you said you're wanting to \[their desired outcome\], but right now \[their logical problem\], and because of that it's caused you to feel I think a little bit of \[emotional impact\] sometimes."*

**The order matters:**

1.  **Their desired outcome** — what they said they want
    
2.  **Their logical problem** — what's currently blocking them
    
3.  **Their emotional impact** — how it's making them feel (minimized — see below)
    

**Wrong order:**

> *"...you know you're working your corporate job and you don't have a lot of time \[problem\], and you'd rather spend time with your kids \[outcome\], and it's making you feel stressed \[emotion\]."*

**Right order:**

> *"...you know how you're saying you want to find a business where you can have more time with your daughter \[outcome\], but right now your job is making you work 60–70 hours a week and you have a long commute \[problem\], and because of that it's caused you to feel I think a little bit of stress sometimes \[emotion minimized\]."*

---

## "Would" vs. "Could" — Read the Room

The word you use here matters:

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Prospect Energy</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Use</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Open, engaged throughout the call</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"would actually work for you"</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Closed off, on the fence, or skeptical</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"could actually work for you"</em></p></td></tr></tbody></table>

*"Would"* is more assertive — appropriate when they've been open. *"Could"* is more neutral — appropriate when they're still warming up.

**Never use "definitely"** — it comes across as hype, not confidence. *"Would"* already implies certainty without overselling it.

---

## The Minimized Emotion Technique

This is one of the most counterintuitive moves in the entire process — and one of the most effective.

When you reflect their emotional pain back to them, **deliberately understate it:**

> *"...and because of that it's caused you to feel I think a little bit of stress sometimes."*

**Why understate it?**

Because when you understate their pain, human nature kicks in — regardless of personality type — and they correct you upward:

> *"Oh, you have no idea — it's way more than a little bit."*

**If you overstate it** (*"it's caused you a lot of stress and problems at home"*) — especially with Type A or ego-driven personalities — they'll instinctively push back:

> *"Well... I mean, it's not that bad."*

Now you've lost the emotional momentum you built in problem awareness.

> *"It doesn't matter what their personality type is. When I say 'a little bit of stress sometimes' — they're going to say 'you have no idea.' Every time."*

---

## What to Do When They Expand on the Pain

If they respond with *"you have no idea how stressful this is"* — you have a choice based on how the call has gone:

**If you haven't built enough emotion yet:**

> *"Hold on — what do you mean by that?"*

Let them expand further. Get them to relive more of the pain before you move into the presentation.

**If you've already built strong emotion throughout the call:** Don't probe again. Over-probing at this stage is annoying — they've already told you. Just acknowledge and move into the presentation.

> *"It's all reactive — based on how the conversation has gone, and their tonality and body language."*

---

## Be Assertive Here — You're the Expert Now

At the transition stage, you've shifted from curious and confused to credible authority. Your tonality needs to reflect that.

Filler words (*"you know"*, *"like"*, *"um"*) and tentative delivery undermine your credibility exactly when you need it most. The prospect needs to feel: *this person knows what they're doing and can actually get me where I want to go.*

> *"At this point, you have to be a bit more assertive. You're going into the presentation — they need to feel like you're credible and you know what you're doing."*

---

## The Full Transition — Assembled

> *"Okay so it's important for you to do something then."* → *"Yeah, definitely."* *"Okay — because based on what you've told me, what we're doing would actually work for you. Because you know how you're saying you want to find a business where you can make more money and have more time with your daughter — but right now at your job they cut your base pay by 20%, and because of that it's caused you to feel I think a little bit of frustration sometimes."* → *"Oh yeah — you have no idea."*

And now you move into the presentation.

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Follow the right order</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Outcome → Problem → Emotion — not problem first</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Would" vs "could"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Match the word to how open or closed they've been</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Never use "definitely"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Sounds like hype — "would" carries quiet confidence</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Minimize the emotion</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Understating pain triggers "you have no idea" — overstating triggers defensiveness</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Only re-probe if needed</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>If emotion is already high, don't go back in — move forward</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Be assertive</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You've earned the expert status — let your delivery show it</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Write your transition statement for your product using the formula: outcome → problem → minimized emotion
    
-   \[ \] Practice delivering it with assertive, calm confidence — no filler words
    
-   \[ \] Record yourself doing the transition and listen back — do you sound like an expert or a salesperson hoping for a yes?
    
-   \[ \] Test the minimized emotion phrase on your next call — note how the prospect responds

# Commitment Questions: Getting Prospects to Take the Next Step

---

## Overview

Commitment questions are the bridge between your presentation and the close. They're simple, short, and often misunderstood. The key insight: **the sale is not made here.** The sale was made in engagement — problem awareness, solution awareness, consequence. Commitment questions simply get the prospect to verbalize what they've already decided internally and take the next step.

> *"Commitment questions are maybe 2–5% of the closing process. Where are they actually deciding to buy? In the engagement stage — problem awareness, solution awareness, consequence. Not here."*

---

## The Core Principle: Still Disarming

Old-school closes (*"Would you like it delivered Thursday or Friday?"*) trigger fight or flight because they feel like a trap. The prospect who was on the fence immediately becomes defensive — generating more objections, not fewer.

ABDS applies here too. **Always Be Disarming — all the way through commitment.**

---

## The Two Commitment Questions

### Commitment Question 1

> *"Do you feel like this could be the answer for you?"*

Then — with calm skepticism:

> *"Why do you feel like it is though?"*

**Why ask why?** It gets them to defend their own decision with their own words. They're not just agreeing — they're persuading themselves out loud.

**Avoid:** *"Can I ask why?"* — too soft, sounds like you're seeking permission. Just ask it directly:

> *"Why do you feel like it is though?"* or *"Hold on — why do you feel like it is?"*

---

### Commitment Question 2

After they give you their reason:

> *"But besides that — what specific parts of \[your solution\] do you feel like is really going to get you \[repeat back their exact desired outcome\]?"*

**Why this matters:** It moves them from a general *"I like it"* to a specific mental picture of how this solution solves their exact problem. They're now actively visualizing the outcome — which locks in the commitment.

**Example for fitness:**

> *"But besides the support and accountability — what specific parts of the training do you feel like is really going to help you lose those 30 pounds?"*

**Example for business/income:**

> *"What specific parts of the training do you feel like is going to help you the most to make sure you're able to leave your job and get up to $200K in the next 12 months?"*

**Key:** Always repeat back their exact stated outcome — not a generic benefit.

---

## The Optional Probing Statement

If their first answer is vague (*"I just do"*), instead of re-asking *"why do you feel like it is?"* — which would be over-probing — use a condensed probing statement:

> *"So that's important to you then."*

This is a probing statement, not a question. It acknowledges what they said and signals them to expand — without making it feel like an interrogation.

---

## Asking for the Order

After the double commitment, transition directly into the next step — clear, simple, confident:

> *"Okay — I don't really have too much else to go over with you. It looks like we covered what you're looking for. The next step, if it's appropriate, is to make some kind of arrangement for \[onboarding/getting started\]. It's fairly simple — we just need \[details\]. That gets you introduced to \[what they get\] to start the process of \[their stated outcome\]. Would that be appropriate, or how would you like to proceed?"*

**Key language choices:**

-   *"The next step, if it's appropriate"* — not *"the next step is"* — keeps it collaborative, not pushy
    
-   *"How would you like to proceed?"* — hands them some control; non-pressuring
    
-   Always end with what they get that connects to their outcome
    

---

## The "Could" vs. "Would" Distinction (Applies Here Too)

> *"Do you feel like this could be the answer for you?"*

Use **could** — not *would* — in the commitment question itself. It's softer and less presumptuous. They haven't bought yet; *could* keeps them in the driver's seat of the decision.

---

## What NOT to Do

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Mistake</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It's Wrong</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Can I ask why?"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Too passive — ask it directly</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Probing the second commitment question too</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>One <em>"why do you feel like it is?"</em> is enough — don't ask twice</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Overselling the order step</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Keep it simple and matter-of-fact</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Skipping the specific parts question</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>That's where they give you the second reason they're buying</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## The Complete Commitment Sequence

1.  *"Do you feel like this could be the answer for you?"*
    
2.  → *"Yeah, I think so."*
    
3.  *"Why do you feel like it is though?"*
    
4.  → They give a reason.
    
5.  *"But besides that — what specific parts of \[solution\] do you feel like is really going to get you \[their exact outcome\]?"*
    
6.  → They give another, more specific reason.
    
7.  *"Okay — I don't really have much else to go over with you. It looks like we've covered what you're looking for. The next step, if it's appropriate, is \[simple description of what getting started looks like\]. Would that be appropriate, or how would you like to proceed?"*
    

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Commitment questions are 2–5% of the close</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The real decision was made in engagement — this just confirms and moves forward</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Always Be Disarming (ABDS)</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Pushy closing triggers resistance — calm, conversational questions don't</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Double commitment strengthens resolve</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Two reasons &gt; one reason; they own the decision more fully</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Specific parts question creates visualization</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>They're not just agreeing — they're seeing themselves getting the result</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Probing statement &gt; probing question (when appropriate)</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"So that's important to you then"</em> — softer than another <em>"why"</em></p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Memorize the two commitment questions and practice until they sound natural, assertive, and conversational
    
-   \[ \] Build your specific parts question with your product's exact outcome language
    
-   \[ \] Practice the order close: *"The next step, if it's appropriate, is..."* — no filler, no hedging, clear and confident
    
-   \[ \] Record yourself doing the commitment sequence and check: are you too soft? Too fast? Sounding like you're asking permission?

---

### 42 Role Play Exercise 12

https://youtu.be/4qZGSNXMM8M

# Objection Prevention vs. Objection Handling: The "Think It Over" Deep Dive

---

## Overview

There are two distinct skills when it comes to objections: **prevention** and **handling**. Prevention is always preferable — a well-run engagement stage eliminates roughly 70% of objections before they surface. But when objections do come up, how you respond determines whether you get back on the path or spiral into a fight you can't win.

---

## Objection Prevention vs. Objection Handling

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 75px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p></p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Prevention</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Handling</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>When</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Throughout the entire call</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>After the objection surfaces</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>How</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Disarming language, seeding, consequence questions</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Acknowledging, booking a time, surfacing the real concern</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Goal</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Stop the objection from forming in their mind</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Resolve the concern and get back on the path</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Effectiveness</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Eliminates ~70% of objections</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Handles the remaining 30%</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

> *"Little things like 'like we do with our other clients' — just those little seeds keep disarming them throughout the call. That's objection prevention in action."*

---

## The "Think It Over" Objection: What NOT to Do

**The old way:**

> *"What do you need to think about?"*

This triggers fight or flight immediately. You're essentially challenging them — *"I thought you said you wanted to change, why would you think about it?"* — and they hear it as argumentative. They put up walls. You go in circles.

**The trap most salespeople fall into:** Leaning into the objection — arguing with it, trying to immediately overcome it — which signals desperation and drops your status below theirs.

---

## The Right Framework: The Four-Step "Think It Over" Response

### Step 1 — Agree and Set a Time (Your Insurance Policy)

> *"That's not a problem — what's your time frame on getting back to me? The next day or two, just to see if I'd be available for you?"*

**Why "just to see if I'd be available for you":**

-   Positions you as busy — not chasing them
    
-   Implies you have other clients and commitments
    
-   Keeps you at or above their status level
    

**If they give a vague timeframe (*"maybe later in the week"*):**

> *"Yeah — it's kind of hard to randomly get me like that with my schedule and clients and everything. What I can do though, if it helps you — if you have your calendar handy, I can pull up mine and have you book a specific time with me. That way you don't have to chase me down and vice versa. Would that be more appropriate?"*

**The key phrase:** *"That way you don't have to chase me down and vice versa."*

This subtly shifts who's chasing whom. You're not chasing them — if anything, they'd be chasing you. That's an expert, not a desperate salesperson.

> *"The booked appointment is your insurance policy. If you can't help them overcome the concern in the call, you still have something. Without a specific time — that deal is going nowhere."*

---

### Step 2 — Signal You're Leaving

Once the time is booked:

> *"Before I go..."*

**Why these three words matter:** The moment a prospect thinks you're leaving — that the call is ending — their guard comes down. They feel safe being honest because there's no immediate pressure to decide. The "exit question" that follows will almost always get the real concern.

---

### Step 3 — The Exit Question

> *"Before I go — what were you wanting to go over in your mind, just so I know what questions you might have when we talk on Friday?"*

**Why this works:**

-   *"What were you wanting to go over in your mind"* is softer than *"what do you want to think over"* — it doesn't trigger the same defensive pattern
    
-   *"Just so I know what questions you might have when we talk on Friday"* gives them permission to be honest — because the pressure of right now has been removed
    
-   They're thinking: *"I can tell him this because we're talking on Friday anyway"*
    

**The result:** They tell you the real concern. Almost every time.

---

### Step 4 — You're Back on the Yellow Brick Road

Once they tell you what's on their mind (*"I'm just not sure about the financing"*), you're no longer dealing with a vague "I need to think it over." You have something real to work with.

> *"What do you mean by the financing?"*

And now you're back in the conversation — addressing an actual concern rather than fighting a smoke screen.

---

## The Status Dynamic: Push-Pull

Think of it like the dating world. When you chase someone too eagerly early on — they pull away. When you maintain mystery and detachment — they lean in.

Sales works the same way.

**Every status-maintaining move in this sequence:**

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>What you say</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>What it signals</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Just to see if I'd be available for you"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You're busy — not waiting by the phone</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Hard to randomly get me like that"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Your time is in demand</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"That way you don't have to chase me down"</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You're the authority — they'd be chasing you, not the other way around</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"Before I go..."</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You're not desperate — you're leaving</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

> *"The moment they feel you really need them — especially before trust is built — they pull away. Stay at their level or above. That's what keeps them interested."*

---

## The Zoom Room Option (Advanced)

If the prospect is with a business partner or spouse and they want time to discuss:

> *"Would it help you if I stepped out for a few minutes so you could talk it over?"*

Leave the Zoom room. Let them discuss privately. When you return, they've often resolved their own concerns — because they talked through it together without the pressure of a salesperson in the room.

*Note: This is a situational move — use it when you can tell they genuinely need to confer, not as a default.*

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Prevention beats handling</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>A disarmed prospect rarely objects at the close</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Never argue with "think it over"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Arguing triggers fight or flight — agree and disarm instead</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Book a specific time — always</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Vague callbacks don't happen; a booked slot is your insurance policy</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Just to see if I'd be available"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Signals you're the expert with a full calendar</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>"Before I go"</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Drops their guard — they'll tell you the real concern</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>The exit question surfaces truth</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"What were you wanting to go over in your mind?"</em> is softer and gets more honest answers</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Status above = they lean in</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Push-pull dynamics apply in sales exactly as in human relationships</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Remove *"What do you need to think about?"* from your vocabulary permanently — replace it with the agree-and-book framework
    
-   \[ \] Memorize: *"What's your time frame on getting back to me — the next day or two — just to see if I'd be available for you?"*
    
-   \[ \] Memorize: *"Before I go — what were you wanting to go over in your mind, just so I know what questions you might have when we talk \[day\]?"*
    
-   \[ \] Practice the calendar close: *"If you have your calendar handy, I can pull up mine — that way you don't have to chase me down and vice versa"*
    
-   \[ \] On your next three "think it over" situations — apply the full four-step framework and note what real concern surfaces at the exit question

---

# Live Objection Handling Role-Plays: Money, Spouse, and Consequence Questions

---

## Overview

This session features live role-plays covering the most common objections salespeople face — the money objection, the spouse/partner objection, and how to apply consequence questions in real time. The through-line across all of it: **Always Be Disarming (ABDS).** The moment you argue with an objection, you trigger fight or flight and lose control of the conversation.

---

## The NEPQ Framework Recap (For Context)

> *"Prospects are subconsciously picking up on your verbal and non-verbal cues based on your tonality and body language — and what you are saying and or asking — that triggers their brain to react."*

Old-school closing (ABC — Always Be Closing) no longer works. Today's prospects have options and know they're being sold to. The new model is **ABD — Always Be Disarming** — from connecting questions all the way through commitment, transition, and objection handling.

---

## Part 1 — The Money Objection

### What NOT to Do

**Wrong response:**

> *"But you said you wanted to change — what do you need to think about?"*

This argues with the prospect. It triggers fight or flight immediately. They either defend themselves aggressively or shut down — and you go in circles.

**The principle behind the right approach:** Get them to come up with the solutions themselves. When you tell them how to find the money, it goes in one ear and out the other. When they discover the solution themselves — they own it.

---

### The Money Objection Framework

**Step 1 — Clarify what they mean**

> *"When you say you don't have the budget — could you help me understand what you mean?"*

Don't assume. *"Can't afford it"* can mean many different things.

**Step 2 — Test if money is the only barrier**

> *"If you did have the funding available right now — is this something you feel would get you from where you are to where you want the company to go?"*

If yes → proceed. If no → you have a different problem to address first.

**Step 3 — Make them articulate why it would work**

> *"Why do you feel like it would though?"* *(skeptical tone)*

Gets them to defend their own decision with their own words.

**Step 4 — Tie the funding to their outcome**

> *"I can appreciate that money might be an issue from what you told me. How do you think you can resolve that so that you can \[repeat back their desired outcome\]?"*

The critical move: always connect the money to what they said they want. If they can't get the funding, they can't have the result. That's the equation — not your price vs. their budget.

**Step 5 — If they can't come up with solutions**

> *"What other avenues do you have to find the funding so that you can \[their outcome\]?"*

**Step 6 — Probe any avenue they suggest**

If they say *"I could borrow from a family member"*:

> *"Why would your family member be willing to loan you capital to grow your business? What's in it for them?"*

If they say *"I could use a 401K"*:

> *"Why would your financial advisor let you pull from your 401K for this? What if they say no — what would you do then?"*

Getting them to address the obstacle before they face it means they own the decision — not the advisor, not the spouse, not the bank.

**Step 7 — Mini consequence if needed**

> *"What happens if you don't find the funding and you continue to lose clients and sales go down?"* → *"I don't want to think about that."* *"But if you don't think about it — is that going to stop it from possibly happening?"*

---

### Key Money Objection Principles

-   **Neutral, not defensive** — Don't change your tone or energy when the objection hits. Same calm, conversational delivery throughout
    
-   **Collaborative, not combative** — You're working with them to solve their concern, not arguing against it
    
-   **Results-based, not price-based** — Always frame the funding as the means to get what they want, not as a cost they're paying
    

---

## Part 2 — The Spouse/Partner Objection

### The Framing Question

When a prospect says *"I need to talk to my spouse"* — don't just acknowledge and move on. Ask:

> *"That's not a problem — tell me, how does your wife/husband feel about you \[repeat back what they said they wanted\]?"*

**Example for Amazon training:**

> *"That's not a problem — how does your wife feel about you starting your own business so you guys can make more money?"*

**Why the wording matters:**

❌ *"Is your wife supportive of you making money?"* — generic, less powerful

✅ *"How does your wife feel about you starting your own Amazon business so you guys can make more money?"* — specific, ties the spouse to the outcome they both share

---

### When the Spouse Has Been Burned Before

If you discover there's a history (*"we invested in something similar before and got burned"*):

> *"Has something happened in the past? What's behind that?"*

Surface the real concern underneath the surface objection. Then:

> *"What specifically do you think held you back from having success with that program?"*

> *"Were there other people making money using that program?"* → If yes: *"So what do you think prevented you from getting the same results?"*

**The goal:** Get them to conclude that the failure was circumstantial — not that the model doesn't work. When they own that conclusion, the past failure stops being an objection.

---

### Coaching Them on How to Approach Their Spouse

When appropriate:

> *"Can I make a suggestion? What if, when you went to her, instead of just talking about being committed — you also talked about some of the challenges you're having at your job, how you feel like you're not going to get promoted, how you're tired of the commute, how you're not getting enough time with her and the kids? Would that help her understand why this matters to you?"*

**Why this works:** You're not coaching them to sell your program to their spouse. You're coaching them to share the same pain they shared with you — which their spouse will connect with emotionally just as they did.

> *"Can I make a suggestion without you getting angry at me?"* — this pre-frame disarms them before a challenging suggestion and almost always gets *"of course, go ahead."*

---

### Always Resolve One Objection Before Moving to the Next

If a prospect raises a second concern while you're still addressing the first:

> *"Before we go there — let's come back here with me for a second. \[Address the first concern fully\]. Okay, now let's go to what you were just mentioning."*

Leaving an unresolved concern sitting in their mind while you address a second one means you're building on an unstable foundation.

---

## Part 3 — Consequence Questions Live

### The Specific Consequence Question

The more specific the consequence question, the more vivid the picture in the prospect's mind — and the more real the urgency.

**Generic (weak):**

> *"What happens if you don't do anything and things get worse?"*

**Specific (powerful):**

> *"What if you don't do anything about this and you keep having to cold call 3–4 hours a day and your listings keep going down the next 3, 6, even 12 months from now?"*

When they start to visualize *no listings → no sales → less income → can't pay bills → stress at home* — the consequence becomes real and the urgency builds on its own.

---

### Handling "I'll Figure Something Out"

One of the trickier responses to a consequence question. Don't argue with it directly. Probe it:

> *"In what way though?"*

When they can't answer — they realize they don't actually have a plan. Then:

> *"Have you always figured something out?"* *(calm, not sarcastic)*

When they say yes — and they will — the natural follow-up is:

> *"Well, you wouldn't have booked on my calendar and raised your hand asking for help if you'd always figured it out."*

They've contradicted themselves — gently, without you pointing it out aggressively. The realization is theirs.

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>ABDS throughout objections</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The moment you argue, you trigger fight or flight</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Let them find the solution</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Self-discovered solutions create commitment; told solutions create resistance</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Tie money to outcome, always</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Price vs. budget is a losing frame; funding vs. desired result is winning</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Probe every avenue they suggest</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Ensure they own the commitment before they face an obstacle</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Spouse framing matters</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Connect the spouse's potential objection to a shared outcome they both want</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Surface the history behind objections</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"What's behind that?"</em> reveals the real concern under the surface objection</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Specific consequence questions</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Generic scenarios feel hypothetical; specific ones feel real and urgent</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Resolve objections one at a time</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Never let an unresolved concern sit while you chase a second one</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Build your money objection sequence: clarify → test the barrier → tie funding to outcome → probe avenues → mini consequence
    
-   \[ \] Practice the spouse framing: *"How does your \[spouse\] feel about you \[their stated desired outcome\]?"* — specific, not generic
    
-   \[ \] Prepare 2–3 responses for *"I'll figure something out"* — including the *"have you always figured it out?"* challenge
    
-   \[ \] Write a specific consequence question for your product — name the exact actions they'll keep doing and the exact results that will keep declining if they do nothing
    
-   \[ \] Practice staying tonally neutral when an objection hits — record yourself and check: did your voice change? Did you lean forward? Did you sound defensive?

---

### 44 How To Set Income Goals

https://youtu.be/Lakq5kIRbgo

# How to Set Income Goals: Turning a Number Into a Vision

---

## Overview

A number by itself is meaningless. *"I want to make more money"* is a wish — not a goal. This module walks through how to build a real income target that's connected to a specific lifestyle, broken down into a realistic timeline, and linked to the skill and opportunity gaps you need to close to get there.

---

## Why Income Goals Need to Be SMART

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 75px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Letter</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Meaning</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Example</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>S</strong> — Specific</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>A precise number, not a range</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><em>"$824,000 per year"</em> — not <em>"I want to make more"</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>M</strong> — Measurable</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>A dollar amount you can track</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Monthly, quarterly, annually</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>A</strong> — Achievable</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Realistic given your current starting point</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Don't set a 12-month goal that requires 10x growth from $50K</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>R</strong> — Relevant</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Tied to a tangible <em>why</em></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>At this income, I can do <em>this specific thing</em></p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>T</strong> — Timely</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Has a clear deadline</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Without a timeframe, it's just a dream</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Step 1 — Build Your Lifestyle Vision First

Before you pick a number, reverse-engineer it from the life you actually want.

Ask yourself:

-   **Housing:** What kind of home do you want in 10 years? Where? What will it cost? What's the down payment and monthly mortgage?
    
-   **Family:** Do you plan to have kids? Public or private school? What does that cost annually?
    
-   **Travel:** How many holidays per year? What does each one cost?
    
-   **Vehicles:** What do you want to drive? What does your partner want? Monthly payments?
    
-   **Savings/Investments:** How much do you want liquid in the bank? How much invested monthly?
    
-   **Living expenses:** What will your monthly overhead look like at that income level? (More income = more spending — factor that in honestly)
    

Once you have the full picture, add it up. That's your **post-tax requirement**. Then add your tax rate on top — that's your **gross income goal**.

> *"In 10 years I need to be making $1.2M gross, which gives me $720K net, which allows me to buy this house, drive these cars, fund my kids' education, take 4 holidays a year, and save $10K per month."*

That's a vision. A number alone is not.

---

## Step 2 — Break It Down Backwards

Income growth is rarely linear. It compounds — the more you make, the more you can invest, which accelerates further growth. Build your timeline accordingly:

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Timeframe</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Where You Should Be</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>10 years</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Full goal achieved</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>5 years</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>~30% of the way there (compounding hasn't kicked in yet)</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>3 years</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Meaningful progress, skills and systems in place</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>1 year</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Foundation established, trajectory confirmed</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Quarterly</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Specific, measurable income targets</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Monthly</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Daily and weekly actions that produce that result</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

> *"You don't need to be 50% there at the halfway mark. You might only be 25–30% of the way at the 5-year mark — because the last 50% often comes in the final 20% of the time as compounding accelerates."*

---

## Step 3 — Identify the Gap

Once you have your 3–6–9 month income target, ask:

**Can I realistically achieve this given:**

1.  The **opportunities** currently in front of me?
    
2.  The **skills** I currently have?
    

This surfaces two types of gaps:

**Opportunity Gap** — The market or vehicle you're in may not have enough earning potential to hit your target. You may need to expand, shift, or access different clients.

**Skill Gap** — You have the opportunity, but you're not converting it. This is where to focus.

**Identify your weakest metric and fix that first:**

-   Great show-up rate but low close rate? → Work on closing
    
-   Great close rate but poor show-up rate? → Work on booking and pre-call engagement
    
-   Both are solid but still short of goal? → Look at offer, pricing, or pipeline volume
    

---

## Step 4 — The Consequence of Not Doing This

Once you have a specific vision, you can run the same logic on yourself that you run on prospects:

> *"What if I don't hit this goal? What if I stay at my current income for the next 5 years — can I actually live the life I want on that? Can I afford two kids in private school, a home in the neighborhood I want, and the holidays I've planned?"*

If the answer is no — that's your motivation. You now have both a positive pull (toward the vision) and a negative push (away from the consequence of staying still).

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Income without lifestyle context is meaningless</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You need to know <em>what the money is for</em> to make it real</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>SMART goals only — no vague targets</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>A dream without a deadline is just a wish</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Growth is non-linear</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Don't expect to be halfway there at the halfway point — plan for compounding</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Announce your goals</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Making them public creates accountability and commitment</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Know your weakest metric</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Fix the bottleneck — don't work harder on what's already working</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Write down your 10-year lifestyle vision in full detail — house, cars, family, travel, savings
    
-   \[ \] Calculate what gross income that requires (post-tax need + tax rate)
    
-   \[ \] Work backwards: what does your 5-year, 3-year, and 1-year income need to be?
    
-   \[ \] Break it into quarterly targets
    
-   \[ \] Identify your current biggest skill or opportunity gap — and define one action to start closing it this week
    
-   \[ \] Share your 12-month income goal with someone who will hold you accountable

### 45 It's OK To Hear NO

https://youtu.be/khsQMasB8IY

# It's OK to Hear No: The Right Mindset Around Rejection

---

## Overview

This is a short but important mindset reset. Detaching from the outcome of any individual call is one of the most important shifts you can make — both for your mental health and your actual performance.

---

## The Core Mindset Shift

> *"There's no such thing as a bad sales call."*

If you do your process on every call — connecting, discovering, building the gap, consequence, transition, commitment — then the outcome is what it is. A yes is great. A no is fine. Your goal after every call should simply be: *"That was a good call."*

You're not judging yourself on the result. You're judging yourself on the quality of your process.

---

## The Camera Mindset

One of the fastest ways to raise your standard on every call:

> *"Imagine that call is going to be listened to by your team, your manager, or someone you respect. No one's a coward in front of a camera."*

When you know someone might hear it — you don't give up when it gets uncomfortable. You stay in the process. You ask the next question. You don't fold at the first sign of resistance.

The practical application: **share your calls.** Submit them for review. Put them in your team Slack channel. The exposure creates accountability that motivation alone can't.

---

## The Probability Reality

Every prospect pool roughly breaks down like this:

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Group</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Reality</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>10%</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Guaranteed no — they were never going to buy regardless of what you do</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>10%</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Guaranteed yes — they were buying no matter what</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>80%</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>In play — your skills determine the outcome here</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

**The critical insight:** You can hit three guaranteed no's in a row. That doesn't mean your next call will also be a no. The roulette wheel doesn't care about your last spin. Don't let a string of no's tell you a story about yourself or your process.

> *"You can hit red five times in a row on roulette — it doesn't make it any more likely to hit red the next time."*

---

## What It Actually Means When You're Good

> *"When you're truly in a good position in sales, you're happy for people to listen to the no's that you get."*

That's the real test. Not whether you close every call — but whether you'd be comfortable with someone reviewing the calls you didn't close. Did you run your process? Did you stay disarmed? Did you ask the right questions and hold your frame?

If yes — those are good calls, regardless of outcome.

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Detach from the outcome</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Attachment creates neediness — and neediness triggers fight or flight</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Judge yourself on process, not result</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You control the process; you don't control the prospect's decision</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>The camera mindset raises your floor</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Knowing others will hear the call keeps you honest and persistent</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>10% will never buy — accept it</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Stop trying to save every call; save your energy for the 80% in play</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Strings of no's are statistical, not personal</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Past results don't predict the next call — reset between every one</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Start submitting your calls for review — or at minimum, listen back to them yourself
    
-   \[ \] After every call (yes or no), ask one question: *"Did I run my process?"* — not *"why didn't they buy?"*
    
-   \[ \] Set a personal standard: *"I want to be proud of every call someone listens to — including the ones I lost"*
    
-   \[ \] If you've hit a string of no's recently, review whether it's the 10% guaranteed no segment — or whether there's a process gap worth addressing
