### 46 Prevent Burn Out

https://youtu.be/fRS1Y31O8UQ

# Preventing Burnout: Staying in the Game for the Long Term

---

## Overview

Sales is one of the highest-earning professions in the world — but the gap between being new and being truly successful is where most people burn out and quit. This short module covers two practical tools to protect your longevity.

---

## The Reality of Sales Burnout

Being a sales rep working remotely makes the boundary between work and life almost invisible. People start to feel like they're working 80 hours a week even when they're not — because they're always mentally on. That's unsustainable.

> *"In order to stay in the sales game for a long time, you have to be able to separate yourself from your job."*

---

## Tool 1 — The Burner Phone for Follow-Ups

When prospects ghost your calls, it's usually not personal. Most of the time they see your number, think *"that's the person I need to talk to — I just don't have 15 minutes right now"* — and then forget to call back.

**The fix:** Use a secondary number for follow-up calls. When an unknown number comes up, the prospect picks up out of curiosity. You get a short window to book the next call — without them dreading a long conversation before they even answer.

This isn't manipulation — it's just removing the friction of a recognised missed call pattern.

---

## Tool 2 — Hard Boundaries on Your Time Off

There is nothing you can do about a deal that dies at 5pm on a Friday. The prospect won't pick up. You can't fix it. Seeing it only ruins your weekend.

**The practice:** Have a method — a separate weekend phone, do not disturb settings, or simply leaving your work phone at your desk — that lets you genuinely switch off from Friday evening to Monday morning.

> *"We're not saving lives. Be great at what you do — but you don't need to be dealing with things at 5pm on a Saturday night. Deal with it Monday morning."*

---

## Key Takeaways

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Why It Matters</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Ghosting is usually circumstantial</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Don't take it personally — a new number can restart the conversation</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Separation is a performance tool</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You can't sustain high performance without genuine rest</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p><strong>Nothing happens on weekends that can't wait until Monday</strong></p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Sales doesn't require 24/7 availability — it requires focused, high-quality selling time</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items / Homework

-   \[ \] Set up a secondary number for follow-up calls if you don't already have one
    
-   \[ \] Define your official "off" hours and set your phone to do not disturb or use a separate device during that time
    
-   \[ \] Commit to not checking sales-related notifications after a set time each evening and on weekends — and stick to it for two weeks

---

### 47 Daily Routine

https://youtu.be/KzMh4cZBjf0

# Lecture Notes: Daily Routine & Calendar Setup

---

## Overview

Everyone's daily routine will look different — and that's okay. What matters is that you **intentionally schedule** the key activities into your diary so nothing gets left to chance.

---

## What Needs to Be Blocked in Your Diary

-   **Triages** (client follow-ups, pipeline reviews)
    
-   **Sales calls / appointments**
    
-   **Personal development / training**
    
-   **Health & fitness activities**
    
-   **Morning routine time**
    

> The goal: your calendar should reflect your ideal life, not just your work obligations.

---

## Sample Daily Structure (Matt's Routine)

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Time</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Activity</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>5:30–6:00 AM</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Wake up, make coffee, go outside</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>6:00–6:30 AM</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Light breathwork, phone-free wind-up</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>6:30–7:00 AM</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Get ready, shower</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>7:00–7:30 AM</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Commute / out the door</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>~7:30 AM</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Emails (once per day, in the morning only)</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Morning</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Training / coaching sessions</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Late morning–early afternoon</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Sales calls / appointments</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Afternoon</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>30-min admin check (banking, follow-ups)</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>5:00 PM</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Hard stop — everything off</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Key Principles

### 1\. Protect Your Morning

-   Avoid your phone for the **first 30 minutes** of the day
    
-   No notifications, no social media — don't spike your nervous system before the day begins
    
-   Use that time to be present: coffee, fresh air, light breathwork
    

### 2\. Batch Similar Tasks Together

-   Emails **once a day** only (morning) — there's no need to check twice
    
-   Admin/banking check **once in the afternoon**
    
-   Sales calls clustered in **late morning to early afternoon** when energy is right
    

### 3\. Lock In Your Non-Negotiables

-   Health/fitness activities go into the diary **first**, not last
    
-   Matt blocks jiujitsu 3x/week (Mon, Wed, Thu) — that time is non-negotiable
    
-   On those days, start slightly earlier or end slightly later to compensate
    

### 4\. Create Consistency Through Routine

-   **Similar days = less decision fatigue = more consistency**
    
-   The more variation you remove from your structure, the more energy you free up for the work that matters
    
-   Routine reduces friction and builds momentum over time
    

### 5\. Design Around Your Ideal Life

-   Your daily routine should reflect the lifestyle you're working toward
    
-   If you love mornings, build a morning you look forward to
    
-   Ask yourself: *"What does my ideal day look like?"* — then build your schedule backward from that
    

---

## Action Items

-   \[ \] Map out your **ideal daily schedule** block by block (use your calendar, not just your memory)
    
-   \[ \] Identify your **3 non-negotiables** each day (e.g. sales activity, training, admin)
    
-   \[ \] Set a **hard stop time** in the evening and stick to it
    
-   \[ \] Batch your emails to **once a day** — note what time works best for your timezone/clients
    
-   \[ \] Lock in any **health/fitness blocks** into your diary this week
    

---

## Key Takeaway

> *"Iron out your daily process. Make your days similar. Less variation = less choice = more consistency. Build a routine you can't wait to wake up to."*

---

### 48 Old Lead Lists

https://youtu.be/rcWT1AsA9Lk

# Lecture Notes: Working Old Lead Lists

---

## Overview

Old lead lists are an underutilized asset that most people ignore. Whether it's people who opted in but never closed, previous no-sales, or past clients — these lists can generate a consistent pipeline when worked systematically.

---

## Why Old Lead Lists Matter

-   **You can't control whether leads come in — but you can control your activity**
    
-   Core principle: *somebody else's ability to generate leads should not affect your ability to make money*
    
-   Old lists give you a sphere of influence you already own — no new ad spend, no new outreach required
    
-   Not the highest-yield activity, but essential for **maintaining consistency** when the pipeline is thin
    

---

## When to Work Your List

Use old lead list sessions to fill **dead time** in your calendar:

-   No-show appointments
    
-   After referrals and other farming is done
    
-   Any 1–2 hour block with nothing scheduled
    

> Don't go on Netflix. Work the list.

---

## How to Work It: The Auto-Dialer Method

### Tool Needed

-   **Auto-dialer** (e.g., [Close.io](http://Close.io)) — approximately $25/month + call charges
    
-   Upload your list, set the dialer, and let it run
    

### Dial Volume

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Setting</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Output</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>1 lead every 15 seconds</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>4 dials/minute</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>1-hour session</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>~240 calls</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>2-hour session</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>~480 calls</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

Even with a low pickup rate, you're having **real conversations** with people who already showed some prior interest.

---

## What to Do When Someone Picks Up

1.  Ask them **why** they downloaded the lead magnet / enquired previously
    
2.  **Find a problem** — what challenge are they dealing with now?
    
3.  Get on script
    
4.  **Book a triage call**
    
5.  Move them into your pipeline
    

---

## Timeline & Expectations

-   **Months 1–3:** Don't expect immediate sales — you're warming the list
    
-   **Month 3+:** A consistently worked list becomes a **thick, well-developed pipeline**
    
-   The compounding effect of regular touchpoints = sales that close later, on their own timeline
    

---

## Action Items

-   \[ \] Pull your old lead list from your CRM (no-sales, no-shows, previous clients, old opt-ins)
    
-   \[ \] Get an auto-dialer set up if you don't have one (~$25/month)
    
-   \[ \] Block **1–2 hours per week** specifically for old list calling
    
-   \[ \] Track conversations and re-tag leads based on where they are now
    
-   \[ \] Be consistent — the pipeline builds over months, not days
    

---

## Key Takeaway

> *"Don't sleep on old lead lists. Spend an hour to two hours a week on them. It's not glamorous — but done consistently, it builds redundancy into your business and a pipeline that pays off months down the track."*

---

### 49 No Sales and No Show Lists

https://youtu.be/DsWspg3_a9s

# Lecture Notes: No-Sales & No-Show Lists

---

## Overview

No-shows and no-sales are two of the highest-priority lists you should be working consistently. Most reps ignore them — which is exactly why working them is such a competitive advantage.

---

## The Mindset Shift

**A no-show ≠ not interested.**

People don't show up because:

-   They forgot
    
-   An automation broke
    
-   Life got in the way
    
-   They had a bad day
    

Most people who downloaded something or booked a call are **still genuinely interested** — they just never heard from anyone again. That's your opportunity.

---

## No-Show Follow-Up Cadence

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Timeframe</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Frequency</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>First month</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Call a couple of times per week</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Months 2–12</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Call once every 2 weeks to once a month</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Ongoing</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Keep in touch via call, text, or email</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

Consistency is the key — don't write them off after one missed attempt.

---

## No-Sales Lists

-   Go back through **your own** no-sales regularly
    
-   If you're a top performer, also work through **your teammates' no-sales** — most reps will write leads off; you shouldn't
    
-   This is one of the fastest ways to out-earn peers on the same team — same leads, more follow-through
    

---

## What to Do When a No-Show Happens

**Immediately enact your protocol.** A no-show is not a break — it's a trigger to get busier.

> Don't high-five, go to the beach, or kill time. Fill that slot.

---

## The Call Script (Simple & Non-Salesy)

Opening:

> *"Hey \[name\], you recently downloaded / booked a call for \[X\] — I'm just calling to see how you went with that."*

Then:

1.  Ask **why** they originally booked or downloaded
    
2.  Ask **how they're going** with the problem they wanted to solve
    
3.  Offer to **book the next chat** — keep it light and conversational
    

There's no need to be salesy or nervous. It's just a human check-in that re-opens the door.

---

## The Bigger Picture

The reps who feel like they're working 60 hours but only producing 20 hours of results are the ones **counting no-show time as working time.** Real professionals:

-   Treat every gap as a chance to book more opportunity
    
-   Work the list when calls fall through
    
-   Build a pipeline that keeps filling itself
    

This is a full-time job — treat it like one.

---

## Action Items

-   \[ \] Pull your no-show list and call anyone from the past month who hasn't been re-contacted
    
-   \[ \] Set up a **weekly cadence** to systematically work no-shows
    
-   \[ \] Review your no-sales list and identify leads worth re-engaging
    
-   \[ \] If you're a senior rep, ask your team leader about accessing older team no-sales lists
    
-   \[ \] Build a simple follow-up script you're comfortable with and keep it natural
    

---

## Key Takeaway

> *"Every time someone no-shows, don't take a break — enact your protocols. The reps who consistently work no-shows and no-sales lists are the ones who out-earn everyone else on the same team with the same leads."*

---

### 50 Referral SOP

https://youtu.be/xNu8f-Qyo1A

# Lecture Notes: Referral SOP

---

## Overview

Referrals are one of the simplest ways to increase your income as a sales rep — and one of the most consistently overlooked. Having a **standard operating procedure (SOP)** for referrals means you're generating leads systematically, not just when you remember to ask.

---

## Why Referrals Matter

-   **No ad cost attached** = higher margin per sale
    
-   In some accounts, self-generated or referred leads can qualify for a **higher commission rate**
    
-   Compounds over time — even one referral sale per month adds up significantly
    

### The Math

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 75px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Avg. commission per sale</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Referral sales per month</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Extra annual income</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>$1,000</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>1</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>$12,000/year</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

And that's the conservative floor — most good reps generate more than one.

---

## Key Principles

-   Referrals should not be random — they need a **consistent, repeatable process**
    
-   A referral SOP means you ask the right way, at the right time, every time
    
-   Treat referral-sourced leads as a **separate sphere of influence** from company-provided leads — you own that pipeline
    

---

## Action Items

-   \[ \] Download and review the Sales Sniper Referral SOP document
    
-   \[ \] Identify the **right moment** in your sales process to ask for referrals (typically post-sale or after a win)
    
-   \[ \] Build the referral ask into your **standard close sequence** — not as an afterthought
    
-   \[ \] Track referral leads separately in your CRM
    
-   \[ \] Check with your team leader whether your account qualifies for **higher commission on self-generated leads**
    

---

## Key Takeaway

> *"If you want to earn more, be more consistent, and be a top-tier sales rep — you have to have a referral SOP. One extra sale a month from referrals alone is $12,000 a year. That's not a bonus. That's a system."*

---

### 51 Deposit Into Sale

https://youtu.be/b3cJVaklKso

# Lecture Notes: Turning a Deposit Into a Sale

---

## Overview

A deposit is **not a sale** — it's a commitment that still needs to be converted. Here are two methods to close the full payment quickly after a deposit is taken.

---

## Method 1: Ride the Momentum ("Let's Go All In")

**Best for:** Prospects who are emotionally bought in and likely have the funds available.

After taking the deposit, while they're still on the phone and feeling good:

**Step 1 — Acknowledge the decision and amplify the feeling:**

> *"Man, how does it feel? Like you're finally taking that first step, getting some momentum, starting to move forward on your goal — how does it feel?"*

Let them express the positive emotion. They'll almost always say it feels great.

**Step 2 — Reframe the hard part as already done:**

> *"You've already made the hard choice. The first step is literally the hardest — so let's just go all in. Let's take the rest of it. What are we waiting for? Let's do it!"*

**Step 3 — Get a little excited, make it fun**

-   Match their energy, add some humor
    
-   Create a moment — make it feel like a team decision, not a sales push
    
-   Keep it light: a little enthusiasm goes a long way
    

> This works far more often than you'd expect. Don't underestimate it.

---

## Method 2: Find the Money Together ("Assets & Liabilities")

**Best for:** Prospects who genuinely want to proceed but are cash-constrained.

After taking the deposit:

> *"Alright, let's look at two things — your assets and your liabilities. What do we have to work with? How can we set up the next payment and get you started faster?"*

### Help them find the money by exploring:

**Sell unused assets:**

-   Gaming consoles they no longer use (PS5, Xbox)
    
-   A car they rarely drive (especially if working from home)
    
-   Motorcycles, bikes, or sports gear sitting idle
    
-   Anything around the house not providing value
    

> Most people, if they look around, can find $3,000–$4,000 worth of stuff they don't use — sellable on Gumtree, Facebook Marketplace, etc.

**Leverage existing options (last resort):**

-   Credit cards
    
-   Car title loans
    

> Avoid pushing debt where possible — focus on selling idle assets first.

---

## Quick Reference

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 75px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Method</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>When to Use</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Core Approach</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Method 1 — Ride the Momentum</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Prospect has funds, just needs a nudge</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Amplify emotion, reframe the hard part as done, invite them to go all in</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Method 2 — Find the Money</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Prospect is genuinely cash-short</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Walk through assets vs liabilities, help them find existing resources</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items

-   \[ \] Practice the **Method 1 script** out loud until it feels natural and enthusiastic — energy is everything here
    
-   \[ \] Prepare a mental checklist of **common sellable assets** to prompt during Method 2 conversations
    
-   \[ \] Never hang up after a deposit without at least attempting to convert to full payment
    
-   \[ \] Role-play both scenarios with your team this week
    

---

## Key Takeaway

> *"A deposit is the door opening — not the sale closing. The moment they've paid something, their emotional commitment is at its peak. That's exactly when you go for the full close."*

---

### 52 Payment Plan Into A PIF

https://youtu.be/jEtRw7JVX9I

# Lecture Notes: Converting a Payment Plan Into a Paid-in-Full

---

## Overview

Once a sale is made, the next goal is to collect as much cash upfront as possible — and ideally convert a payment plan into a paid-in-full (PIF). This requires a specific sequencing: **secure the sale first, then introduce the incentive.**

---

## The Core Pricing Principle: Pitch Big, Drop Down

**Don't pitch the discounted price first.** Always lead with the full payment plan price.

Example:

-   Payment plan: **$18,000** over 6 months
    
-   Paid in full: **$15,000** (discount not revealed upfront)
    

Why this works:

-   Pitching the higher number makes dropping to PIF feel like a win for the client
    
-   It can also **induce a useful objection** — *"I want to pay in full, I just need time to get the funds"* — which is a buying signal, not resistance
    
-   If no incentive is created, most people will default to a payment plan just to keep cash available — so you need to create one
    

---

## The Process: Step by Step

### Step 1 — Close the Sale on the Payment Plan

Get them committed and processed. Don't mention PIF discount yet.

> Secure the sale first. The incentive is a bonus, not a crutch.

### Step 2 — Collect More Than the Minimum Upfront

When setting up the payment plan, always push for a higher first payment.

Example:

-   Instead of $3,000/month × 6 = $18,000
    
-   Offer: **$6,000 down + $3,000/month × 4 months**
    

If they agree to $3,000 down, push:

> *"Could you do four? Could you do five?"*

Always collect more cash than the baseline.

### Step 3 — Introduce the PIF Incentive Post-Sale

Once payment is processed and they're feeling good:

> *"I'm not sure if I mentioned this — there's actually a slight discount for paying in full. You've already put $6,000 down. If you put in an additional $9,000, that's the whole thing wiped and you actually get a month for free. Do you want to have a quick chat about that and save yourself some cash?"*

### Step 4 — Read Their Reaction and Offer a Bridge Option

If they're genuinely considering it but hesitant:

> *"How about we do a two-pay — $7,500 today and $7,500 in 30 days — and I'll count that as a paid in full. Does that work for you?"*

They've already paid $6,000 — the additional $1,500 now feels small, and the second $7,500 in 30 days closes it out.

---

## Why This Structure Works

-   Client is already emotionally committed (they've paid something)
    
-   The PIF offer feels like a reward, not pressure
    
-   The two-pay bridge option reduces the psychological barrier of a large lump sum
    
-   Both payments land within one billing cycle — essentially as good as PIF for the business
    

---

## Quick Reference

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Stage</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>What to Do</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Pricing pitch</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Always lead with payment plan (full price)</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>First payment collection</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Push for more than the minimum down</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Post-sale</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Introduce PIF discount casually, after they're already in</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>If hesitant</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Offer a two-pay split counted as PIF</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items

-   \[ \] Know your account's **PIF discount structure** before every call
    
-   \[ \] Practice pitching the payment plan price first — never lead with the discount
    
-   \[ \] Always ask for more than the minimum on the first payment
    
-   \[ \] Memorise the **post-sale PIF bridge script** and practice the delivery so it feels natural, not rehearsed
    
-   \[ \] Role-play the two-pay close with a teammate this week
    

---

## Key Takeaway

> *"Secure the sale first — then introduce the incentive. Never use the PIF discount as a crutch to close. Use it as a reward after they're already in. Collect as much cash upfront as possible at every stage."*

---

### 53 Door to Door Selling

https://youtu.be/08npMyGt5G0

# Lecture Notes: Door-to-Door Selling with NEPQ

---

## Overview

This session covers how to apply NEPQ (New Model Selling) to door-to-door sales. While the core methodology is the same, D2D requires specific adjustments — particularly in the first 5–10 seconds — because the prospect's mental state when they open a door is very different from a phone call.

**Featured:** Joseph Robberry — went from $15–20K/month to $100K+/month in commissions after applying NEPQ to door-to-door solar sales.

---

## Why D2D Is Different

When someone opens their door to a stranger unannounced, a lot is happening in their mind simultaneously. They're picking up on:

-   Your **body language**
    
-   Your **tonality**
    
-   Your **words and questions**
    

This triggers one of two responses:

-   **Fight or flight** → "Not interested, we already had someone come by, come back later"
    
-   **Curiosity** → "Yeah, what's going on? How can I help?"
    

> If you're getting a lot of "not interested" in the first 30 seconds, you are not creating curiosity. That's the only reason.

**Key insight:** You will never hear "not interested" from someone who is genuinely curious.

---

## The Door Approach

### Physical Setup (Before They Even Open)

-   Stand **5–6 feet back** from the door — never right up at it
    
-   **Turn slightly sideways** — non-threatening body posture
    
-   Wear **low-key, plain clothing** — work vest, Carhartt, nothing with logos or company branding
    
-   No flashy jewelry, watches, or branded gear — all of it screams "salesperson"
    
-   Hold a **clipboard** or look like you're surveying the area (roof, yard, trees)
    
-   **Act slightly distracted** — looking at your clipboard or at the surroundings when they open the door
    

> The goal: they open the door and think "is this a contractor? A surveyor?" — not "here's a salesperson."

### The Opening Line

When they come to the door, act slightly confused — not rehearsed:

> *"Oh hey, I apologize for interrupting — I was wondering if you could help me out for just a moment. Is this still the \[Johnson\] residence?"*

Or if you don't have their name:

> *"Quick question — you guys selling the place?"*

**Why this works:**

-   The confused, slow, uncertain tonality is disarming
    
-   It forces them to engage and lean in
    
-   "Maybe you could help me out" is low-threat and invites cooperation
    
-   Asking if they're selling forces them to confirm they're the homeowner without it feeling like a sales qualifier
    

---

## Two-Touch vs. One-Touch Sale

For solar specifically (and many other D2D products), this is typically a **two-touch sale**:

-   **First visit:** Set an appointment — nothing more, nothing less
    
-   **Problem statement** and full presentation happen at the **second visit**, not at the door
    
-   Don't try to do everything in one knock — focus on getting enough hooks to secure the appointment
    

> Goal of the first touch: get them curious enough to say yes to a follow-up meeting.

---

## Creating Curiosity at the Door (Solar Example)

Once disarmed and they're engaging, use a **neighbour reference** for social proof and familiarity:

> *"Not sure if you know your neighbour Kathy — she's the one with the golden retriever three doors down. Not sure if you guys speak with her or know what's going on, but she actually buys her electricity from us — not the power company."*

Then handle the "is this solar?" objection proactively:

> *"We're not selling solar panels — if that made sense you probably would have had them already. It's a little bit different. It might not be for you, it might not. But is it ridiculous to think that people should have more than one company to buy electricity from?"*

Then pull back:

> *"I'm just step one in the process. I don't know enough about your situation to even know if we could help you."*

Use a **1–10 scale check-in** to gauge interest:

> *"On a scale of 1 to 10 — one being completely closed off, ten being this might help you — where would you say you are?"*

Or alternatively:

> *"I just said a lot of information. What's the first thought or question that pops into your head?"*

---

## Situation Questions (First Touch)

Keep it minimal — just enough to qualify and get hooks. Examples for solar:

-   "How long have you been in the home using \[NV Power / the current provider\]?"
    
-   "What was the main reason you chose \[current provider\] as your electrical supplier?"
    

The second question is intentionally a trick question — they didn't choose, they had no option. When they say "we didn't have a choice," that's your first wedge.

> *"I know, that's a bit of a trick question — you can punch me — but seriously, when they raised your rates, did you have a choice in that?"*

**Why humour here:** It humanises the conversation, breaks tension, and people who are laughing are open. People who are objecting are serious. Get them laughing.

---

## The Full Presentation (Second Touch — Both Spouses Present)

### Reframe the Purpose of the Meeting

Open by giving them control:

> *"We've prepared quite a bit, but honestly — what would be the most valuable way for us to spend this time together today so we can focus on what you're actually looking for?"*

This is a frame-setter: it signals you're different from every other salesperson they've met, and it puts the agenda in their hands.

### Problem Awareness Questions

-   "How long have you been using \[NV Energy\]?"
    
-   "Do you like them? What do you enjoy about them?"
    
-   If they say "not bad": *"Not bad... not bad compared to what though? Like what do you have to compare it to?"*
    
-   "Is there anything you'd change about your current electricity situation if you could?"
    
-   "When they raise your rates, do you have a choice in that?"
    
-   "How much have they actually raised your rates in the last 1–3 years? Did you say that was okay? Did they ask your permission?"
    
-   *"What kind of agreement do you have with your electric provider that allows them to raise your rate without your permission — and are you okay with that?"*
    

### Solution Awareness Questions

-   "Before today, were you actively looking for ways to save money on your electric bill?"
    
    -   If yes: "How'd that work out for you?"
        
    -   If no: "What prevented you from looking into that?"
        
-   "If this ended up working out for you — like it did for your neighbour Kathy — and we were able to permanently lower your power bill for the next 10 years and save you around $X, what would that do for you personally?"
    
-   "What would your perfect scenario actually look like?"
    

> If they give a one-word answer, push deeper: *"Hold on — when you say 'that'd be awesome,' what do you mean by that? Can you tell me more?"*

### Breaking Down the Numbers

Use a simple side-by-side comparison:

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 75px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p></p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Current Path</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>With Solar</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Monthly bill</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>$137 (rising 4–5%/year)</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Fixed/lower rate</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>10-year total</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>~$21,000+</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>~$11,500</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>25-year total</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>~$87,000</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>~$39,600 (then $0)</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

**The mortgage analogy** (for the long-term pitch):

> *"It's like paying off your mortgage. After 25 years your house is paid off and you own it. Same thing here — after the term, your power is paid off. No bill. Everything coming in on Social Security or retirement is yours — not going to the utility company."*

**The ownership flip:**

> *"You own your house right now. What would I have to say to convince you to go back to renting it? ...It's the same thing with your electricity."*

### Consequence Questions

These are the most emotionally powerful — use them deliberately.

**Question 1 (General):**

> *"I don't mean to offend you, but have you actually thought about how much money is going to come out of your pocket over the next 10 years if you don't do anything — if you just keep letting them raise your rate as much as they want, whenever they want?"*

**Question 2 (For retirees or near-retirement):**

> *"Have you thought about what happens when you retire in 20–25 years, your income gets cut in half, and your electricity bill has been going up 5–7% every year for the last two decades — and it keeps going up? What are you going to do then?"*

**Question 3 (The "stuck contract" close):**

> *"Have you considered that right now you're in a never-ending contract with your utility company — but it's not in writing, which means they can raise your rate as much as they want, whenever they want, and they don't need your permission? Last year alone they raised it 6.7%. Do you want them to keep doing that?"*

**Key principle on consequence questions:**

> People make decisions to avoid pain more than to gain pleasure. Get them to feel the cost of inaction — not just the benefit of action.

---

## Commitment Questions

Once the presentation is complete, don't pitch — check in:

**Step 1 — Check-In Question:**

> *"Just up to this point — do you feel comfortable with what we've covered so far?"*

If yes: *"Why though? Can I ask why?"* (Get them to articulate it in their own words.)

**Step 2 — Commitment Question:**

> *"Do you feel like this is something you could do that would get you what you're looking for?"*

> *"Do you feel like this could be the answer for you?"*

**Why this works:** Once someone answers yes to that question, they have mentally committed. They have placed themselves in the future experiencing the result — cancellation rate drops dramatically.

**Step 3 — Set the Appointment / Next Step:**

> *"It looks like we've covered what you're looking for. The next step is to set up a survey on your home to make sure it meets the program requirements — would that be appropriate?"*

---

## Holding the Frame (Anti-Cancellation)

For solar especially, the period between commitment and installation is a risk window.

**Frame it as selective:**

> *"Keep in mind — we haven't even selected your program or approved your home yet. This program is a little more selective. There are some homeowners we genuinely can't help."*

This creates mild scarcity and shifts the dynamic — they want to be approved, not sold.

**Before you leave:**

-   Leave something physical behind (e.g., a business card magnet for the fridge)
    
-   Leave them with a comparison sheet (10-year or 25-year breakdown)
    
-   Normalise having questions: *"You'll have questions — totally normal. Write them all down and we'll go through them after the survey."*
    
-   Frame the next step as necessary: *"That's when we'll know 100% whether we can do this for you or not."*
    

---

## Handling Early Objections

**"Not interested" (within first minute):**

> *"Of course — keep in mind, we haven't even selected this program for your home yet. It's a bit more selective — there are a lot of people we can't even help. I'm not even sure if we could do this for you yet. Can I ask you just one quick question though — how long have you been using \[current provider\]?"*

**"Is this solar?":**

> *"It's a little bit different. We're not trying to sell you a $30,000 solar system. We actually just sell you the electricity it produces — and that electricity costs far less than what you're buying from the power lines. But we're getting ahead of ourselves — I don't even know enough about your situation to know if this could be an option for you."*

**"My bill's fine / not that much":**

> *"I can appreciate that — nobody complains about a low bill. But just curious — do you know how much they've actually raised your rates in the last year or two? Last year alone they raised the average rate 7.5%. Did you say that was okay? Did they ask for your permission?"*

---

## Key Principles Summary

<table class="tiptap-table" style="min-width: 50px;"><colgroup><col style="min-width: 25px;"><col style="min-width: 25px;"></colgroup><tbody><tr><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Principle</p></th><th colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Application</p></th></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Disarm first, sell second</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Physical presence, confused tonality, no company branding</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Curiosity over pitch</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>The right questions make them lean in — not push back</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Two-touch for complex sales</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>First visit = appointment only; presentation = second visit</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Probe deeper before solving</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Always ask "why do you ask that?" before answering</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Pain over pleasure</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Consequence questions drive more decisions than benefit pitches</p></td></tr><tr><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>Hold the frame</p></td><td colspan="1" rowspan="1"><p>You're selective — not desperate</p></td></tr></tbody></table>

---

## Action Items

-   \[ \] Practice the **door approach script** until the confused tonality feels natural — not rehearsed
    
-   \[ \] Memorise your **neighbour reference opener** for your product/market
    
-   \[ \] Build your **situation question list** specific to what you sell (max 3–4 for the first touch)
    
-   \[ \] Write out your **top 3 consequence questions** for your product and practise them out loud
    
-   \[ \] Role-play the full two-touch sequence with a teammate this week
    
-   \[ \] Review your current close rate and identify which part of the flow (door → appt → presentation → close) is leaking
    

---

## Key Takeaway

> *"The difference between $20K/month and $100K/month wasn't effort — it was removing the pressure. NEPQ at the door is about creating so much curiosity in the first 10 seconds that 'not interested' never even comes up. Everything else flows from that."*
